Hall algebras and shifted quantum affine algebras
P. Goyal, P. Samuelson

Abstract
In [ FT19a ] , Finkelberg and Tsymbaliuk introduced the notion of shifted quantum affine algebras and described their role in the study of quantized Coulomb branches associated to certain 3D N = 4 N=4 quiver gauge theories. We describe a new geometric construction of a deformation of one of these shifted quantum affine algebras as the Hall algebra of the category of representations of a certain quiver Q Rud Q_{\textrm{Rud}} (modulo relations). This quiver first arose in the work of Rudakov in the study of the tame blocks of the category of restricted representations of the Lie algebra 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 ​ ( 𝔽 q ) \mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q}) .



1
Introduction

The shifted quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
was defined in
[
FT19a
]
as a tool for algebraically describing
K
-theoretic Coulomb branches of 3D
N=4
SUSY quiver gauge theories. Our main theorem realizes (a deformation of) the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})^{FT}_{1,1}
inside the Hall algebra of the category of representations of a certain quiver
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
(modulo relations)
1
1

1

We actually work with a twisted and localized Hall algebra. Also, there are two versions of the affine algebra: the simply connected one and the adjoint one. We ignore these technicalities for now.
. Below we describe these objects in more detail, state a more precise version of our results, and describe some future directions.

1.1
Hall Algebras

The
Hall algebra
of a category
\mathcal{C}
can be viewed as a convolution algebra on the space
\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C}))
, where we view the set
\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
of objects as some kind of geometric space. The convolution product comes from the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Exact}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

(1.1)

where the map
\pi_{1}
sends a short exact sequence
M\to E\to N
to the pair
(M,N)
, and
\pi_{2}
sends the same sequence to
E
. For this construction to make sense, the category
\mathcal{C}
needs a notion of exactness (e.g. exact sequences in an abelian category, exact triangles in a derived category). We also need a notion of finiteness to ensure that the map
\pi_{2}
is proper.

There are several levels of generality for which the Hall algebra construction has been studied: point-counting, derived, motivic, cohomological,
K
-theoretic, etc. The nicely written survey
[
Sch12b
]
has become the canonical introduction to Hall algebras; other natural surveys with complementary points of view are
[
Bri12
,
Bri18
,
Dyc18
]
. In what follows, we will use the point-counting version of the Hall algebra, since this is most amenable to direct computation (see Definition
2.8
); in this version, we view
\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
as a discrete space, which requires quite strong finiteness assumptions. Precisely, we will assume
\mathcal{C}
is
finitary
, which (for us) means
\mathcal{C}
is abelian and linear over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)
and
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)
finite dimensional for all
M,N
.

One of the first theorems about Hall algebras relates them to quantum groups. Given an acyclic quiver
Q
, let
\mathcal{C}_{Q}=\mathcal{R}ep_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(Q)
be the category of representations of
Q
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{Q})
be the positive part of the quantum Kac-Moody algebra associated to
Q
. A combination of results by Ringel and Green
[
Rin90
,
Gre95
]
constructs an injective bialgebra map

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{Q})\hookrightarrow Hall(\mathcal{C}_{Q})
which specializes the formal parameter
v
to
q^{1/2}
. This result has had far-reaching consequences and generalizations; one of the most influential applications is the use of this realisation by Lusztig to define canonical bases for quantum groups and their irreducible representations with quite remarkable positivity and integrality properties
[
Lus90
,
Lus91
,
Lus92
]
(see also the survey
[
Sch12a
]
).

The results of Ringel and Green predict that it would be fruitful to study the Hall algebras of other non-semisimple
finitary categories; a natural candidate is the category of sheaves on a smooth, projective curve
X/\mathbb{F}_{q}
. When
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, Kapronov
[
Kap97
]
found a map from the positive half
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathcal{L}{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}^{+})
of the quantum loop algebra to
Hall(Coh(\mathbb{P}^{1}))
, which inspired several further results
[
BK01
,
BS12b
,
Szá06
,
Zha00
,
McG05
,
XZ06
,
Sch06
]
.
In particular, in
[
BS12a
]
Burban and Schiffmann computed the Hall algebra of an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and showed it is the positive half of the so-called
quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
; this result has found applications in algebraic combinatorics, knot theory, mathematical physics, and more
[
GN15
,
Mel22
,
Mel21
,
MS17
,
SV13
]
. Hall algebras of higher genus curves have also been studied extensively
[
Sch11
,
SV12
,
BS13
,
KSV17
]
.

All the theorems above only realize “halves” of various quantum algebras; it is quite natural to ask whether there is a similar realization of the entire algebra instead. There was considerable progress on this question
[
Kap98
,
PX97
,
PX00
,
Toë06
]
, but a complete answer was given by Bridgeland in
[
Bri13
]
, which can be phrased as follows. Define the
Bridgeland quiver

Q_{\mathrm{Bri}}:=\left[\hbox to95.22pt{\vbox to28.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 47.61105pt\lower-14.32889pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(1.2)

Given a quiver
Q
with no oriented cycles, let
\mathrm{Fun}(Q_{\mathrm{Bri}},Q)
be the abelian category of functors from
Q_{\mathrm{Bri}}
(viewed as a 2-object category) into
\mathcal{R}ep(Q)
. Then in
[
Bri13
]
, Bridgeland constructed an injective algebra map
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})\hookrightarrow Hall(\mathrm{Fun}(Q_{\mathrm{Bri}},Q))
(up to some technical details, see Section
2.2
). We also note that the realizations of entire quantum groups have been constructed via other (orthogonal) strategies, for example through the geometry of quiver varieties
[
Nak94
,
Nak98
,
Nak01
]
and through categorification via
2
-categories
[
KL09
,
KL11
,
Rou11
]
.

Another natural source of finitary non-semisimple categories comes from positive characteristic representation theory. In this paper, we initiate the study of Hall algebras of such categories by computing the Hall algebra in the simplest example: the category of equi-characteristic restricted representations of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Surprisingly, we show that it receives a map from a deformation of an
entire
(shifted) quantum affine algebra, and not just the positive half.

1.2
Restricted representations of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of (equi-characteristic, finite dimensional) restricted representations of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, where
p=\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is assumed to be odd in this section. Equivalently,
\mathcal{C}
is the category of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-representations with trivial
p
-character; explicitly, these are the representations which are annihilated by
e^{p},f^{p}
, and
h^{p}-h
. In general, for an arbitrary reductive Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a fixed
p
-character
\chi
, the category of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-representations with
p
-character
\chi
is wild. However when
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
and
\chi=0
, the category
\mathcal{C}
turns out to be tame. In fact, it has an elementary quiver theoretic description (see (
1.3
) below) which we will be using throughout this paper (also see Remark
2.35
.)

Just as in the characteristic 0 case, let
V_{n}:=\mathbb{F}_{q}[x,y]_{n}
be the
Weyl module
(where
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
acts in the standard way). Unlike the characteristic 0 case, the category
\mathcal{C}
is not semisimple, making it interesting from the point of view of Hall algebras. By the works of
[
Jac41
,
Pol68
]
, the category
\mathcal{C}
splits up into blocks:

\mathcal{C}\simeq Vect\oplus\bigoplus_{i=0}^{(p-3)/2}\mathcal{C}_{i},

where block
\mathcal{C}_{i}
contains exactly 2 simple Weyl modules (
V_{i}
and
V_{p-i-2}
). In
[
Rud82
]
, Rudakov showed that each block
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is equivalent to the category of representations of the following quiver (modulo relations), which we refer to as the
Rudakov quiver
:

{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}:=\left[\hbox to95.22pt{\vbox to33.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 47.61105pt\lower-12.83882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\displaystyle ee^{\prime}=e^{\prime}e

\displaystyle=ff^{\prime}=f^{\prime}f=0,

\displaystyle ef^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-fe^{\prime},

(1.3)

\displaystyle e^{\prime}f

\displaystyle=-f^{\prime}e.

Rudakov also classified indecomposable representations of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
(see Section
2.3
); except for two modules
M
and
M^{\prime}
, every other indecomposable
N
comes from one of the two Kronecker subquivers (precisely, in
N
either
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
or
e=f=0
). Translated in terms of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-representations, Rudakov’s results say that every indecomposable representation in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
(except
M
and
M^{\prime}
) is a submodule (as well as a quotient) of a Weyl module. The modules
M
and
M^{\prime}
are both projective and injective, and are central elements in the Hall algebra (see Section
4
). In terms of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-representations, the modules
M
and
M^{\prime}
correspond to the principal indecomposable modules that cover the Weyl modules
V_{i}
and
V_{p-i-2}
respectively.

1.3
Shifted affine algebras

Morally, the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-representations is a doubled version of the category of representations of the Kronecker quiver. Based on the theorems of Ringel, Green, and Bridgeland described above, one might guess that the Hall algebra of the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules realizes the whole quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})
, since its positive half is realized by the Hall algebra of the Kronecker quiver.

While this guess is not exactly correct, we show that the Hall algebra of

{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules does give a realization of a closely related algebra. There exist shifted versions
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}})_{m,n}^{FT}
of the quantum affine algebra defined by Finkelberg and Tsymbaliuk
[
FT19a
]
, depending upon parameters
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
(or on a pair of co-weights
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
for an arbitrary simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
). These algebras were introduced in the study of quantized
K
-theoretic Coulomb branches of 3D
N=4
SUSY quiver gauge theories. The term “shifted” originates from the theory of shifted Yangians introduced in
[
BK06
,
KWWY14
,
BFN19
]
,
and these algebras can be viewed as trigonometric counterparts of shifted Yangians. Shifted quantum affine algebras have become a subject of wide interest in geometric representation theory and have deep connections to quantum integrable systems, cyclotomic Hecke algebras, and cluster algebras (see
[
FT19a
,
FT19b
,
Her23
,
GHL24
,
Wad24
,
VV25
]
).

On a very concrete level, the shifted quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}^{FT}
can be seen as a variation of the new Drinfeld presentation of the quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
: by a theorem due to Drinfeld and Beck (see Theorem
2.2
), the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
is isomorphic to the quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathcal{L}{\mathfrak{g}})
. The algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathcal{L}{\mathfrak{g}})
has a triangular decomposition, and the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}^{FT}
is obtained by gluing together the pieces of this decomposition after “translating” them by the pair of co-weights
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. This translation procedure has the effect of making the loop generators of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}^{FT}
more commutative or more non-commutative depending on the dominance behavior of
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
.
(See Section
2.1
for more precise details.) In the unshifted case
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=0
, the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{0,0}^{FT}
is isomorphic to a central extension of the usual quantum affine algebra without central charge.

1.4
Main results

Following the loose analogy with Bridgeland’s result, we define
\mathcal{H}
to be the localization
2
2

2

Like Green, we use a twisted product Hall algebra, and we also adjoin formal
-1/4
roots of
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
.
of the Hall algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
at the (central) elements
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
. In Section
2.1
we define a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
via explicit generators and relations. This algebra is a (flat) deformation of the adjoint version of the shifted quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
defined in
[
FT19a
]
(see Proposition
8.11
). Our first main theorem relates
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
to the Hall algebra
\mathcal{H}
as follows:

Theorem A

(see Theorem
5.4
and Corollary
7.23
)
.

There exists an injective algebra map from the specialization
\mathcal{U}_{v=q^{1/2}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
to
\mathcal{H}
, and the image is precisely the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
(defined below).

As a consequence of the above theorem, we define a
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]
-algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}
, which is an integral form of the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
(see Definition
8.9
). This integral form is a slight refinement of the one constructed in
[
FT19b
]
for the shifted quantum affine algebra.

As often happens with point-counting Hall algebras, the map
g
is far from being surjective. Intuitively, the space of objects has positive dimension, so the number of objects depends on the order of the field, which is not a property that is desirable in representation theory. (Concretely, in our case, for every
[m:n]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we have a
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
representation where the vertices are assigned 1-dimensional spaces,
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
, and
e=m
and
f=n
.) In the setting of the Ringel-Green theorem, the image is the
composition subalgebra
, which is the subalgebra generated by the simple objects. However, as we explain in Remark
7.5
, the image of our map
g
is
not
contained in the composition subalgebra. To remedy this, for a finitary category
\mathcal{C}
, we define the
spherical subalgebra

Hall^{\mathrm{sph}}(\mathcal{C})\subset Hall(\mathcal{C})
to be the subalgebra generated by the elements

\Theta_{\beta}:=\sum_{Y\in\min(\beta)}\alpha(Y)[Y],

(1.4)

where
\beta\in K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, the coefficient
\alpha(Y)
is the size of the automorphism group of
Y
, and the sum is over objects whose endomorphism ring has minimal dimension amongst all objects of class
\beta
. In the case of Hall algebras of coherent sheaves over a curve, our notion of spherical subalgebra agrees with the standard definition in the literature (see Remark
7.6
).

Apart from the spherical subalgebra, the Hall algebra has several other interesting subalgebras that we also study. Let
\Sigma
denote the set of all monic irreducible polynomials over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(including the zero polynomial). For any
\phi\in\Sigma
, there exist two sequences of representations
R_{\phi}(n)
and
R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}
for
n\geq 1
in
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
, which correspond to regular representations of the Kronecker quiver with
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
and
e=f=0
respectively (see Section
2.3
). Let
Hall_{\phi}
be the subalgebra of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
that is generated by these two sequences of representations.

Theorem B
.

(see Corollary
6.9
) The algebra
Hall_{\phi}
is isomorphic to a quantum Heisenberg algebra. Furthermore, if
\phi,\pi\in\Sigma
are polynomials of equal degree, the algebras
Hall_{\phi}
and
Hall_{\pi}
are canonically isomorphic.

Together, the spherical subalgebra
Hall^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
and the algebras
Hall_{\phi}
for
\phi\in\Sigma
generate the entire Hall algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
.

While the regular representations aren’t themselves in the spherical subalgebra, there are certain ‘averages’
R_{d}
(resp.
R_{d}^{\prime}
) of the elements
[R_{\phi}(n)]
(resp.
[R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}])
of dimension
(d,d)
which indeed lie in
Hall^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
. (These averages are exactly the sums defined using equation (
1.4
) above and are already defined in literature, e.g. in
[
Szá06
]
). Let
\mathcal{H}^{0}
be the subalgebra of
\mathcal{H}
generated by these elements
R_{n},R_{n}^{\prime},[M]^{\pm 1/4}
and
[M^{\prime}]^{\pm 1/4}
. Then, analogously to the above theorem, we show that the algebra
\mathcal{H}^{0}
is a quantum Heisenberg algebra, which can be viewed as a quantization of the maximal commutative subalgebra of
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
as a consequence of Proposition
8.11
(see Remark
8.12
). We use this observation and Theorem
B
to give an independent proof of Proposition
5.12
, which is a key step in the proof of Theorem
A
.

Finally, we establish some facts about the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
that parallel those of
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
.

Theorem C
.

The deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
has the following algebraic properties:

1.

A
\mathbb{C}(v)
-basis for the space
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is given by the set of ordered monomials in the defining generators of the algebra. (Corollary
7.23
)

2.

The center of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is equal to the ring of Laurent polynomials in the central charge
C^{1/2}
. (Proposition
8.5
)

3.

There exists a presentation for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
using four generators and finitely many relations. (Theorem
8.14
)

The finite presentation for
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is an analog of the Levendorskii presentation of the Yangian
[
Lev93
]
involving finitely many generators and relations, and is an extension of the finite presentation of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
in
[
FT19a
]
to the dominant co-weight case when
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
.

1.5
Future directions

One natural way to generalize our results is to study Hall algebras of blocks in categories of restricted
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-representations of a higher rank Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. These blocks are typically wild, but Gordon and Premet
[
GP02
]
have classified the blocks of finite and tame representation type. We intend to compute Hall algebras of these in future work. Categories of positive characteristic representations of finite groups of Lie type would also be natural candidates to study.

Another natural generalization is already suggested by our description of Bridgeland’s results. The starting point of his construction is the category of 2-periodic complexes of
Q
-modules (where
Q
is an acyclic quiver), but this is equivalent to the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(Q_{\mathrm{Bri}},\mathcal{R}ep(Q))
. By analogy, in future work we will study the Hall algebra of the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}},\mathcal{R}ep(Q))
and relate it to higher rank shifted affine algebras. The precise details of this relationship will be subtle, but this Hall algebra already has some nice structural properties; for example, it receives an algebra map from
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
for every vertex in
Q
. There is also an interesting model category structure on this functor category.

There are also a number of directions where results are harder to predict. For example, the spherical subalgebra strictly contains the composition subalgebra in many cases, but the Ringel-Green theorem only describes the composition subalgebra; describing the larger algebra explicitly in examples may be interesting. It would also be natural to study other versions of the Hall algebra construction for
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, such as the cohomological Hall algebra defined over
\mathbb{C}
.
Another natural question would be to study the theory of canonical bases for shifted quantum affine algebras: canonical basis constructions typically come from geometric/Hall frameworks, and our realization of
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
inside an explicit Hall algebra is well-placed to be the starting point for a canonical basis theory in the shifted setting. Finally, one would also hope to find applications of our Hall-theoretic computations to finite characteristic representation theory.
Other natural questions could be to find applications of our Hall-theoretic computations to finite characteristic representation theory and to the theory of canonical bases for shifted quantum affine algebras.

1.6
Structure and notation

In Section
2
we provide background and restate some results in the literature that we will use. We collect results of a homological nature in Section
3
, and we use these to perform certain Hall algebra computations in Section
4
. We construct the algebra map in the statement of Theorem
A
from the deformed quantum affine algebra in Section
5
by showing our Hall algebra computations imply the necessary relations. Regular representations only appear as averages in our spherical algebra; we compute relations between specific regular representations in Section
6
. We characterize the image of our map and prove injectivity in Section
7
. Finally, in Section
8
we provide a finite presentation of our algebra, show that it is a flat deformation of
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})^{FT}_{1,1}
, and compute its center.

Throughout the paper, we work over
\mathbb{C}
for concreteness, and all tensor products will be assumed to be over
\mathbb{C}
, unless specified otherwise.
We also include a summary of the most important notation:

1.

\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
– Simply connected shifted quantum affine algebra (see Definition
2.3
)

\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
– Adjoint version of the shifted quantum affine algebra (see Definition
2.3
)

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
– Deformed quantum affine algebra (see Definition
2.6
)

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}
– Integral form of the deformed quantum affine algebra (see Definition
8.9
)

2.

\mathbb{F}_{q}
– a finite field with
q
elements

v
– Formal parameter in quantum groups

\mathbf{v}
– Numerical value to which
v
is specialized (usually chosen so that
\mathbf{v}^{2}=q
)

[n]
,
[n]!
,
{n\choose k}
– quantum numbers (see Section
2.1
)

[x,y]_{q}:=xy-qyx
is the
q
commutator (whose generalized Jacobi relation is in equation (
4.7
))

3.

Q_{K}
and
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
– Kronecker quiver and Rudakov quiver (see Sections
2.3
and
2.4
)

P_{k},I_{k},R_{\phi}(k)
– indecomposable modules over
Q_{K}
(and over
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, with
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
) (see Section
2.3
)

M,M^{\prime}
– indecomposable
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
modules (but not
Q_{K}
modules) (see Section
2.4
)

\Sigma
–
\{\text{Set of all monic irreducible polynomials over }\mathbb{F}_{q}\}\cup\{0\}
(see Definition
2.11
)

4.

Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
– Hall algebra of the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
representations over
\mathbb{F}_{q}

Hall_{\phi}
– subalgebra of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
generated by
R_{\phi}(n)
and
R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}
, for some
\phi\in\Sigma
(see Definition
6.1
)

\mathcal{H}
– The algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
with
\left((q-1)[M]\right)^{-1/4}
and
\left((q-1)[M^{\prime}]\right)^{-1/4}
adjoined (see Definition
4.4
)

\mathcal{H}^{tw}
– The algebra
\mathcal{H}
with twisted multiplication (see Definition
5.1
)

Hall^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
– Spherical subalgebra of
Hall(-)
(see Definition
7.2
)

\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
– Spherical subalgebra of
\mathcal{H}^{tw}

We would especially like to thank B. Tsvelikhovskiy for many helpful and enthusiastic discussions at the beginning of this project, and, in particular, for explaining the results of

. The work of the second author is partially supported by a Simons Travel Grant.

2
Background

2.1
Infinite dimensional quantum groups

We work over the field of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
and fix an indeterminate
v
. Define the following
v
-integers and
v
-binomial coefficients:

\displaystyle[n]

\displaystyle:=\frac{v^{n}-v^{-n}}{v-v^{-1}},

\displaystyle[n]!

\displaystyle:=[n][n-1]\cdots[2][1],

\displaystyle{n\choose k}

\displaystyle:=\frac{[n]!}{[k]![n-k]!}.

(2.1)

It is possible to specialize the above elements at
v=\mathbf{v}
for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, since they are all Laurent polynomials in
v
. We will abuse the above notation and use it to refer to both the polynomials in
v
as well as their specializations.

Fix a finite index set
I
. Let
A=(a_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
be a symmetric generalized Cartan matrix and fix a realization
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{*},\Pi,\Pi^{\vee})
for
A
. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Kac-Moody algebra associated with the matrix
A
.

Definition 2.1
.

Define the
quantized Kac-Moody algebra

\mathcal{U}_{v}({\mathfrak{g}})
to be the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra generated by elements
\{E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in I\}
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle K_{i}K_{j}

\displaystyle=K_{j}K_{i}

\displaystyle K_{i}E_{j}K_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{a_{ij}}E_{j}

\displaystyle K_{i}F_{j}K_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-a_{ij}}F_{j}

\displaystyle[E_{i},F_{j}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{v-v^{-1}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{k}{1-a_{ij}\choose k}

\displaystyle E_{i}^{k}E_{j}E_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-k}=0

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{k}{1-a_{ij}\choose k}

\displaystyle F_{i}^{k}F_{j}F_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-k}=0,

(assuming
i\neq j
in the last two relations).
∎

In the above definition (and the rest of the paper), the binomial coefficients are the Gaussian ones defined in (
2.1
).

The algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}({\mathfrak{g}})
in the above definition, in fact, also has a Hopf structure defined as follows. The coproduct
\Delta
is defined by the equations

\displaystyle\Delta(K_{i})

\displaystyle=K_{i}\otimes K_{i}

\displaystyle\Delta(E_{i})

\displaystyle=E_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}\otimes E_{i}

\displaystyle\Delta(F_{i})

\displaystyle=1\otimes F_{i}+F_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{-1},

the antipode
S
is

S(K_{i})=K_{i}^{-1},\quad S(E_{i})=-K_{i}^{-1}E_{i},\quad S(F_{i})=-F_{i}K_{i},

and the counit
\epsilon
is

\epsilon(K_{i})=1,\quad\epsilon(E_{i})=\epsilon(F_{i})=0.

The algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}({\mathfrak{g}})
and its representation theory have been extensively studied, and are particularly well understood at least when
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a simple Lie algebra (see for example
[
Jan96
,
CP95
,
Kas95
,
Lus10
,
DCP93
]
). Furthermore, the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}({\mathfrak{g}})
has an integral form, which allows us to specialize
v=\mathbf{v}
for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
to get a
\mathbb{C}
-Hopf algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}({\mathfrak{g}})
. From the point of view of Hall algebras, the specialization at
v=q^{1/2}
for a prime power
q
is especially pertinent.

The next easiest case to study after simple Lie algebras, which will also be the example of interest to us, is when
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the affine Lie algebra
\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}
associated to a simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. The Lie algebra
\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}
can be identified with the universal central extension of the loop algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}[t,t^{-1}]
. A quantization of this identification gives a new presentation of the quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
, known as Drinfeld’s new realization:

Theorem 2.2
.

[
Dri87
,
Bec94
]

When
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is simple, the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
is isomorphic to the quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathcal{L}{\mathfrak{g}})
, which is the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra generated by elements
E_{i,l},F_{i,l},H_{i,n},K_{i}^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}
for
i\in I
and
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle C^{1/2}

is central

(2.2)

\displaystyle K_{i}E_{j,k}K_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{a_{ij}}E_{j,k}

(2.3)

\displaystyle K_{i}F_{j,k}K_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-a_{ij}}F_{j,k}

(2.4)

\displaystyle K_{i}H_{n}K_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=H_{n}

(2.5)

\displaystyle E_{i,k+1}E_{j,l}-v^{a_{ij}}E_{j,l}E_{i,k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{a_{ij}}E_{i,k}E_{j,l+1}-E_{j,l+1}E_{i,k}

(2.6)

\displaystyle F_{i,k+1}F_{j,l}-v^{-a_{ij}}F_{j,l}F_{i,k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{-a_{ij}}F_{i,k}F_{j,l+1}-F_{j,l+1}F_{i,k}

(2.7)

\displaystyle[E_{i,k},F_{j,l}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{i,k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{i,k+l}}{v-v^{-1}}

(2.8)

\displaystyle[H_{i,l},E_{j,k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[la_{ij}]}{l}C^{-\lvert l\rvert/2}E_{j,k+l}

(2.9)

\displaystyle[H_{i,l},F_{j,k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{-[la_{ij}]}{l}C^{\lvert l\rvert/2}F_{j,k+l}

(2.10)

\displaystyle[H_{i,l},H_{j,k}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{C^{l}-C^{-l}}{v-v^{-1}}

(2.11)

\displaystyle\textrm{Sym}_{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n}}\sum_{t=0}^{n:=1-a_{ij}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{t}{n\choose t}E_{i,k_{1}}E_{i,k_{2}}\dots E_{i,k_{t}}E_{j,l}E_{i,k_{t+1}}\dots E_{i,k_{n}}=0

(2.12)

\displaystyle\textrm{Sym}_{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n}}\sum_{t=0}^{n:=1-a_{ij}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{t}{n\choose t}F_{i,k_{1}}F_{i,k_{2}}\dots F_{i,k_{t}}F_{j,l}F_{i,k_{t+1}}\dots F_{i,k_{n}}=0,

(2.13)

(assuming
i\neq j
in the last two relations),
where the elements
\Psi_{i,k}
and
\Phi_{i,k}
are defined via the following generating series:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{i,k}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K_{i}\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{i,k}u^{k}\right)

(2.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{i,-k}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K_{i}^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{i,-k}u^{k}\right).

The central charge
C^{1/2}
in the above algebra acts by
1
on all finite dimensional representations of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
(up to a twist), and thus we often consider the quotient of the above algebra by the relation
C^{1/2}=1
(see next definition).
When
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
, the index set
I
consists of a single element, so we can (and will) suppress the first index from each generator in
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})
and write
E_{l}:=E_{i,l}
, etc. In this case, the isomorphism
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})\to\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathcal{L}{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
of the above theorem can be seen to be a consequence of the derived equivalence between the category of coherent sheaves over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and the category of representations of the Kronecker quiver (see
[
BS12b
]
).

The algebras that we are most interested in this paper are shifted versions of the quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})
defined by Finkelberg and Tsymbaliuk
[
FT19a
]
, that depend upon the choice of a pair of co-weights
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
. Given any co-weight
\mu
, we can define a sequence of integers
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
by evaluating
\mu
at the simple roots, that is
b_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(\mu)
.

Definition 2.3
.

[
FT19a
]
For any simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and co-weights
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, the simply connected
shifted quantum affine algebra

\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}^{FT}
is defined as the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra that is generated by elements
E_{i,l},F_{i,l},H_{i,n},(K_{i}^{\pm})^{\pm 1}
for
i\in I
and
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the same relations as (
2.2
)-(
2.13
) (with
C^{1/2}
specialized to
1
),
where the elements
\Psi_{i,k}
and
\Phi_{i,k}
are defined via the following generating series (instead of the series in (
2.14
)):

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{i,k-b_{i,1}}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K_{i}^{+}\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{i,k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{i,-k+b_{i,2}}u^{k}

\displaystyle=(K_{i}^{-})^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{i,-k}u^{k}\right),

where
b_{i,j}=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(\mu_{j})
for
i\in I
and
j\in\{1,2\}
.

There is also an
adjoint version

\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
of the shifted quantum affine algebra, which is obtained from
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}^{FT}
by introducing additional generators
\{(S_{i}^{\pm})^{\pm 1}:i\in I\}
and relations:

\displaystyle(S_{i}^{\pm})^{2}\prod_{j\neq i}(S_{j}^{\pm})^{a_{ij}}

\displaystyle=K_{i}^{\pm},

\displaystyle[S_{i}^{\pm},S_{j}^{\pm}]=[S_{i}^{\pm},S_{j}^{\mp}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[S_{i}^{\pm},H_{j}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle S_{i}^{\pm}E_{j}(S_{i}^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{\pm\delta_{ij}}E_{j},

\displaystyle S_{i}^{\pm}F_{j}(S_{i}^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{\mp\delta_{ij}}F_{j}.

∎

For
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
, the above relations simplify slightly and have been restated in Section
8.3
. Furtheremore, in that case, the choice of a pair of co-weights
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is equivalent to a choice of a pair of integers
(b_{1},b_{2})
.

It is clear that for all
i\in I
, the elements
K_{i}^{+}K_{i}^{-}
are central in the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
. When
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=0
, we have the following isomorphism with the usual quantum affine algebra:

\frac{\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{0,0}}{(K_{i}^{+}K_{i}^{-}-1)_{i\in I}}\cong\frac{\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})}{(C^{1/2}-1)}.

One way to think about these algebras is that the co-weights
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
dictate how non-commutative the loop generators of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
are. For example, consider the relation (
2.8
):

[E_{i,k},F_{i,l}]=\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{i,k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{i,k+l}}{v-v^{-1}}.

Note that, by definition,
\Psi_{i,n}=0
if
n<-b_{i,1}
and
\Phi_{i,n}=0
if
n>b_{i,2}
. Thus, if the co-weights
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are chosen to be anti-dominant, the integers
b_{i,j}^{\prime}s
are all negative, and then, in the range
b_{i,2}<k+l<-b_{i,1}
, the right side of the above commutator vanishes. On the other hand, if
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are dominant, the right side of the above commutator never vanishes, and therefore, fewer commutators vanish compared to the usual quantum affine algebra, as will be the case that is relevant to this paper. (Nevertheless, we do note here that the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
is actually a subalgebra of
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{0,0}
when
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
is dominant (see
[
FT19a
, Proposition 10.9]
), but we won’t be needing that fact in this paper.)

We note a structural result about these algebras from
[
FT19a
]
here which will be useful for us. Let
\mathcal{U}^{>0}

\mathcal{U}^{<0}
and
\mathcal{U}^{0}
be the subalgebras of
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
generated by the sets
\{E_{i,l}:i\in I,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,
\{F_{i,l}:i\in I,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and
\{H_{i,n},(K_{i}^{\pm})^{\pm 1}:i\in I,n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}
respectively.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
FT19a
, Proposition 5.1]
)
.

The multiplication map

\mathcal{U}^{>0}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{<0}\to\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}

is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}(v)
-vector spaces. Furthermore, the algebras
\mathcal{U}^{>0}
,
\mathcal{U}^{<0}
and
\mathcal{U}^{0}
are respectively generated by the sets
\{E_{i,l}:i\in I,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,
\{F_{i,l}:i\in I,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and
\{H_{i,n},(K_{i}^{\pm})^{\pm 1}:i\in I,n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}
with defining relations given by
\{\eqref{rrel4}\}
,
\{\eqref{rrel5}\}
and
\{\eqref{rrel3.5},\eqref{rrel9}\}
(with
C^{1/2}
specialized to
1
).

Remark 2.5
.

When
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
, a strengthening of the above proposition is given by the fact that ordered monomials in the defining generators form a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-basis for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{sc}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
. (For details, see
[
FT19a
, Theorem E.2]
or the proof of Proposition
8.11
.)
∎

Finally, we define the deformed quantum affine algebra which will be our main algebra of interest.

Definition 2.6
.

For any simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and co-weights
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, the
deformed quantum affine algebra

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
is defined as the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra that is generated by elements
E_{i,l},F_{i,l},H_{i,n},K_{i}^{\pm 1},S_{i}^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}
for
i\in I
and
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the same relations as (
2.2
)-(
2.13
), where the elements
\Psi_{i,k}
and
\Phi_{i,k}
are defined via the following generating series (instead of the series in (
2.14
)):

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{i,k-b_{i,1}}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K_{i}\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{i,k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{i,-k+b_{i,2}}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K_{i}^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{i,-k}u^{k}\right),

where
b_{i,j}=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(\mu_{j})
for
i\in I
and
j\in\{1,2\}
.
Additionally, we also impose the relations:

\displaystyle S_{i}^{2}\prod_{j\neq i}S_{j}^{a_{ij}}

\displaystyle=C^{1/2}K_{i},

\displaystyle[S_{i},S_{j}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[S_{i},H_{j}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle S_{i}E_{j}S_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{\delta_{ij}}E_{j},

\displaystyle S_{i}F_{j}S_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-\delta_{ij}}F_{j}.

∎

Remark 2.7
.

The main difference between the above definition and that of the adjoint version of the shifted quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})^{FT}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
is the presence of the central element
C^{1/2}
, which we do not specialize to 1 here. It is easy to see that the above algebra is actually defined over the ring
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1},(v-v^{-1})^{-1}]
. Therefore, we can talk about the specialization of this algebra
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
at
v=\mathbf{v}
for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{-1,0,1\}
. We find an integral form for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
in Section
8.2
, which will in fact allow the specialization at any non-zero
\mathbf{v}
. ∎

In this paper, we will solely be interested in the case when
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
. Therefore, we will identify the co-weights
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
with integers
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
. Furthermore, we will mostly be interested in the case
b_{1}=b_{2}=1
through the paper (except in Section
8.4
).

2.2
Hall algebras

We will use the following definition of the Hall algebra in the remainder of the paper.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an abelian category which is linear over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and assume that
\mathcal{C}
is
finitary
(which means
\Hom(M,N)
and
\Ext^{1}(M,N)
are finite dimensional for all
M,N
). The
Hall algebra
of
\mathcal{C}
is the vector space
Hall(\mathcal{C}):=\mathbb{C}\{X:X\in\mathrm{Iso}(\mathcal{C})\}
with basis the set of isomorphism classes of objects. The structure constants are given by the following formulas:

[M][N]:=\sum_{[E]}c_{M,N}^{E}[E],\qquad\qquad c_{M,N}^{E}:=\#\{E^{\prime}\subset E\mid E^{\prime}\simeq N\textrm{ and }E/E^{\prime}\simeq M\}.

(Both the sum and the structure constants are finite by the finitary condition.)
∎

It is an enjoyable but elementary exercise to show the associativity of this product, and that the structure constants come from the convolution diagram (
1.1
), where the space of objects is viewed as a discrete space. We also note that a rescaling of the generators
[X]\mapsto\widetilde{[X]}:=\alpha(X)[X]
(where
\alpha(X)
is the size of the automorphism group of
X
) leads to the following structure constants which are sometimes more convenient to compute:

\widetilde{c}_{\widetilde{M},\widetilde{N}}^{\widetilde{E}}=\frac{\lvert\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)_{E}\rvert}{\lvert\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)\rvert},

where
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)_{E}
is the subset of extensions whose middle term is isomorphic to
E
(see, e.g.
[
Bri13
, Sec. 2.3]
).

It is evident from the definition that
Hall(\mathcal{C})
is graded by the Grothendeick group
K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
. This grading allows us to twist the multiplication in the Hall algebra. Precisely, when
\mathcal{C}
is hereditary, Ringel and Green defined the following twisted product:

[X]\diamond[Y]:=\frac{\lvert\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\rvert}{\lvert\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\rvert}[X][Y].

We too will twist our Hall algebra before constructing a map from the (twisted affine) quantum group, but our twist is slightly different from Green’s (see Definition
5.1
).

One of the first modern theorems about Hall algebras was proved by Ringel and extended by Green. Given a quiver
Q
, let
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{Q})
be the positive part of the associated quantum Kac-Moody algebra.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
Rin90
,
Gre95
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with no oriented cycles and vertex set
I
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
be the category of representations of
Q
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Then there is an injective algebra map

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{Q})\hookrightarrow Hall^{tw}(\mathcal{C}_{Q}),\qquad E_{i}\mapsto[S_{i}]

where
E_{i}
is the standard generator associated to vertex
i
, and
S_{i}
is the simple module at vertex
i
, and where the parameter
v
is specialized to
q^{1/2}
. Furthermore, this map is an isomorphism precisely when
Q
is some orientation of a simply-laced Dynkin diagram.

In
[
Bri13
]
, Bridgeland found a realization of the entire quantum group using periodic complexes as follows. Given a quiver
Q
with no oriented cycles, let
C(Q)
be the abelian category of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded complexes in
\mathcal{R}ep(Q)
, and let
C(P)
be the subcategory of complexes of projective objects. Let
DH(Q)
be the localization of the (twisted) Hall algebra of
C(P)
at the set of acyclic complexes (so
[M_{\bullet}]
becomes invertible if
H_{\ast}(M_{\bullet})=0
). Finally, let
DH_{\mathrm{red}}(Q)
be the quotient by the relations
[M]=1
for all acyclic, shift-invariant complexes
M
.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
Bri13
]
)
.

There is an injective algebra map
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})\hookrightarrow DH_{\mathrm{red}}(Q)
specializing
v
to
q^{1/2}
. Again, this map is an isomorphism precisely when
Q
is some orientation of a simply-laced Dynkin diagram.

The Hall algebra of the Kronecker quiver

In this section we summarize some results in the literature about the Kronecker quiver:

(2.15)

First, the classification of indecomposable objects in
\mathcal{R}ep(Q_{K})
is well known, and every indecomposable is in one of the following families:

•

Pre-projective representations
: For any
n\geq 0
, the pre-projective indecomposable representation
P_{n}
is of dimension
(n,n+1)
and is given by:

P_{n}:=\hbox to119.29pt{\vbox to26.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 59.64468pt\lower-15.17274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}

Regular representations
: Fix any monic irreducible polynomial
\phi
of degree
d
over the field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
M_{\phi}
denote the companion matrix of
\phi
, which is a

d\times d
matrix whose characteristic polynomial is
\phi
. Then, for any
n\geq 1
, we can construct the following
n\times n
block matrix:

M_{\phi}(n):=\begin{bmatrix}M_{\phi}&I_{d\times d}&&&\\
&M_{\phi}&I_{d\times d}&&\\
&&.&.&\\
&&&.&.\\
&&&&M_{\phi}\end{bmatrix},

where
I_{d\times d}
is the identity matrix and all the empty blocks are zero.
Then there exists an indecomposable representation
R_{\phi}(n)
of dimension
(dn,dn)
given by:

R_{\phi}(n):=\hbox to117.49pt{\vbox to31.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 58.74503pt\lower-16.40884pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where
J_{n}
is a regular nilpotent matrix.

Definition 2.11
.

For later reference, define

\Sigma:=\{\text{monic irreducible polynomials over }\mathbb{F}_{q}\}\cup\{0\}=:\bigcup_{d\geq 1}\Sigma_{d},

where
\Sigma_{d}
represents the subset of monic irreducible polynomials of degree
d
. Here, by convention, we put
0
in the set
\Sigma_{1}
.
∎

Definition 2.12
.

We define the following sequence of elements
(R_{n})
in the Hall algebra:

R_{n}:=\frac{q^{n}}{q-1}\sum\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{1}}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{2}}}\right)\cdots\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{r}}}\right)[R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus R_{\phi_{2}}(n_{2})\oplus\cdots R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})],

where the sum is over all finite sets
\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\cdots,\phi_{r}\}
of monic irreducible polynomials such that
\deg(\phi_{i})=l_{i}
and
\sum l_{i}n_{i}=n
. As a convention, we define
R_{0}=\frac{1}{q-1}
.
∎

It will be helpful in later sections to be able to write a more convenient expression for
R_{n}
.

Definition 2.14
.

To shorten notation, write
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{r})
and
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{r})
, and write
\underline{\phi}\cdot\underline{n}=\sum_{i}(\deg(\phi_{i}))n_{i}
. We also write
R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n}):=R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})
, and we will say
\underline{\phi}
is
minimal
if all the
\phi_{i}
are distinct. ∎

This definition of minimality is generalized in Definition
7.2
.

Lemma 2.15
.

We have the identities

\displaystyle R_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q-1}\sum_{\underline{\phi}\cdot\underline{n}=m}{\alpha\left(R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n})\right)}[R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n})]

\displaystyle[R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n})]

\displaystyle=[R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})]\cdots[R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})],

where the sum is taken over minimal
\underline{\phi}
and the product is taken inside the Hall algebra.

Proof.

By
[
Mac15
, Chapter II, (1.6)]
, if
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
we have that
\alpha(R_{\phi}(k))=|Z(M_{\phi}(k))|=q^{dk}(1-q^{-d})
(where
Z(-)
is the centralizer in
GL_{k\deg{\phi}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
). Note that if
\underline{\phi}
is minimal, then
[
Szá06
, Lem. 1.1(g)]
(recalled in Theorem
2.17
below) implies
\Hom(R_{\phi_{i}},R_{\phi_{j}})=0
when
i\not=j
. Then bilinearity of the
\Hom(-,-)
functors implies
\alpha(R_{\underline{\phi}})(\underline{n})=\prod_{i}\alpha(R_{\phi_{i}}(n_{i}))
. We then compute

\displaystyle R_{n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{q^{n}}{q-1}\sum\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{1}}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{2}}}\right)\cdots\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{r}}}\right)[R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus R_{\phi_{2}}(n_{2})\oplus\cdots R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q-1}\sum\big\lvert Z(M_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1}))\big\rvert\cdots\big\lvert Z(M_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r}))\big\rvert[R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus R_{\phi_{2}}(n_{2})\oplus\cdots R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q-1}\sum\alpha\left((R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus R_{\phi_{2}}(n_{2})\oplus\cdots R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})\right)[R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus R_{\phi_{2}}(n_{2})\oplus\cdots R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q-1}\sum\alpha\left(R_{\underline{\phi},\underline{n}}\right)[R_{\underline{\phi},\underline{n}}].

The product identity follows from the fact that for minimal
\underline{\phi}
, there are no extensions between any of the summands (again see Theorem
2.17
below), which means the only term appearing in the Hall product is the direct sum. The coefficient is 1 by Lemma
4.2
, since there are no nonzero homomorphisms between the summands (again by Theorem
2.17
).
∎

Remark 2.16
.

For any point
\phi=[\phi_{0}:\phi_{1}:\cdots:\phi_{n}]\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\phi_{0}\neq 0
, we can view
\phi
as a (not necessarily irreducible) monic polynomial of degree
n
whose coefficients are
\phi_{i}/\phi_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
M_{\phi}
denote the unique (up to conjugation)
n\times n
matrix with characteristic polynomial equal to
\phi
, that has a centralizer of minimal size amongst all such matrices. (That is,
M_{\phi}
is a regular element in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
.) Define the representation
R_{\phi}
as

R_{\phi}:=\hbox to109.17pt{\vbox to31.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.58215pt\lower-15.1916pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Similarly, if
\phi_{i}=0
for
0\leq i\leq k-1
, we can view
\phi
as a polynomial
\phi^{\prime}
of degree
n-k
and define

R_{\phi}:=R_{\phi^{\prime}}\oplus R_{0}(k).

Then, a reformulation of the previous lemma says that the element
R_{n}
can be rewritten as:

R_{n}=\frac{1}{q-1}\sum_{\phi\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\alpha(R_{\phi})[R_{\phi}].

∎

The Hall algebra of the Kronecker quiver (and its relation to the Hall algebra of coherent sheaves over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
) has been studied by a number of authors (see, e.g.
[
Kap97
,
BK01
,
BS12b
,
Szá06
,
Zha00
,
McG05
,
XZ06
,
Sch06
,
Sch12b
]
). For the convenience of the reader, we now recall some of these results that we will use throughout the paper, translated into our notation.

Theorem 2.17
.

The following statements are proved in
[
Szá06
]
Lemmas 1.1, 4.1 and Theorems 4.2, 4.3.

(1.1a)

If
R
is regular,
I
is pre-injective, and
P
is pre-projective, then

\Hom(R,P)=\Hom(I,P)=\Hom(I,R)=\Ext^{1}(P,R)=\Ext^{1}(P,I)=\Ext^{1}(R,I)=0.

(1.1b)

If
\phi\not=\pi
, then

\Hom(R_{\phi}(n),R_{\pi}(m))=0\,\textrm{ and }\,\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(n),R_{\pi}(m))=0.

(1.1c)

For
n\leq m
, we have
\dim\Hom(P_{n},P_{m})=m-n+1
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{n},P_{m})=0
;
otherwise
\Hom(P_{n},P_{m})=0
and
\dim\Ext^{1}(P_{n},P_{m})=n-m-1
. In particular

\End(P_{n})\cong\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{n},P_{n})=0
.

(1.1f)

If
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
and
r\geq 1
, then

\dim\Hom(P_{n},R_{\phi}(r))=\dim\Hom(R_{\phi}(r),I_{n})=dr=\dim\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(r),P_{n})=\dim\Ext^{1}(I_{n},R_{\phi}(r)).

(1.1g)

If
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
, then

\dim\Hom(R_{\phi}(m),R_{\phi}(n))=\dim\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(m),R_{\phi}(n))=d\min(m,n).

(4.1b)

If
X=R_{\phi}(1)
with
\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
, then

[X][P_{n}]=[P_{n+1}]+q[P_{n}][X],\qquad[I_{n}][X]=[I_{n+1}]+q[X][I_{n}].

(4.2)

If
i<j
, we have the identities

\displaystyle[P_{i}][P_{j}]

\displaystyle=[P_{i}\oplus P_{j}]

\displaystyle[P_{i}^{\oplus n}][P_{i}^{\oplus m}]

\displaystyle=\binom{m+n}{m}_{q}[P_{i}^{\oplus m+n}]

\displaystyle[P_{j}][P_{i}]

\displaystyle=q^{j-i+1}[P_{j}\oplus P_{i}]+q^{j-i}(q-q^{-1})\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lfloor(j-i)/2\rfloor}[P_{j-\ell}\oplus P_{i+\ell}].

(4.3)

We have the identity

[I_{n-1-i}][P_{i}]=R_{n}+q^{n-1}[P_{i}][I_{n-1-i}].

(4.9)

If
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
, we have the identity

[I_{m}][R_{\phi}(k)]=q^{dk}[R_{\phi}(k)][I_{m}]+I_{m+dk}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(q^{d(k-i)}-q^{d(k-i-1)}\right)[R_{\phi}(k-i)][I_{m+di}].

(4.12)

For
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 0
we have the identity

R_{n}[P_{m}]=q^{n}[P_{m}]R_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(q^{n+i}-q^{n+i-2})[P_{m+i}]R_{n-i}.

2.4
The Rudakov quiver

In this section we describe the quiver (with relations)
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
that Rudakov used in
[
Rud82
]
to describe blocks in the category of equi-characteristic restricted representations of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

.

The
Rudakov quiver
is the following quiver-with-relations:

(2.16)

Throughout the paper we will refer to this quiver modulo relations as
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
.∎

Rudakov classified indecomposable representations of this quiver in the following theorem, and we use this classification extensively throughout the paper.
3
3

3

Technically, Rudakov works with the quiver where the relations are
ef^{\prime}=fe^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}f=f^{\prime}e
, but it is clear that those relations give an equivalent category of representations, the equivalence being given by negating
f^{\prime}
.
For the reader’s convenience, we provide a self-contained proof of this classification theorem at the end of this section.

Theorem 2.19

(
[
Rud82
]
)
.

All but two of the indecomposable representations of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
satisfy either
e=f=0
or
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
. The other two indecomposables are the objects
M
and
M^{\prime}
, described explicitly in equations (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) below; they are both projective and both injective.

A corollary of the above theorem is that all indecomposable representations of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
except
M
,
M^{\prime}
and the two simples have Loewy length two.

Remark 2.20
.

Because of the previous theorem, it is most efficient to reuse notation from the previous section to describe
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
modules; more precisely, if
Y
is a module over the Kronecker quiver, we will write
Y
for the
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
module with the same vector spaces and maps
e,f
, and with
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
. (See Example
2.22
for an instance of this notation.)
∎

Let
A
be the path algebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
and let
s_{1},s_{2}\in A
be the idempotents projecting onto vertex 1 and 2, respectively.

Definition 2.21
.

For any
V\in\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
, we define the vector spaces:

V_{1}:=s_{1}\cdot V,\qquad\qquad V_{2}:=s_{2}\cdot V.

We will often also use the shorthand
V=(V_{1},V_{2})
. Furthermore, we will use
e_{V}:V_{1}\to V_{2}
to refer to the linear map in
V
corresponding to the action of
e\in{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, etc.
∎

The two representations
M
and
M^{\prime}
of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
in the above theorem both have dimension
(2,2)
. If we write
M_{1}=\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle
and
M_{2}=\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle
, we can represent the maps in
M
explicitly via the diagram

. All the arrows that have not been drawn are equal to zero, and the arrow notation means
e\cdot x_{1}=y_{1}
,
f^{\prime}\cdot y_{1}=-x_{2}
etc.

(2.18)

(We use the above bases for
M
and
M^{\prime}
throughout the paper, whenever there is no ambiguity.)

Example 2.22
.

Let us illustrate an explicit example of an exact sequence involving
M
:

0\to I_{0}\to M\to P_{1}\to 0.

The image of the inclusion map is spanned by
x_{2}\in M
, and the quotient is obviously isomorphic to
P_{1}
. This shows that the subcategory of
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
consisting of objects where
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
is not closed under extensions, which is an important fact throughout the paper.
∎
