in
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
, where
V
and
W
are
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-vector spaces and
e,f,e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
are linear maps that satisfy the relations in equation (
2.16
).

Definition 2.23
.

We define two commuting endofunctors
\sigma,\tau:\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})\to\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
, with
\sigma
covariant and
\tau
contravariant. The functor
\sigma
swaps the vector spaces
V
and
W
and swaps the linear map
e
with
e^{\prime}
and
f
with
f^{\prime}
. Next, let
\tau
be the map that replaces
V
and
W
by their duals and sends

\tau(e)=(e^{\prime})^{\ast},\quad\tau(f)=(f^{\prime})^{\ast},\quad\tau(e^{\prime})=e^{\ast},\quad\tau(f^{\prime})=f^{\ast}.

If
Y
is a representation of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, we often denote
\sigma(Y)
by
Y^{\prime}
.
∎

It is clear that
\sigma
and
\tau
are both involutions that commute with each other. Also, we note/define:

\displaystyle\sigma(P_{n})

\displaystyle=:P_{n}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\sigma(I_{n})

\displaystyle=:I_{n}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\sigma(M)

\displaystyle=M^{\prime},

\displaystyle\sigma(M^{\prime})

\displaystyle=M

\displaystyle\tau(P_{n})

\displaystyle=I_{n}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\tau(I_{n})

\displaystyle=P_{n}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\tau(M)

\displaystyle=M,

\displaystyle\tau(M^{\prime})

\displaystyle=M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{\phi}(n))

\displaystyle=:R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime},

\displaystyle\tau(R_{\phi}(n))

\displaystyle=R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime},

for all
\phi\in\Sigma
.

Remark 2.24
.

For non-projective modules, the functor
\sigma
can also be expressed in terms of the syzygy functor: for an arbitrary ring
R
, let
\mathcal{C}
denote the category of left
R
-modules and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
denote the stable module category obtained by factoring out the projectives from
\mathcal{C}
. We assume that
R
is perfect, so that every object in
\mathcal{C}
has a projective cover. Then, there exists a syzygy functor
\Omega:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
which takes an object
X
to the kernel of its projective cover. The restriction of
\Omega
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivalence of categories.

When
R
is the path algebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, we have the following set of equalities (by Lemma
3.16
) for
n\geq 1
and
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
:

\begin{aligned} \Omega(P_{n})&=\sigma(P_{n-1}),\\
\Omega(I_{n-1})&=\sigma(I_{n}),\\
\Omega(R_{\phi}(n))&=\sigma(R_{\phi}(n)),\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \Omega(P_{n}^{\prime})&=\sigma(P_{n-1}^{\prime}),\\
\Omega(I_{n-1}^{\prime})&=\sigma(I_{n}^{\prime}),\\
\Omega(R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime})&=\sigma(R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}).\end{aligned}

The discrepancy in indices in the first two lines can be viewed as another manifestation of the presence of the “shifted” algebra in our main theorem (also see Remark
4.13
). This discrepancy is related to the classical reflection functors of
[
BGP73
]
. For example, if
X\in\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is
(e,f)
-saturated (see Definition
3.3
), then we have the equality
\Omega(X)=\sigma\circ\mathbb{S}^{+}(X)
, where
\mathbb{S}^{+}
is a reflection functor associated with the Kronecker subquiver of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
with maps
e
and
f
(see
[
BS12b
, Definition 3.1]
).
∎

Lemma 2.25
.

The map
\sigma
induces an algebra automorphism and the map
\tau
induces an algebra anti-automorphism on the Hall algebra.

Proof.

Since both these functors are equivalences they induce invertible maps on the Hall algebra, and since
\tau
is contravariant, it induces an anti-automorphism.
∎

Definition 2.26
.

We will say that a module with dimension vector
(d_{1},d_{2})
has
positive slope
if
d_{2}-d_{1}>0
, and we similarly define
zero slope
and
negative slope
.
∎

We note that the Hall algebra is graded by the abelian group of dimension vectors, so the subspace of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
spanned by positive slope modules is a subalgebra.

Let
A
be the path algebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, considered as a
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
module.

Lemma 2.27
.

The path algebra
A
splits as a sum
A\cong M\oplus M^{\prime}
, and the summands are also injective.

Proof.

Over the field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, the path algebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
is an
8
-dimensional vector space and has a basis given the following paths:

\{s_{1},s_{2},e,e^{\prime},f,f^{\prime},ef^{\prime},f^{\prime}e\},

where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are paths of length zero with source and end at vertices 1 and 2 respectively, and the identity element is
1=s_{1}+s_{2}
. As a left
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-module, the path algebra
A
splits into a sum of two modules as follows:

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle:=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\{s_{1},e,f,f^{\prime}e\}\cong As_{1},

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle:=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\{s_{2},e^{\prime},f^{\prime},ef^{\prime}\}\cong As_{2}.

It is clear that
V\cong M
and
W\cong M^{\prime}
, which implies both
M
and
M^{\prime}
are projective modules. (In fact, this shows that they are the only projective indecomposables.) As the contravariant functor
\tau
preserves both
M
and
M^{\prime}
, we conclude that they are both injective modules as well.
∎

Corollary 2.28
.

If
Y
is any
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
representation, we have the following isomorphisms of vector spaces:

\displaystyle\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(M,Y)\cong Y_{1}\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(M^{\prime},Y)\cong Y_{2}

\displaystyle\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(Y,M)\cong Y^{\vee}_{1}\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(Y,M^{\prime})\cong Y^{\vee}_{2}.

Proof.

Recall that for an algebra
A
and a left
A
-module
X
, the space
\Hom_{A\mathrm{-Mod}}(A,X)
inherits a left
A
-module structure from the right action of
A
on itself. If
A
is unital, then
\Hom_{A\mathrm{-Mod}}(A,X)
is canonically isomorphic to
X
via the map
\left[f:A\to X\right]\mapsto f(1_{A})
. Then we have the following equalities of vector spaces:

\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(M,Y)=\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(As_{1},Y)=s_{1}\cdot\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(A,Y)=s_{1}\cdot Y=Y_{1}

where the first equality uses Lemma
2.27
and the second and third equalities follow from the above isomorphism
\Hom_{A\mathrm{-Mod}}(A,Y)=Y
. The second claim follows from the computation

\Hom(Y,M)=\Hom(\tau(M),\tau(Y))=\Hom(M,\tau(Y))=Y^{\vee}_{1}

where
Y_{1}^{\vee}
is the linear dual.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.19
.

Let
X=(X_{1},X_{2})
be any indecomposable representation of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
. We first assume that the composition
f^{\prime}e
is not zero in
X
, so there exists
x\in X_{1}
such that
0\neq f^{\prime}e(x)=-e^{\prime}f(x)
. Then it is easy to see that the space spanned by the vectors

\{x,e(x),f(x),f^{\prime}e(x)\}

is a submodule of
X
that is isomorphic to
M
. Since
M
is injective and
X
is indecomposable, this implies that
X=M
.

By a similar argument, we can show that if the composition
ef^{\prime}
is not zero in
X
, then
X
is isomorphic to
M^{\prime}
. So, we assume henceforth that
ef^{\prime}=0
and
f^{\prime}e=0
. This implies that the composition of any two of
e,e^{\prime},f
and
f^{\prime}
on
X
is zero. Consider the following vector space decompositions:

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle=(\ker(e)\cap\ker(f))\oplus X_{1}^{\prime},

\displaystyle X_{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Im}(e)+\mathrm{Im}(f))\oplus X_{2}^{\prime}

for some
X_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq X_{2}
. Then, it is clear that:

\displaystyle V:

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Im}(e)+\mathrm{Im}(f))\oplus X_{1}^{\prime},

\displaystyle W:

\displaystyle=(\ker(e)\cap\ker(f))\oplus X_{2}^{\prime}

are submodules of
X
and that
X=V\oplus W
. Since
X
was assumed to be indecomposable, we must have that either
V={0}
or
W=0
. In the first case we have that
e=f=0
and the second we have that
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
, completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

2.5
Rudakov’s theorem and
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})

In this section we discuss the precise relation between Rudakov’s quiver and the representation theory of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Let
F
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.

Definition 2.29
.

A
restricted Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},[p])
over
F
is a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
F
equipped with a linear map
(\cdot)^{[p]}:{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}
, called the
p
-operator, satisfying the following conditions:

1.

\mathrm{ad}\text{ }x^{[p]}=(\mathrm{ad}\text{ }x)^{p}
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

2.

(ax)^{[p]}=a^{p}x^{[p]}
for all
a\in F
and
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

3.

(x+y)^{[p]}=x^{[p]}+y^{[p]}+\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}s_{i}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}

where
s_{i}(x,y)
is
1/i
times the coefficient of
t^{i}
in the formal expansion of
(\mathrm{ad}\text{ }(tx+y))^{p-1}(x)
.
∎

We suppress
[p]
from the definition and simply refer to
{\mathfrak{g}}
as a restricted Lie algebra.

Example 2.30
.

Let
A
be an associative algebra over
F
. Consider the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathrm{Der}(A)
of
F
-linear derivations on
A
and fix
D\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. By an elementary computation involving the Leibniz rule, it can be shown that the linear map
D^{p}=D\circ D\circ\cdots\circ D
(
p
times) is itself a derivation, and thus,
D^{p}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then, the linear map
D\mapsto D^{p}
provides a restricted Lie algebra structure on
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

In the special case when
A
is the algebra of regular functions on an algebraic group
G
defined over
F
, the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the space of left invariant vector fields on
G
and is equivalent to the Zariski tangent space to
G
at the identity. The above construction equips
{\mathfrak{g}}
with a restricted structure (which is the unique restricted structure on
{\mathfrak{g}}
when it is a simple Lie algebra).
∎

With the above restricted structure, the element
x^{p}-x^{[p]}
is central in the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. The commutative subalgebra of
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
generated by such elements is known as the
p
-center of
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
. A representation
V
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
is called a
restricted representation
if the
p
-center acts trivially on
V
. Thus, a restricted representation is the same as a module over the
restricted enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}_{0}({\mathfrak{g}})
defined as:

\mathcal{U}_{0}({\mathfrak{g}}):=\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})/(x^{p}-x^{[p]}:x\in{\mathfrak{g}}).

We will be interested in the case when
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle e,f,h\rangle
, where
q
is some power of
p
. In that case, the construction in the above example gives the following explicit restricted structure on
{\mathfrak{g}}
:

e^{[p]}=0,\quad f^{[p]}=0,\quad h^{[p]}=h.

Henceforth, we will be assuming that
p>2
throughout this section. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of (equi-characteristic, finite dimensional)
restricted
representations of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. By the discussion above, these are the representations which are annihilated by
e^{p},f^{p}
, and
h^{p}-h
. Just as in the characteristic 0 case, let
V_{n}:=\mathbb{F}_{q}[x,y]_{n}
be the
Weyl module
. Explicitly, this is the space of degree
n
polynomials in 2 variables, which is a module over
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
via the standard action

e\mapsto y\partial_{x},\quad f\mapsto x\partial_{y},\quad h\mapsto y\partial_{y}-x\partial_{x}.

Theorem 2.31

(
[
Jac41
,
Pol68
]
)
.

The Weyl modules
V_{n}
are indecomposable for all
n\geq 0
and are simple if and only if
n<p
. This is a complete list of the simples in the category
\mathcal{C}
. For
0\leq i,j\leq p-2
, we have

\dim(\Ext^{1}(V_{i},V_{j}))=\begin{cases}2&\textrm{if }i+j=p-2\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}.

On the other hand,
V_{p-1}
is simple, projective, and injective and is known as the Steinberg representation. Furthermore, as a left module over itself, the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{0}({\mathfrak{g}})
splits as a sum of indecomposable modules:

\mathcal{U}_{0}({\mathfrak{g}})=V_{p-1}^{\oplus p}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=0}^{p-2}Q_{i}^{\oplus i+1},

where
Q_{i}
is the principal indecomposable module surjecting onto
V_{i}
.

These results imply that the category
\mathcal{C}
splits up into the following blocks:

\mathcal{C}\simeq Vect\oplus\bigoplus_{i=0}^{(p-3)/2}\mathcal{C}_{i}

where the block
\mathcal{C}_{i}
contains exactly 2 simple Weyl modules (
V_{i}
and
V_{p-i-2}
), and
Vect
is generated by the Steinberg module
V_{p-1}
. As a result, the Hall algebras of these categories are related as:

Hall(\mathcal{C})\cong Hall(Vect)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=0}^{(p-3)/2}Hall(\mathcal{C}_{i}).

Since
Vect
is semisimple and generated by the unique simple object, the algebra
Hall(Vect)
is isomorphic to the polynomial ring in one variable. Thus, in order to study
Hall(\mathcal{C})
, it suffices to study
Hall(\mathcal{C}_{i})
for each
i
. Fix
0\leq i\leq(p-3)/2
and let
j=p-i-2
.

Theorem 2.32

(
[
Rud82
]
)
.

Each block
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is equivalent to the category
\mathcal{R}ep_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
of representations of the Rudakov quiver. Under this equivalence, the simples
I_{0}
and
P_{0}
are sent to
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
respectively.

Example 2.33
.

Let
V
be an extension of
V_{i}^{m}
by
V_{j}^{n}
for some
m,n\geq 0
. By Theorem
2.31
, we know that

\Ext^{1}(V_{i}^{n},V_{j}^{m})\cong\Ext^{1}(V_{i},V_{j})^{mn}\cong(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2})^{mn}\cong(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{mn})^{2}.

Therefore, the data of any such extension can be thought of as being given by two
m\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. This gives a representation of
Q_{Rud}
having dimension
(m,n)
: The maps
e
and
f
can be defined as being given by these matrices, whereas the maps
e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
are taken to be zero. Similarly, representations of
Q_{Rud}
with
e=f=0
correspond to extensions of
V_{j}^{m}
by
V_{i}^{n}
.
∎

Here, we create a table which translates between representations in the categories
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}ep_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
.

\mathcal{R}ep_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})

\mathcal{C}_{i}

P_{n}

V_{np+j}

P_{n}^{\prime}

V_{np+i}

I_{n}

(V_{np+i})^{\vee}

I_{n}^{\prime}

(V_{np+j})^{\vee}

R_{\phi}(n)

Indecomposable maximal proper submodules of
(V_{np+i})^{\vee}

(Equivalently, indecomposable minimal proper quotients of
V_{np+j}
)

R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}

Indecomposable maximal proper submodules of
(V_{np+j})^{\vee}

(Equivalently, indecomposable minimal proper quotients of
V_{np+i}
)

M

Q_{i}

M^{\prime}

Q_{j}

Remark 2.34
.

For
n\geq 0
, our map from the (deformed) quantum affine algebra to
Hall(\mathcal{C}_{i})
sends the generators
E_{n}
and
F_{n}
to the Weyl modules (up to central coefficients involving
[Q_{i}]
and
[Q_{j}]
). Furthermore, the spherical subalgebra of
Hall(\mathcal{C}_{i})
(see Definition
7.2
) is generated by
[V_{i}]
,
[V_{j}]
,
[V_{i}\oplus V_{j}]
and
[Q_{i}]
by the remark following Corollary
5.5
.∎

Remark 2.35
.

In general, we can consider representations of a restricted Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
on which the
p
-center doesn’t necessarily act trivially. That is, we can consider the category of modules over the reduced enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}_{\chi}({\mathfrak{g}}):=\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})/(x^{p}-x^{[p]}-\chi(x)^{p}:x\in{\mathfrak{g}}),

where
\chi\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
is an arbitrary linear function known as the
p
-character. These categories have been quite extensively studied in geometric representation theory (see, for example,
[
Hum71
,
FP87
,
Pre97
,
Gor01
,
BMR08
,
BMR06
]
).

Rudakov’s theorem (Theorem
2.32
) was generalized in Section 9.14 of
[
GP02
]
. More generally, for good primes
p
, they classify
p
-characters
\chi
and the blocks of
\mathcal{U}_{\chi}(\mathfrak{g})
that have finite and tame representation type, and find a quiver theoretic description in the tame cases. In particular, tame blocks exist precisely when
\chi
is a regular or a subregular element of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
.∎

3
Homological Statements

In this section we collect and prove various homological statements involving representations of the Kronecker and Rudakov quivers. Roughly, these results will be used to compute whether certain Hall algebra structure constants
c_{M,N}^{E}
are zero or nonzero. The precise computation of the actual values of (certain) structure constants will be performed in the next section.

3.1
The Kronecker quiver

The main goal of this section is to understand the precise relationship between representations of the Kronecker quiver
Q_{K}
and of the Rudakov quiver
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
. In particular, we will show that a “large” subcategory of
Q_{K}
-representations is equivalent to “half” of the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-representations. This induces a map from a
subalgebra of the Hall algebra of the Kronecker quiver to the Hall algebra of the Rudakov quiver, and this allows us to use Hall algebra identities from the literature that were discussed in the previous section.

Definition 3.1
.

The
extension by 0
functor

Func:\mathcal{R}ep(Q_{K})\to\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})

outputs the same vector spaces and maps, with the maps
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}
defined to be
0
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

The extension by 0 functor is exact and fully faithful.

Proof.

This is obvious from the definition, or from the fact that the extension by 0 is isomorphic to the restriction of scalars along the surjection
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}\to Q_{K}
of path algebras taking
e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
to 0.
∎

Even though it is exact and fully faithful,
Func
does not induce a map on Hall algebras because its image is not closed under extensions. For example, in the following short exact sequence,
I_{0}
and
P_{1}
are in the image of
Func
, but
M
isn’t:

0\to I_{0}\to M\to P_{1}\to 0

(see Example
2.22
). However, this short exact sequence is essentially the only obstruction to the existence of a map between Hall algebras. To make this statement precise, we provide the following definitions.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
Y
be a representation of the Kronecker quiver
Q_{K}
or the Rudakov quiver
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
.

1.

Y
is
(e,f)
-injective
if
\ker(e_{Y})\cap\ker(f_{Y})=0
.

2.

Y
is
(e,f)
-surjective
if
\mathrm{coker}(e_{Y}\oplus f_{Y})=0
.

3.

Y
is
(e,f)
-saturated
if it is both
(e,f)
-injective and
(e,f)
-surjective.

4.

For
Q\in\{Q_{K},{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}\}
, we denote by
Sat(Q)\subset\mathcal{R}ep(Q)
be the full subcategory of
(e,f)
-saturated objects.

For
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, we modify the above definitions by replacing “
(e,f)
” with “
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
” if
e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
satisfy the analogous conditions. For example,
Y
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-surjective
if
\mathrm{coker}(e^{\prime}\oplus f^{\prime})=0
, etc.
∎

It is easy to see which
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
indecomposables satisfy these properties (assuming
n\geq 1
):

(e,f)
-inj.

(e,f)
-surj.

(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-inj.

(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-surj.

I_{0}
,
M

✓

✓

P_{0}
,
M^{\prime}

✓

✓

P_{n},I_{n},R_{\phi}(n)

✓

✓

P^{\prime}_{n},I^{\prime}_{n},R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}

✓

✓

.

Note from the above table that an indecomposable is
(e,f)
-saturated or
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-saturated precisely when its Loewy length is two.

Remark 3.4
.

These definitions do not seem to be widely used in the quiver literature, so we provide some elementary remarks.

1.

For the Kronecker quiver, the saturated objects are precisely those without a simple summand.

2.

The functor
\sigma
swaps the
(e,f)
-versions of the above definition with the respective
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-counterparts. The functor
\sigma\circ\tau
swaps
(e,f)
-injective (respectively
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-injective) representations with
(e,f)
-surjective (respectively
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-surjective) representations.

3.

Finally, the full subcategory of
(e,f)
-saturated representations is not an abelian subcategory: for any regular
(1,1)

(e,f)
-saturated representation
X
, there exists a short exact sequence

0\to X\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to 0.

Then
X
and
P_{1}
are
(e,f)
-saturated but the quotient
P_{0}
is not.

∎

The next lemmas show that these definitions interact well with short exact sequences.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
0\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}Z\to 0
is a short exact sequence of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-representations. If
X
and
Z
are
(e,f)
-injective, then
Y
is too. Similarly, if
X
and
Z
are
(e,f)
-surjective, then
Y
is too.

Proof.

To prove the first claim, suppose
y\in\ker(e_{Y})\cap\ker(f_{Y})
. Since
Z
is
(e,f)
-injective, this implies
\pi(y)=0
, so there exists
x\in X
with
\iota(x)=y
. Then
\iota(e_{X}(x))=0=\iota(f_{X}(x))
, and since
\iota
is injective and
X
is
(e,f)
-injective, this implies
x=0
.

For the second claim, suppose
y\in Y_{2}
. Since
Z
is
(e,f)
-surjective, there exist
z_{e},z_{f}\in Z_{1}
with
\pi(y)=e_{Z}(z_{e})+f_{Z}(z_{f})
. Pick
\pi
-lifts
y_{e},y_{f}\in Y_{1}
of
z_{e},z_{f}
respectively. Then
\pi(y-e_{Y}(y_{e})-f_{Y}(y_{f}))=0
, so there exists
x\in X_{2}
with
\iota(x)=y-e_{Y}(y_{e})-f_{Y}(y_{f})
. Since
X
is
(e,f)
-surjective, there exist
x_{e},x_{f}\in X_{1}
with
x=e_{X}(x_{e})+f_{X}(x_{f})
. Then

\displaystyle e_{Y}(y_{e}+\iota(x_{e}))+f_{Y}(y_{f}+\iota(x_{f}))

\displaystyle=e_{Y}(y_{e})+\iota(e_{X}(x_{e}))+f_{Y}(y_{f})+\iota(f_{X}(x_{f}))

\displaystyle=e_{Y}(y_{e})+f_{Y}(y_{f})+\iota(x)

\displaystyle=e_{Y}(y_{e})+f_{Y}(y_{f})+(y-e_{Y}(y_{e})-f_{Y}(y_{f}))

\displaystyle=y.

∎

Lemma 3.6
.

If
Y
is
(e,f)
-surjective, then

\mathrm{Im}(e^{\prime})+\mathrm{Im}(f^{\prime})\subset\ker(e)+\ker(f).

If
Y
is
(e,f)
-saturated, then
e^{\prime}_{Y}=0=f^{\prime}_{Y}
.

Proof.

Since
Y
is
(e,f)
-surjective, if
y\in Y_{2}
, then we can find
a,b\in Y_{1}
with
y=e(a)+f(b)
. Then

\displaystyle e^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}(e(a))+e^{\prime}(f(b))

\displaystyle=0-f^{\prime}(e(b))

\displaystyle\in\ker(f).

Similarly,
\mathrm{Im}(f^{\prime})\subset\ker(e)+\ker(f)
. If
Y
is also
(e,f)
-injective, this implies
e^{\prime}(y)=0=f^{\prime}(y)
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The converse of Lemma
3.5
is false: take
X
and
Z
to be the two simples and
Y
to be a regular representation, which is
(e,f)
-saturated. Also, Lemma
3.6
uses the relation
e^{\prime}f=-f^{\prime}e
in an essential way in the second line of the displayed equality.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

The extension by 0 functor
Func
induces both an equivalence
Sat(Q_{K})\to Sat({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
and an algebra map
Hall(Sat(Q_{K}))\to Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
.

Proof.

Lemma
3.2
shows
Func
is fully faithful and exact, which implies its restriction to
Sat(Q_{K})
is also fully faithful and exact. Then the first claim follows from Lemma
3.6
, which shows the restriction
Func:Sat(Q_{K})\to Sat({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is essentially surjective. The second claim follows from this and Lemma
3.5
, which shows that
Sat({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is closed under extensions.
∎

This proposition shows that except when
A
is pre-projective and
B
is pre-injective, any extensions of
Func(A)
by
Func(B)
in
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
already come from extensions of
A
and
B
in
\mathcal{R}ep(Q_{K})
.

Remark 3.9
.

Technically, our definition of the Hall algebra starts with an abelian category, and
Sat({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is not abelian. However, since it is an extension-closed full subcategory category of an abelian category,
Sat({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is an
exact
category. In
[
Hub06
, Sec. 2]
, Hubery provides a definition of the Hall algebra of an exact category – his results immediately imply that there is an injective algebra map
Hall(Sat({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}))\hookrightarrow Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
.
∎

3.2
Computations with Hom and Ext

In this section we provide some homological computations that will be necessary to prove the Hall algebra identities we need.

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose
X
is
(e,f)
-surjective and
Y
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-saturated. Then

\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(X,Y)=\Hom_{Vect}(X_{1},Y_{1}).

If
X
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-surjective and
Y
is
(e,f)
-saturated, then

\Hom_{{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}}(X,Y)=\Hom_{Vect}(X_{2},Y_{2}).

Proof.

Since
Y
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-saturated,
e_{Y}=0=f_{Y}
, so any map
\alpha:X\to Y
must satisfy
\alpha\circ e_{X}=0=\alpha\circ f_{X}
. Since
X
is
(e,f)
-surjective, any
x_{2}\in X_{2}
is in the image of
e_{X}\oplus f_{X}
. Combining these facts implies
\alpha_{2}:X_{2}\to Y_{2}
must be 0. Conversely, since
e_{Y}=0=f_{Y}
, any linear map
\alpha_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{1}
can be extended to a map
(\alpha_{1},0=\alpha_{2}):X\to Y
of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
modules. The second claim follows from the first by applying the covariant functor
\sigma
(which switches
Y_{1}\leftrightarrow Y_{2}
and switches the pairs
(e,f)\leftrightarrow(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
).
∎

For the next two propositions, fix a point
[m:n]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and let
X
be the
(e,f)
-saturated regular representation of dimension
(1,1)
such that the maps
e
and
f
are multiplication by
m
and
n
respectively, and the maps
e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
are zero. Without loss of generality, we will assume that
m\neq 0
. Thus, we have that
X=R_{\phi}(1)
, where
\phi(t)=t-n/m
.

Lemma 3.11
.

We have
\Ext^{1}(X,P_{0}^{\prime})=0
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{0}^{\prime},X)=\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and the nontrivial extension is
I_{1}
.

Proof.

Since
P_{0}^{\prime}=I_{0}
is
(e,f)
-surjective, any extension of
X
by
I_{0}
(or vice-versa) must be
(e,f)
-surjective and have dimension
(2,1)
. Since
M
has dimension
(2,2)
, it can’t be a summand of any such extension, so all extensions arise from those in the Kronecker quiver. Then this lemma is a consequence of
[
Szá06
, Lemma 1.1]
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

For
k\geq 0
we have
\Ext^{1}(X,P_{k+1}^{\prime})=0
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{k+1}^{\prime},X)=\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and the nontrivial extension is
M^{\prime}\oplus P^{\prime}_{k}
.

Proof.

We start with a linear map
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}^{k+2}
, which is given by the inclusion into the first
k+1
coordinates. This map at vertex 1 of the quiver induces an inclusion of representations
P_{k}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\iota}P_{k+1}^{\prime}.
Then we have a short exact sequence

0\to P_{k}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\iota}P_{k+1}^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}\to 0,

where the quotient
X^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-saturated regular representation such that the maps
e,f
and
e^{\prime}
are zero, whereas the map
f^{\prime}
is an isomorphism. Thus, we have that
X^{\prime}=R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}
, where
\pi(t)=0
.

Applying
\Ext^{\bullet}(X,-)
to this sequence gives the following piece of the long exact sequence:

\Ext^{1}(X,P_{k}^{\prime})\to\Ext^{1}(X,P_{k+1}^{\prime})\to\Ext^{1}(X,X^{\prime}).

The first term in this sequence is 0 by induction, and the last term is 0 by Lemma
3.14
.

For the second claim, we apply
\Ext^{\bullet}(-,X)
to the same short exact sequence to obtain the exact sequence

\Ext^{1}(X^{\prime},X)\to\Ext^{1}(P^{\prime}_{k+1})\to\Ext^{1}(P^{\prime}_{k},X).

The first term in this sequence is 0 by Lemma
3.14
, and the last term is
\mathbb{F}_{q}
by induction. Finally, to show the middle term is nonzero, we construct a nonsplit extension of
P^{\prime}_{k}
by
X
as follows:

0\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}M^{\prime}\oplus P_{k}^{\prime}\to P_{k+1}^{\prime}\to 0.

The inclusion is defined by
\iota(x)=mx_{2}+nx_{1}+v
, where
x\in X_{1}
is a generator and
v
is a basis vector for the first coordinate at vertex
1
of
P_{k}^{\prime}
. (We’re using notation from (
2.18
) for
x_{1},x_{2}\in M^{\prime}
.) Then, it is easy to check that the cokernel of the above sequence is indeed isomorphic to
P_{k+1}^{\prime}
, proving the claim.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
R
be an arbitrary
(e,f)
-saturated regular representation of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
and
S=P_{0}
or
I_{0}
. Then, any extensions of
R
by
S
, and vice-versa, arise from those in the Kronecker quiver.

Proof.

We will only prove the claim when
S=I_{0}
, since the claim for
P_{0}
follows by applying the functor
\sigma\circ\tau
.

Since
R
and
I_{0}
are both
(e,f)
-surjective, so must be any extension between them. Therefore, the only possible indecomposable summand of such an extension that doesn’t arise from the Kronecker quiver is
M
. Suppose we have a short exact sequence

0\to I_{0}\xrightarrow{\Theta}M\oplus V\to R\to 0,

for some representation
V
. Since
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
in
R
, we must have that
\langle x_{2}\rangle\oplus 0\subseteq\Theta(I_{0})
(using notation from (
2.17
) for
x_{2}\in M
), so that the maps
e^{\prime}_{M}
and
f^{\prime}_{M}
become zero in the quotient. But then, the quotient
(M\oplus V)/\Theta(I_{0})\cong P_{1}\oplus V
can not be isomorphic to
R
.

Next, suppose we have a short exact sequence

0\to R\xrightarrow{\Theta}M\oplus V\to I_{0}\to 0,

for some representation
V
. Since
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
in
R
, we must have that
\Theta(R)\subseteq\langle x_{2}\rangle\oplus V
. But then, the maps
e_{M}
and
f_{M}
won’t be zero in the quotient, so the quotient can’t be isomorphic to
I_{0}
.
∎

3.3
Regular representations

Recall that the set
\Sigma
can be partitioned into subsets
\Sigma_{d}
consisting of polynomials of degree
d
. The subset
\Sigma_{1}
is in natural bijection with the set of points
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(see Remark
2.16
) as follows:

\displaystyle\phi(x)=x-c\in\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow[1:c]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),

\displaystyle 0\in\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow[0:1]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).

The following lemma describes the most fundamental homological properties of regular representations:

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\phi,\pi\in\Sigma
.

(a)

If
\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
, then

\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(1),R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{F}_{q},

and the nontrivial extension is isomorphic to
M
. Similarly,

\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime},R_{\phi}(1))\cong\mathbb{F}_{q},

and the nontrivial extension is
M^{\prime}
.

(b)

If
\phi\neq{\pi}
, then
\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(m),R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime})=\Ext^{1}(R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime},R_{\phi}(m))=0
.

Proof.

Suppose we have a non-split extension of
R_{\phi}(1)
by
R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}
. Such an extension will be a representation of dimension
(2,2)
such that both pairs
(e,f)
and
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
of maps are nonzero. It is clear that the direct sum can not occur as a non-split extension. Therefore, the only possible candidate of such a representation is
M
. (It is not possible to have
M^{\prime}
as it does not have a subrepresentation isomorphic to
R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}
for any
\pi\in\Sigma_{1}
.) So, we assume that we have the following short exact sequence:

0\to R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}\to M\to R_{\phi}(1)\to 0.

Suppose the polynomials
\phi
and
\pi
respectively correspond to the points
[m:n]
and
[m^{\prime}:n^{\prime}]
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
under the above bijection. Explicitly, this means that

R_{\phi}(1)\cong\hbox to107.11pt{\vbox to31.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.55518pt\lower-15.38054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 32.93 6.67 L -30.16 6.67}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-21.80008pt}{4.81943pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-22.74977pt}{9.00554pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Fix the basis
\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}
for
M
as described in equation (
2.17
). Then, the only subrepresentation
W
of
M
that is isomorphic to
R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}
is the one spanned by
\{x_{2},-n^{\prime}y_{1}+m^{\prime}y_{2}\}
. For the quotient
M/W
to be isomorphic to
R_{\phi}(1)
, we must have that
ne(x_{1})=mf(x_{1})
or
ny_{1}=my_{2}
in
M/W
. This is possible if and only if
ny_{1}-my_{2}
lies in the span of
\{x_{2},-n^{\prime}y_{1}+m^{\prime}y_{2}\}
. This implies that
nm^{\prime}-mn^{\prime}=0
, which is equivalent to the condition that
\phi={\pi}
. This implies the first claims in (a) and the
m=n=1
case of (b) when
\pi,\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
. The second claims in (a) and the
m=n=1
case of (b) follow by a similar argument.

We next prove part (b) when
\pi,\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
and when at least one of
m,n
is greater than
1
. In this case, we have short exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to R_{\phi}(n-1)

\displaystyle\to R_{\phi}(n)\to R_{\phi}(1)\to 0

\displaystyle 0\to R_{\pi}(m-1)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime}\to R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}\to 0.

We can construct long exact sequences of Ext functors from these short exact sequences, which can be used to prove by induction that
\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(n),R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime})=\Ext^{1}(R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime},R_{\phi}(n))=0
for all
m,n
.

Finally, we prove part (b) when
\phi,\pi\in\Sigma
are arbitrary. Pick a large enough finite extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
in which both
\phi
and
\pi
split. Then, in the category of modules for
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, the objects
R_{\phi}(n)
and
R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime}
both split into sums of representations of dimension
(1,1)
. Furthermore, by assumption, none of the roots of
\phi
are equal to any of the roots of
\pi
. So, using part (a), we conclude that there aren’t any non-trivial extensions between
[R_{\phi}(n)]
and
[R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime}]
over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. But this implies that there can’t be any non-trivial extensions between
[R_{\phi}(n)]
and
[R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime}]
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
either. This is because if such an extension
V
existed, we would have that
V
splits as a sum of regular representations of dimension
(1,1)
over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. However, this implies that
V
must be a regular representation over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(as no other representations split upon extension). Furthermore, any regular direct summand of
V
should correspond to an irreducible polynomial whose roots coincide with those of either
\phi
or
\pi
, which proves that it is a trivial extension.
∎

We then have the following immediate corollary of the previous lemma:

Corollary 3.15
.

Let
\phi,\pi\in\Sigma_{1}
.

(a)

If
\phi={\pi}
, then

\displaystyle[R_{\phi}(1)][R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=q[R_{\phi}(1)\oplus R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}]+[M],

\displaystyle[R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}][R_{\phi}(1)]

\displaystyle=q[R_{\phi}(1)\oplus R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}]+[M^{\prime}].

(b)

If
\phi\neq{\pi}
, then

[R_{\phi}(m)][R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime}]=q^{mn}[R_{\phi}(m)\oplus R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime}]=[R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime}][R_{\phi}(m)].

Proof.

For all
\phi,\pi
, Lemma
3.10
shows that
\dim(\Hom(R_{\phi}(1),R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}))=\dim(\Hom(R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime},R_{\phi}(1)))=1
. If
\phi={\pi}
, Lemma
3.14
shows that the only non-trivial extension of
R_{\phi}(1)
by
R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}
is
M
, which proves the first equality. The second equality follows by a similar argument.

Conversely, if
\phi\not={\pi}
, Lemma
3.14
shows
\Ext^{1}(R_{\phi}(n),R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime})=0=\Ext^{1}(R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime},R_{\phi}(n))
. Lemma
3.10
then implies
\dim(\Hom(R_{\phi}(m),R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime})=mn=\dim(\Hom(R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime},R_{\phi}(m))
; combining this with Lemma
4.2
implies the final equality.
∎

3.4
Projective resolutions of indecomposables

In this section, we record projective resolutions of all indecomposable objects in
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
. While we won’t be needing these in the rest of the paper, they may be of independent interest. (In fact, we use certain Hall product computations to find these resolutions.) In particular, we show that the category
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
has infinite global dimension.

Recall that the only indecomposable projective objects in
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
are
M
and
M^{\prime}
. In the following lemma, we construct surjections from projective objects to every other indecomposable object:

Lemma 3.16
.

(a)

For all
n\geq 1
, there exist short exact sequences:

\displaystyle 0\to P^{\prime}_{n-1}\to M^{n}\to P_{n}\to 0

,

\displaystyle 0\to P_{n-1}\to M^{\prime n}\to P_{n}^{\prime}\to 0

,

\displaystyle 0\to I^{\prime}_{n}\to M^{n}\to I_{n-1}\to 0

,

\displaystyle 0\to I_{n}\to M^{\prime n}\to I_{n-1}^{\prime}\to 0

.

(b)

For all
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
and all
n\geq 1
, there exist short exact sequences:

\displaystyle 0\to R_{{\phi}}(n)^{\prime}\to M^{dn}\to R_{\phi}(n)\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to R_{{\phi}}(n)\to M^{\prime dn}\to R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}\to 0.

Proof.

(a)

Taking
n\geq 1
and
m=1-n
in Theorem
4.12
, we get that
M^{n}
occurs as an extension of
P_{n}
by
P_{n-1}^{\prime}
(since it occurs in their Hall product), proving the existence of the first sequence. The others can be seen to follow similarly from Theorem
4.12
, or by applying
\sigma,\tau
and
\sigma\circ\tau
to the first sequence respectively.

(b)

By Lemma
6.3
, we conclude that
M^{dn}
occurs in the Hall product
[R_{\phi}(n)][R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}]
with non-zero coefficient, proving the existence of the first short exact sequence. The second one follows by applying
\sigma
to the first sequence.

∎

Corollary 3.17
.

We have projective resolutions of the following form for all
n\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{\prime n+4}\to M^{n+3}\to M^{\prime n+2}\to M^{n+1}\to I_{n}\to 0

,

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{n+4}\to M^{\prime n+3}\to M^{n+2}\to M^{\prime n+1}\to I_{n}^{\prime}\to 0

,

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{\prime 3}\to M^{2}\to M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}\to M^{2}\to\cdots\to M^{n-1}\to M^{\prime n}\to M^{n+1}\to P_{n+1}\to 0

\displaystyle\text{ (}n+1\text{ even),}

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{3}\to M^{\prime 2}\to M\to M\to M^{\prime 2}\to\cdots\to M^{n-1}\to M^{\prime n}\to M^{n+1}\to P_{n+1}\to 0

\displaystyle\text{ (}n+1\text{ odd),}

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{3}\to M^{\prime 2}\to M\to M\to M^{\prime 2}\to\cdots\to M^{\prime n-1}\to M^{n}\to M^{\prime n+1}\to P_{n+1}^{\prime}\to 0

\displaystyle\text{ (}n+1\text{ even),}

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{\prime 3}\to M^{2}\to M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}\to M^{2}\to\cdots\to M^{\prime n-1}\to M^{n}\to M^{\prime n+1}\to P_{n+1}^{\prime}\to 0

\displaystyle\text{ (}n+1\text{ odd).}

For all
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
and
n\geq 1
, we have projective resolutions of the following form:

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{\prime dn}\to M^{dn}\to M^{\prime dn}\to M^{dn}\to R_{\phi}(n)\to 0

,

\displaystyle\cdots\to M^{dn}\to M^{\prime dn}\to M^{dn}\to M^{\prime dn}\to R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}\to 0

.

Remark 3.18
.

Applying
\tau
to the above sequences, we get injective resolutions for all the indecomposables.
∎

Lemma 3.19
.

If
X
is any non-projective
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-representation, then
X
has infinite projective dimension. In particular, the category
\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
has infinite global dimension.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we will assume that
X
is indecomposable. First, suppose
X
is a preprojective or a preinjective. Thus, the object
X
doesn’t have slope zero. However, as
M
and
M^{\prime}
are the only projective indecomposables in our category, both of which have slope zero, an Euler characteristic argument shows that
X
can not have a finite projective resolution.

Next, suppose
X
is a regular indecomposable. Without loss of generality, let
X=R_{\phi}(n)
for some
\phi\in\Sigma
. The proof is by induction on
n
. Suppose
X
has projective dimension
k
. Thus, there must be a short exact sequence:

0\to Y\to P\to X\to 0,

where
P
is projective, and
Y
has projective dimension
k-1
. By the first part of the proof, we get that
Y
can not have any preprojective or preinjective summands, and so
Y
must be a direct sum of regular representations. Then, by Lemma
3.14
, we must have that
Y
is a direct sum of representations of the form
R_{\phi}(m)^{\prime}
, for varying
m
. By induction, if
m<n
, we get that
R_{\phi}(m)^{\prime}
has infinite projective dimension. So, we can assume that
Y=R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}
. However, by the duality
\sigma
, the projective dimensions of
R_{\phi}(n)
and
R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}
should be equal, which gives us the required contradiction, showing that their projective dimension must be infinite.
∎

4
Hall algebra computations

In this section we prove a number of identities in the (untwisted) Hall algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
. In the next section we will use these identities to prove our main theorem – the existence of an algebra map from the deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
to our (twisted, localized) Hall algebra. We start by proving several elementary lemmas that we use frequently throughout this section.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are modules with no isomorphic indecomposable summands. Then

\mathrm{Aut}(X\oplus Y)=\mathrm{Aut}(X)\times\mathrm{Aut}(Y)\times\Hom(X,Y)\times\Hom(Y,X).

Proof.

Note that

\mathrm{End}(X\oplus Y)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{End}(X)&\mathrm{Hom}(Y,X)\\
\mathrm{Hom}(X,Y)&\mathrm{End}(Y)\end{bmatrix}.

Pick a block matrix
\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}
in the above space. This block matrix can be written as the sum

\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&B\\
C&0\end{bmatrix},

where the second summand is nilpotent by Fitting’s Lemma. Therefore, the matrix
\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}
is invertible if and only if
\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}
is invertible, proving the claim.
∎

We combine this with
[
Rin96
, Proposition 4]
to obtain the following useful technical lemma:

Lemma 4.2
.

If
\Ext^{1}(X,Y)=0
, then the coefficient
c
in the product
[X][Y]=c[X\oplus Y]
is

c=\frac{\alpha({X\oplus Y)}}{\alpha(X)\alpha(Y)\big\lvert\Hom(X,Y)\big\rvert}

where
\alpha(P):=|\mathrm{Aut}(P)|
. If
X
and
Y
have no isomorphic indecomposable summands, then
c=\lvert\Hom(Y,X)\rvert
.

Proof.

The first claim is
[
Rin96
, Proposition 4]
, and the second claim follows directly from Lemma
4.1
. ∎

We start by studying the Hall products involving the projective representations
M
and
M^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The elements
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
are central in the Hall algebra.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we only need to show that
[M]
is central. Since the Hall algebra is generated as an algebra by the indecomposable representations by
[
BG16
]
(see Theorem
7.20
), we only need to show that
[M]
commutes with
[Y]
for every indecomposable
Y\neq M
.
We know
\Ext^{1}(Y,M)=\Ext^{1}(M,Y)=0
since
M
is projective and injective, so Lemma
4.2
implies

\displaystyle[Y]\cdot[M]

\displaystyle=q^{\dim(\Hom(M,Y))}[Y\oplus M],

\displaystyle[M]\cdot[Y]

\displaystyle=q^{\dim(\Hom(Y,M))}[Y\oplus M].

Then Corollary
2.28
shows the equality of the powers of
q
in these two products.
∎

Recall that in Bridgeland’s construction of the quantum group
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\mathfrak{g})
, acyclic complexes are inverted and shift-invariant cyclic complexes are set equal to 1. Our construction requires a localization of a similar flavor – it is well-defined because
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
are central and hence satisfy the Ore conditions.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the localization of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
at the elements
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
with formal fourth roots of
(q-1)[M]
and
(q-1)[M^{\prime}]
adjoined

\mathcal{H}:=Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})\langle a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle/R

where the relations
R
we impose are that
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
are central, and that

a^{4}=(q-1)[M],\quad ab=1,\quad(a^{\prime})^{4}=(q-1)[M^{\prime}],\quad a^{\prime}b^{\prime}=1.

From now on we will very mildly abuse notation and write
(q-1)^{1/4}[M]^{1/4}:=a
, and so on.
∎

Most of the computations in the rest of this section have two flavors: computing relations inside the Hall algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
between specific modules using Definition
2.8
, or performing formal algebraic manipulations in
\mathcal{H}
to show our Hall algebra relations imply the relations in the deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
(see Definition
2.6
). For the convenience of the reader, we collect all identities of the first type into the following Theorem.

Theorem 4.5
.

The following identities hold in the (untwisted) Hall algebra of the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules and are proved in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
:

\displaystyle[[X],[P_{n}]]_{q}

\displaystyle=[P_{n+1}]

\displaystyle[[I_{n}],[X]]_{q}

\displaystyle=[I_{n+1}]

\displaystyle[[P_{n+1}^{\prime}],[X]]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q-1)[M^{\prime}][P_{n}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle[[X],[I_{n+1}^{\prime}]]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q-1)[M][I_{n}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle[[I_{0}],[P_{0}]]

\displaystyle=R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle[[I_{0}],[P_{1}]]_{q}

\displaystyle=R_{2}-q[M]

\displaystyle[[X],R_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=[M]-[M^{\prime}]

\displaystyle[[P_{1}],[P_{1}^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=(q-1)([M]R_{1}^{\prime}-[M^{\prime}]R_{1})

\displaystyle[M],[M^{\prime}]\,

\displaystyle\textrm{are central}.

The following identities between regular representations are proved in Section
6
:

\displaystyle[[R_{\phi}(n)],[R_{\pi}(m)]]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[[R_{\phi}(n)^{\prime}],[R_{\pi}(m)^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[[R_{\phi}(m)],[R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mathrm{if}\quad\phi\neq{\pi}.

If
\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
, then

\displaystyle[R_{\phi}(1)][R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=q[R_{\phi}(1)\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}]+[M],

\displaystyle[R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}][R_{\phi}(1)]

\displaystyle=q[R_{\phi}(1)\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}]+[M^{\prime}].

More generally, when
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
we shorten notation by writing
J_{m}:=R_{\phi}(m)
and
J_{n}^{\prime}:=R_{{\phi}}(n)^{\prime}
, and prove

[J_{m}][J_{n}^{\prime}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\min\{m,n\}}c_{k}[J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}]

where the constants
c_{k}
are computed explicitly in Lemma
6.3
.

Remark 4.6
.

The identities from Section
6
in the above theorem aren’t strictly needed in the proof of our main result Theorem
5.4
(although we do use them to provide an independent proof of Proposition
5.12
in the form of Proposition
6.13
).
We state them here so that this is a complete list of the identities that we prove Hall-theoretically (instead of by formal algebraic manipulation).
∎

4.1
Slope subalgebras

Recall that a
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-module
Y
has positive slope if
\dim(Y_{1})<\dim(Y_{2})
. This condition is preserved under extensions, so positive slope modules span a subalgebra of the Hall algebra. In this section we study relations between indecomposables with positive (resp. negative) slope (see Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
).

It will be helpful to first prove some relations involving “small” regular representations; to simplify notation, let
X
be an indecomposable regular representation
R_{\phi}(1)
with
\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
. We will view
\phi
as a point
[a:b]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, so that
e
and
f
are multiplication by
a
and
b
respectively. Next, fix
X^{\prime}=R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}
such that
\pi\in\Sigma_{1}
and
\pi\neq\phi
. Thus, by Corollary
3.15
, the elements
[X]
and
[X^{\prime}]
commute.

First, we consider the commutators of
[X]
and
[X^{\prime}]
with non-regular indecomposables in the “same direction.” The following relations can thus be seen as relations in a Kronecker subquiver of the
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For all
n\geq 0
, we have:

\displaystyle q[X][I_{n}]-[I_{n}][X]=-[I_{n+1}],

\displaystyle[X][P_{n}]-q[P_{n}][X]=[P_{n+1}],

\displaystyle q[X^{\prime}][I_{n}^{\prime}]-[I_{n}^{\prime}][X^{\prime}]=-[I_{n+1}^{\prime}],

\displaystyle[X^{\prime}][P_{n}^{\prime}]-q[P_{n}^{\prime}][X^{\prime}]=[P_{n+1}^{\prime}].

Proof.

We only need to prove the first equality, since the others follows from it by applying
\sigma\circ\tau
,
\sigma
and
\tau
respectively. When
n\geq 1
, we have that
I_{n}
is
(e,f)
-saturated, and so the statement is a consequence of Proposition
3.8
and
[
Szá06
, Lemma 4.1(b)]
. When
n=0
, we use Lemma
3.11
and
[
Szá06
, Lemma 4.1(b)]
to conclude the result.
∎

Next, we consider commutators with non-regular indecomposables in the “opposite direction.”

Lemma 4.8
.

For all
n\geq 0
, we have:

\displaystyle q[X][P_{n+1}^{\prime}]-[P_{n+1}^{\prime}][X]

\displaystyle=-(q-1)[M^{\prime}][P_{n}^{\prime}],

\displaystyle[X][I_{n+1}^{\prime}]-q[I_{n+1}^{\prime}][X]

\displaystyle=(q-1)[M][I_{n}^{\prime}],

\displaystyle q[X^{\prime}][P_{n+1}]-[P_{n+1}][X^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=-(q-1)[M][P_{n}],

\displaystyle[X^{\prime}][I_{n+1}]-q[I_{n+1}][X^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=(q-1)[M^{\prime}][I_{n}].

Proof.

Again, we only need to prove the first equality, since the others follows from it by applying
\sigma\circ\tau
,
\sigma
and
\tau
respectively. The proof is by induction on
n
. For
n=0
, the required equality is a consequence of the following Hall product computations:

\displaystyle[X][P_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=q[X\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}],

(4.1)

\displaystyle[P_{1}^{\prime}][X]

\displaystyle=q^{2}[X\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}]+(q-1)[M^{\prime}\oplus P_{0}^{\prime}].

(4.2)

To compute these products, Lemma
3.12
shows
\Ext^{1}(X,P_{1}^{\prime})=0
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{1}^{\prime},X)=\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and also shows that in the latter case, the nontrivial extension is isomorphic to
M^{\prime}\oplus P^{\prime}_{0}
. To complete the proof of (
4.1
), all that remains is the computation of the coefficients.

To compute the coefficients in (
4.1
), we note that
\dim(\Hom(P_{1}^{\prime},X))=1
(see Lemma
3.10
). For (
4.2
), the coefficient of
[X\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}]
follows from
\dim(\Hom(X,P_{1}^{\prime}))=2
(see Lemma
3.10
). In order to compute the coefficient of
[M^{\prime}\oplus P_{0}^{\prime}]
, we need to compute the number of subrepresentations
W
of
M^{\prime}\oplus P_{0}^{\prime}
isomorphic to
X
such that
(M^{\prime}\oplus P_{0}^{\prime})/W\cong P_{1}^{\prime}
.

Fix a basis vector
x
for the simple representation
P_{0}^{\prime}
and basis
\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}
for
M^{\prime}
as in equation (
2.18
). Then, the only subrepresentations
W
of
P_{0}^{\prime}\oplus M^{\prime}
that are isomorphic to
X
are the ones that are spanned by
\{ax_{2}+bx_{1}+cx,y_{2}\}
for some
c\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. If
c\neq 0
, the quotient
(M^{\prime}\oplus P_{0}^{\prime})/W
is isomorphic to
M^{\prime}/X\oplus P_{0}^{\prime}
, which is decomposable, and thus, not isomorphic to
P_{1}^{\prime}
. On the other hand, when
c\neq 0
, it is clear that the quotient
(M^{\prime}\oplus P_{0}^{\prime})/W
is indeed isomorphic to
P_{1}^{\prime}
. Thus, there are
q-1
valid choices for
c
, which completes the proof of (
4.2
). This proves the base case
n=0
for the statement of the lemma.

For the inductive step, suppose the equality

q[X][P_{n+1}^{\prime}]-[P_{n+1}^{\prime}][X]=-(q-1)[M^{\prime}][P_{n}^{\prime}]

(4.3)

is known for some
n\geq 0
. We take the
q
-commutator of both sides with
[X^{\prime}]
:

\displaystyle[[X^{\prime}],LHS\eqref{eq:tempo}]_{{q}}

\displaystyle=q[X][[X^{\prime}],[P_{n+1}^{\prime}]]_{q}-[[X^{\prime}],[P_{n+1}^{\prime}]]_{q}[X]

\displaystyle=q[X][P_{n+2}^{\prime}]-[P_{n+2}^{\prime}][X],

where the first equality uses the fact that
[X^{\prime}]
commutes with
[X]
, and the second equality follows from Lemma
4.7
. Similarly, using Lemma
4.7
and the fact that
[M^{\prime}]
is central, we compute that
[[X^{\prime}],RHS\eqref{eq:tempo}]_{q}=-(q-1)[M^{\prime}][P_{n+1}^{\prime}]
. Equating the
q
-commutators of both sides completes the induction.
∎

Next, to simplify notation we define the following sequences of elements in the localization
\mathcal{H}
of the Hall algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
:

\displaystyle A_{n}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}[P_{n}]&if $n\geq 0$\\
(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}[I_{-n}^{\prime}]&if $n\leq 0$\end{cases},

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}[I_{n}]&if $n\geq 0$\\
(q-1)^{n}[M^{\prime}]^{n}[P_{-n}^{\prime}]&if $n\leq 0$\end{cases}.

Note that

\displaystyle\tau(\sigma(A_{n}))

\displaystyle=B_{n},

\displaystyle\sigma(B_{n})=\tau(A_{n})

\displaystyle=(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}A_{-n},

\displaystyle\sigma(A_{n})=\tau(B_{n})

\displaystyle=(q-1)^{n}[M^{\prime}]^{n}B_{-n}.

Using this notation, the above lemmas can be reformulated as follows:

Corollary 4.9
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have the equalities:

\displaystyle[[X],A_{n}]_{q}

\displaystyle=A_{n+1}

\displaystyle[A_{n},[X^{\prime}]]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q-1)[M]A_{n-1}

\displaystyle[B_{n},[X]]_{q}

\displaystyle=B_{n+1}

\displaystyle[[X^{\prime}],B_{n}]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q-1)[M^{\prime}]B_{n-1}.

Proposition 4.10
.

For any
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
m\geq n
:

(a)

If
m-n
is odd,

A_{m}A_{n}=q^{m-n+1}A_{n}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+1}-q^{m-n-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n-1}{2}}A_{n+k}A_{m-k}.

(b)

If
m-n
is even,

A_{m}A_{n}=q^{m-n+1}A_{n}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+1}-q^{m-n-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n}{2}}A_{n+k}A_{m-k}+(q^{m-n}-q^{m-n+1})A_{\frac{m+n}{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

Given
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
m\geq n
, let
R_{m,n}
be the statement that the above proposition is true. By
[
Szá06
, Theorem 4.2]
, we know that
R_{m,n}
is true whenever
m\geq n\geq 0
. We’ll prove the proposition by using negative induction on
n
. That is, assuming that
R_{m,n}
is true for all
m\geq n\geq t
, we will show that
R_{m,n}
is true for all
m\geq n\geq t-1
.

Suppose
R_{m,n}
is known to be true for some
m\geq n
such that
m-n
is odd. By induction, we know that

A_{m}A_{n}=q^{m-n+1}A_{n}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+1}-q^{m-n-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n-1}{2}}A_{n+k}A_{m-k}.

(4.4)

We compute the
q^{-2}
-commutator of both sides of equation (
4.4
) with the element
[X^{\prime}]
. The left side becomes

\displaystyle[[X^{\prime}],A_{m}A_{n}]_{q^{-2}}

\displaystyle=[[X^{\prime}],A_{m}]_{q^{-1}}A_{n}+q^{-1}A_{m}[[X^{\prime}],A_{n}]_{q^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(q^{-1}-1)[M](A_{m-1}A_{n}+q^{-1}A_{m}A_{n-1}).

(4.5)

Similarly, the
q^{-2}
-commutator of the right side with
[X^{\prime}]
is

\displaystyle[[X^{\prime}],RHS\eqref{eq:aa}]

\displaystyle=(q^{-1}-1)[M]\Big(q^{m-n+1}A_{n-1}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+1}-q^{m-n-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n-1}{2}}A_{n+k-1}A_{m-k}\Big)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(q^{-1}-1)[M]\Big(q^{m-n}A_{n}A_{m-1}+(q^{m-n}-q^{m-n-2})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n-1}{2}}A_{n+k}A_{m-k-1}\Big).

By induction, we know that

A_{m-1}A_{n}=q^{m-n}A_{n}A_{m-1}+(q^{m-n}-q^{m-n-2})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n-1}{2}}A_{n+k}A_{m-k-1}+(q^{m-n-1}-q^{m-n})A_{\frac{m+n-1}{2}}^{2}.

Equating the
q^{-2}
-commutators (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) and using the above inductive hypothesis, we see that

\displaystyle A_{m}A_{n-1}

\displaystyle=q^{m-n+2}A_{n-1}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+2}-q^{m-n})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n-1}{2}}A_{n+k-1}A_{m-k}-(q^{m-n}-q^{m-n+1})A_{\frac{m+n-1}{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=q^{m-n+2}A_{n-1}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+2}-q^{m-n})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n+1}{2}}A_{n+k-1}A_{m-k}-(q^{m-n+2}-q^{m-n})A_{\frac{m+n-1}{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(q^{m-n}-q^{m-n+1})A_{\frac{m+n-1}{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=q^{m-n+2}A_{n-1}A_{m}+(q^{m-n+2}-q^{m-n})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{m-n+1}{2}}A_{n+k-1}A_{m-k}+(q^{m-n+1}-q^{m-n+2})A_{\frac{m+n-1}{2}}^{2},

which proves
R_{m,n-1}
. In case
m-n
is even, we can use a computation similar to the one above to show that
R_{m,n}
implies
R_{m,n-1}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Applying
\tau\circ\sigma
, we get a dual of the above proposition involving the sequence
(B_{n})
:

Proposition 4.11
.

For any
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
m\leq n
:

(a)

If
n-m
is odd,

B_{m}B_{n}=q^{n-m+1}B_{n}B_{m}+(q^{n-m+1}-q^{n-m-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{n-m-1}{2}}B_{n+k}B_{m-k}.

(b)

If
n-m
is even,

B_{m}B_{n}=q^{n-m+1}B_{n}B_{m}+(q^{n-m+1}-q^{n-m-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{n-m}{2}}B_{n+k}B_{m-k}+(q^{n-m}-q^{n-m+1})B_{\frac{m+n}{2}}^{2}.

4.2
Relations between slope subalgebras

In this section, we prove relations between the sequences
(A_{n})
and
(B_{n})
, which we state as follows:

Theorem 4.12
.

For all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

[B_{m},A_{n}]_{q^{m+n}}=R_{m+n+1}-q^{m+n}(q-1)^{m+n}[M^{\prime}]^{m}[M]^{n}R_{-m-n+1}^{\prime}.

(Here, by convention, we assume that
R_{d}=R_{d}^{\prime}=0
for
d<0
.)

Proof.

When
m,n\geq 1
, the theorem is a consequence of
[
Szá06
, Theorem 4.3]
and Proposition
3.8
. Applying
\sigma

to that, we get that the theorem is also true when
m,n\leq-1
.
When
n\geq 0\geq m
, this is a reformulation of Proposition
4.19
below. Applying
\sigma
to this, we get that the theorem is true when
m\geq 0\geq n
. That covers all the cases and proves the theorem.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

At a very concrete level, one reason we see the shifted affine algebra inside our Hall algebra is apparent in the above formula – on the right hand side we see
R_{m+n+1}
, whereas in the unshifted case we would expect to see
R_{m+n}
.
∎

The identities we need can be proved inductively with the help of some commutator identities involving “small” regular representations – the indecomposable representation
X=R_{\phi}(1)
of dimension
(1,1)
from the previous section. Also, recall that we have defined

R_{1}=\sum_{[a:b]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}}\hbox to107.11pt{\vbox to26.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.55518pt\lower-14.5194pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
