{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.16983pt}{-11.00552pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=:\sum_{[a,b]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}}R(a,b)

where, to ease notation, we have defined
R(a,b)
using the sum above.

The following 3-parameter version of the Jacobi relation holds in any algebra where
a,b,c
are central and invertible, and it will be useful in some of the computations below.

[x,[y,z]_{a}]_{bc^{-1}}+ac^{-1}[z,[x,y]_{b}]_{a^{-1}c}+bc^{-1}[y,[z,x]_{c}]_{ab^{-1}}=0.

(4.7)

Lemma 4.14
.

We have the following commutator identities:

\displaystyle[B_{0},A_{0}]

\displaystyle=R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle[B_{0},A_{1}]_{q}

\displaystyle=R_{2}-q[M].

Proof.

Note that for any
(a,b)
we have a (non-split) short exact sequence

0\to P_{0}\to R(a,b)\to I_{0}\to 0.

Any extension of
I_{0}
by
P_{0}
must have dimension vector
(1,1)
, and hence must be either
I_{0}\oplus P_{0}
or
R(a,b)
for a unique
(a,b)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Furthermore, each
R(a,b)
has a unique proper submodule, and this submodule is isomorphic to
P_{0}
. These observations imply

B_{0}A_{0}=[I_{0}][P_{0}]=[P_{0}\oplus I_{0}]+R_{1}.

A similar argument shows the identity

A_{0}B_{0}=[P_{0}][I_{0}]=[P_{0}\oplus I_{0}]+R_{1}^{\prime},

and combining these proves our first claim.

The second claim follows from the following Hall products:

\displaystyle B_{0}A_{1}

\displaystyle=[I_{0}][P_{1}]=q[I_{0}\oplus P_{1}]+R_{2},

\displaystyle A_{1}B_{0}

\displaystyle=[P_{1}][I_{0}]=[I_{0}\oplus P_{1}]+[M].

To see these, we note that
I_{0}
and
P_{1}
are both
(e,f)
-surjective, and so, by Lemma
3.5
, any extensions between them should also be
(e,f)
-surjective. Therefore, the only possible extension that doesn’t come from the Kronecker quiver is
M
. Then, the above equalities follow from
[
Szá06
, Theorem 4.3]
after observing that
[M]
has exactly one submodule isomorphic to
I_{0}
and none isomorphic to
P_{1}
. ∎

Lemma 4.15
.

We have the following commutator identities:

\displaystyle[[X],R_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=[M]-[M^{\prime}]

\displaystyle[P_{1},P_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=(q-1)\left([M]R_{1}^{\prime}-[M^{\prime}]R_{1}\right)

\displaystyle[[X],R_{2}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=(q-1)([M]-[M^{\prime}])R_{1}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Recall that
X=R_{\phi}(1)
for some
\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
. Thus, by Lemma
3.15
, we have that
[X,[R_{\pi}(n)]]=0
for any
n
, if
\pi\neq\phi
. By definition,

R_{1}^{\prime}:=\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{1}}[R_{\pi}(1)^{\prime}].

So, by Lemma
6.8
, we have

[[X],R_{1}^{\prime}]=[R_{\phi}(1),R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}]=[M]-[M^{\prime}].

The second identity follows from the following Hall algebra computation (and its dual obtained by applying
\sigma
):

[P_{1}][P_{1}^{\prime}]=q^{2}[P_{1}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}]+\sum_{\phi\in\Sigma_{1}}(q^{2}-q)[M\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}]=q^{2}[P_{1}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}]+(q-1)[M]R_{1}^{\prime}.

(4.8)

The second step in (
4.8
) follows from Lemma
4.2
.
To prove the first step, we note that in the representation
P_{1}
, we have
\mathrm{rank}(e\oplus f)=2
, and similarly, in
P_{1}^{\prime}
we have
\mathrm{rank}(e^{\prime}\oplus f^{\prime})=2
. Thus, in an extension of
P_{1}
by
P_{1}^{\prime}
, we must have both
\mathrm{rank}(e\oplus f)\geq 2
and
\mathrm{rank}(e^{\prime}\oplus f^{\prime})\geq 2
. The only representations of dimension
(3,3)
that satisfy both of these conditions are
P_{1}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}
,
M\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\oplus R_{\phi}(1)
for some
\phi\in\Sigma_{1}
. It is clear that
M^{\prime}\oplus R_{\phi}(1)
does not have any submodule isomorphic to
P_{1}^{\prime}
. The
q^{2}
coefficient of
P_{1}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}
occurs because the image of any injective map
P_{1}^{\prime}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by an element in the vector space
(P_{1})_{2}
, which can be chosen arbitrarily by Lemma
3.10
.

To compute the
q^{2}-q
coefficient in (
4.8
), note that any map
g:P_{1}^{\prime}\to M\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}
is determined by the image of a generator
p\in(P_{1}^{\prime})_{2}
, which must map to an element of the form
ax+by_{1}+cy_{2}
(where
x
is a generator of
(R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime})_{2}
). If the maps
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}
in
R_{\phi}(1)
are multiplication by
m,n\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
, respectively, then

\displaystyle e^{\prime}\cdot g(p)

\displaystyle=(bx_{2},am)\in M\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\cdot g(p)

\displaystyle=(-cx_{2},an)\in M\oplus R_{\phi}(1)^{\prime}.

In order to have
\mathrm{rank}(e^{\prime}\oplus f^{\prime})\geq 2
, we must have
bn+cm\neq 0
. This removes a hyperplane
H
from the space
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\ni(b,c)
. Therefore, the set of parameters
(a,b,c)
lives in
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{F}_{1}^{\times}\times(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\setminus H)=:X
, and the number of submodules which are the image of such a
g
is
\lvert X/\mathbb{F}_{q}\rvert=q^{2}-q
. It is easy to check that the cokernel is indeed
P_{1}^{\prime}
for each such submodule.

For the third claimed equality, note that while the commutator
[[X],R_{2}^{\prime}]
can be directly computed using Lemma
6.8
, we provide a different proof here that is independent of the results in Section
6
. We have

\displaystyle[[X],R_{2}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=[X,[P_{0},P_{1}^{\prime}]_{q}+q[M^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=-[P_{1}^{\prime},[X,P_{0}]_{q}]-[P_{0},[P_{1}^{\prime},X]_{q}]

\displaystyle=-[P_{1}^{\prime},P_{1}]-[P_{0},(q-1)[M^{\prime}]P^{\prime}_{0}]

\displaystyle=-[P_{1}^{\prime},P_{1}]-(q-1)[M^{\prime}][P_{0},P^{\prime}_{0}]

\displaystyle=-[P_{1}^{\prime},P_{1}]-(q-1)[M^{\prime}](R_{1}^{\prime}-R_{1})

\displaystyle=(q-1)\left([M]-[M^{\prime}]\right)R_{1}^{\prime}.

where the first equality uses Lemma
4.14
(up to an application of
\tau
), the second equality follows by the Jacobi relation (
4.7
) with
a=b=c=q
and the fact that
[M^{\prime}]
is central, the third equality is a consequence of Corollary
4.9
, the second to last equality again follows from Lemma
4.14
, and the final equality follows from our computation of
[P_{1},P_{1}^{\prime}]
above.

∎

We now find the commutator of
B_{0}
with
A_{n}
for arbitrary
n
.

Lemma 4.16
.

For all
n\geq 2
, we have

[B_{0},A_{n}]_{q^{n}}=R_{n+1}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
, for the base case
n=2
, we start with the equality in Lemma
4.15
:

[B_{0},A_{1}]_{q}=R_{2}-q[M].

Note that any two regular representations with
e=f=0
commute in the Hall algebra as a consequence of Proposition
3.8
and
[
Szá06
,
\mathsection 3
]
. Therefore, since
[X]
commutes with the right hand side of the above identity and the identity we want to prove, we can prove the base case and inductive step simultaneously by taking the commutator of both identities with
[X]
. When
n\geq 1
we then compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=[[X],[B_{0},A_{n}]_{q^{n}}]

(4.9)

\displaystyle=-q^{n+1}[A_{n},[[X],B_{0}]_{q^{-1}}]_{q^{-n-1}}-[B_{0},[A_{n},X]_{q^{-1}}]_{q^{n+1}}

\displaystyle=-q^{n+1}[A_{n},-qB_{1}]_{q^{-n-1}}-[B_{0},-qA_{n+1}]_{q^{n+1}}

where in the first step we used the Jacobi relation (
4.7
) with
a=q^{n}
and
b=c=q^{-1}
, and in the second step we used Corollary
4.9
. Rearranging this equation and applying the identity
[x,y]_{a}=-a[y,x]_{a^{-1}}
, we obtain

[B_{0},A_{n+1}]_{q^{n+1}}=[B_{1},A_{n}]_{q^{n+1}}=R_{n+2},

where the final step follows from
[
Szá06
, Theorem 4.3]
and Proposition
3.8
.
∎

Corollary 4.17
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following identities:

[B_{0},A_{n}]_{q^{n}}:=\begin{cases}R_{n+1}&if $n\geq 2$\\
R_{2}-q[M]&if $n=1$\\
R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime}&if $n=0$\\
-q^{-1}(q-1)^{-1}[M]^{-1}(R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M])&if $n=-1$\\
-q^{n}(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}R_{-n+1}^{\prime}&if $n\leq-2$\\
\end{cases}.

Proof.

The first three cases are proved above, and the last two follow from the first two after applying
\tau
.
∎

By applying
\tau\circ\sigma
to Corollary
4.17
, we can compute the commutators
[B_{n},A_{0}]_{q^{n}}
for all
n
:

Corollary 4.18
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following identities:

[B_{m},A_{0}]_{q^{m}}:=\begin{cases}R_{m+1}&if $m\geq 2$\\
R_{2}-q[M^{\prime}]&if $m=1$\\
R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime}&if $m=0$\\
-q^{-1}(q-1)^{-1}[M]^{-1}(R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M^{\prime}])&if $m=-1$\\
-q^{m}(q-1)^{m}[M^{\prime}]^{m}R_{-m+1}^{\prime}&if $m\leq-2$\\
\end{cases}.

The following proposition partly generalizes the two corollaries above:

Proposition 4.19
.

Suppose
n\geq 0\geq m
. Then,

[B_{m},A_{n}]_{q^{m+n}}:=\begin{cases}R_{n+m+1}&if $n+m\geq 2$\\
R_{2}-q[M^{\prime}]^{m}[M]^{n}&if $n+m=1$\\
R_{1}-[M^{\prime}]^{m}[M]^{n}R_{1}^{\prime}&if $n+m=0$\\
-q^{-1}(q-1)^{-1}[M^{\prime}]^{m}[M]^{n}(R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M]^{-n}[M^{\prime}]^{-m})&if $n+m=-1$\\
-q^{m+n}(q-1)^{m+n}[M^{\prime}]^{m}[M]^{n}R^{\prime}_{-n-m+1}&if $n+m\leq-2$\\
\end{cases}.

Proof.

The proof will by induction on
\min(n,|m|)
. The base case has been covered by Corollaries
4.17
and
4.18
. We note that we only need to prove the first three cases, as the last two cases follow from the first two after applying
\tau
.

Suppose the claim is true for whenever
min(n,|m|)\leq k
. Thus, we know that

[B_{-k},A_{k+1}]_{q}=R_{2}-q[M^{\prime}]^{-k}[M]^{k+1}.

Applying
\sigma
to the above relation, we get

[A_{k},B_{-k-1}]_{q}=(q-1)^{-1}[M^{\prime}]^{-k-1}[M]^{k}(R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M]^{-k}[M^{\prime}]^{k+1}).

(4.10)

We take commutator of both sides of equation (
4.10
) with
[X]
. On the left, we get

\displaystyle[X,LHS\eqref{eq:indstep}]

\displaystyle=[[X],[A_{k},B_{-k-1}]_{q}]

\displaystyle=-[B_{-k-1},[[X],A_{k}]_{q}]-[A_{k},[B_{-k-1},[X]]_{q}]

\displaystyle=-[B_{-k-1},[[X],A_{k+1}]-[A_{k},B_{-k}]

\displaystyle=-[B_{-k-1},A_{k+1}]+\Big(R_{1}-[M^{\prime}]^{-k}[M]^{k}R_{1}^{\prime}\Big),

where we used identity (
4.7
) with
a=b=c=q
in the first step, Corollary
4.9
in the second step and induction in the final step.

Next, using the fact that
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
are central and equality
[[X],R_{2}^{\prime}]=(q-1)([M]-[M^{\prime}])R_{1}^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.14
we get that the commutator of the right side of equation (
4.10
) with
X
is

[X,RHS\eqref{eq:indstep}]=[M^{\prime}]^{-k-1}[M]^{k}([M]-[M^{\prime}])R_{1}^{\prime}.

Equating the commutators of both the sides, we get the required equality

[B_{-k-1},A_{k+1}]=R_{1}-[M^{\prime}]^{-k-1}[M]^{k+1}R_{1}^{\prime}.

(4.11)

To proceed further, we take the commutator of equation (
4.11
) with
[X]
. Using a similar argument as above, we get that the commutator of the left side is equal to

\displaystyle[[X],LHS\eqref{eq:commid3}]

\displaystyle=[[X],[B_{-k-1},A_{k+1}]]

\displaystyle=q^{-1}[B_{-k-1},A_{k+2}]_{q}-q^{-1}[B_{-k},A_{k+1}]_{q}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}[B_{-k-1},A_{k+2}]_{q}-q^{-1}\Big(R_{2}-q[M^{\prime}]^{-k}[M]^{k+1}\Big),

where the last equality follows by induction. To compute the commutator of the right side, we use the fact that
[X]
commutes with
R_{1}
and the identity
[[X],R_{1}^{\prime}]=[M]-[M^{\prime}]
from Lemma
4.14
to get

[[X],RHS\eqref{eq:commid3}]=-[M^{\prime}]^{-k-1}[M]^{k+1}([M]-[M^{\prime}]).

Equating the two commutators of
[X]
with equation (
4.11
), we get the required equality

[B_{-k-1},A_{k+2}]_{q}=R_{2}-q[M^{\prime}]^{-k-1}[M]^{k+2}.

To complete the proof, we need to compute
[B_{-k-1},A_{k+t}]_{q^{t-1}}
for all
t\geq 2
. For this, we proceed by induction on
t
, using the same argument as we did in the proof of Lemma
4.16
, by taking the commutator of the equality at the
t^{th}
step with
[X]
to obtain the
(t+1)^{th}
step.
∎

5
Map from the quantum affine algebra to the Hall algebra

In this section, we construct a map from the deformed quantum affine algebra to a twisted version of our Hall algebra. We fix a square root
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}
of
q
that will be used through this section. We also fix a square root of
\mathbf{v}
which is needed in the definition of the twist below (although it won’t appear in any of our computations).

First, we twist the multiplication on the Hall algebra and define a new
*
-product. Note that the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(Hall(\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})))=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is generated by the classes of the two simple representations. More concretely, for any representation
X
, its class
\overline{X}
in the Grothendieck group is given by
\dim(X)=(\dim(X_{1}),\dim(X_{2}))
.
The group
K_{0}(\mathcal{H})
is obtained by adjoining
\left(-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)
to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to account for
((q-1)[M])^{-1/4}
and
(q-1)[M^{\prime}])^{-1/4}
.

Definition 5.1
.

For elements
X,Y\in\mathcal{H}
that are homogeneous with respect to the
K_{0}(\mathcal{H})
-grading, define

[X]*[Y]:=\mathbf{v}^{-det(\overline{X},\overline{Y})}[X]\cdot[Y],

where ‘
\cdot
’ is the usual product in
\mathcal{H}
.
More generally, the above formula can be extended additively to define a product of any two elements of
\mathcal{H}
. The space
\mathcal{H}
with this product will be referred to as the
twisted Hall algebra

\mathcal{H}^{tw}
. ∎

Remark 5.2
.

The composition of functors
\tau\circ\sigma
defines an anti-automorphism on the twisted Hall algebra.
The equivalences
\tau
and
\sigma
themselves do not induce algebra maps on the twisted algebra; instead, for any representations
X
and
Y
, it is true that

\displaystyle\sigma([X]*[Y])

\displaystyle=q^{-det(\dim(X),\dim(Y))}\sigma([X])*\sigma([Y]),

\displaystyle\tau([X]*[Y])

\displaystyle=q^{-det(\dim(X),\dim(Y))}\tau([Y])*\tau([X]).

∎

Under this new product, the elements
[M]
and
[M]^{\prime}
aren’t central anymore: given a representation
V
of dimension
(m,n)
, they satisfy

\displaystyle[M]*[V]

\displaystyle=q^{2(n-m)}[V]*[M],

\displaystyle[M^{\prime}]*[V]

\displaystyle=q^{2(n-m)}[V]*[M^{\prime}].

Nevertheless, the above equalities imply that that element
[M^{\prime}]*[M]^{-1}
is still central under the twisted product. A related observation is the fact that given two representations
X
and
Y
of slope zero, the twisted product
[X]*[Y]
coincides with the usual product
[X]\cdot[Y]
.

We recall the following definition:

Definition 5.3
.

The specialization
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
of the deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
at
v=\mathbf{v}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra generated by elements
E_{l},F_{l},H_{n},S^{\pm 1},K^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle C^{1/2}

is central

(5.1)

\displaystyle S^{2}

\displaystyle=KC^{1/2}

(5.2)

\displaystyle SE_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}E_{k}

(5.3)

\displaystyle SF_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-1}F_{k}

(5.4)

\displaystyle SH_{n}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=H_{n}

(5.5)

\displaystyle E_{k+1}E_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{2}E_{l}E_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{2}E_{k}E_{l+1}-E_{l+1}E_{k}

(5.6)

\displaystyle F_{k+1}F_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{-2}F_{l}F_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-2}F_{k}F_{l+1}-F_{l+1}F_{k}

(5.7)

\displaystyle[E_{k},F_{l}]

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{k+l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}

(5.8)

\displaystyle[H_{l},E_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{-\lvert l\rvert/2}E_{k+l}

(5.9)

\displaystyle[H_{l},F_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{-[2l]}{l}C^{\lvert l\rvert/2}F_{k+l}

(5.10)

\displaystyle[H_{l},H_{k}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{C^{l}-C^{-l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}

(5.11)

where the elements
\Psi_{k}
and
\Phi_{k}
are defined via the following generating series:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{k-1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K\mathrm{exp}\left((\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{-k+1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{-k}u^{k}\right).

∎

In order to state our main result, we define some elements in
\mathcal{H}
. First of all, we define:

\displaystyle s^{\pm 1}

\displaystyle:=((\mathbf{v}^{2}-1)[M])^{\mp 1/4},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\pm 1}

\displaystyle:=((\mathbf{v}^{2}-1)[M^{\prime}])^{\mp 1/4},

\displaystyle\kappa^{\pm 1}

\displaystyle:=(s*s^{\prime})^{\pm 1},

\displaystyle c^{\pm 1/2}

\displaystyle:=(s*s^{\prime-1})^{\pm 1}.

For all
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, define:

\displaystyle e_{l}

\displaystyle:=s*c^{l/2}*\begin{cases}[P_{l}]&if $l\geq 0$\\
\mathbf{v}^{2l}s^{-4l}*[I_{-l}^{\prime}]&if $l\leq 0$\end{cases},

\displaystyle f_{l}

\displaystyle:=-s^{\prime}*c^{-l/2}*\begin{cases}[I_{l}]&if $l\geq 0$\\
\mathbf{v}^{2l}s^{\prime-4l}*[P_{-l}^{\prime}]&if $l\leq 0$\end{cases}.

Next, we define the sequences for
d\geq-1
:

\displaystyle\psi_{d}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{v}^{-d}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\kappa*R_{d+1},

\displaystyle\varphi_{-d}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{v}^{-d}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\kappa^{2d+1}*R^{\prime}_{d+1}.

Furthermore, let
\psi_{d}=\varphi_{-d}=0
if
d<-1
. Finally, define the sequence
(h_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi_{k-1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=\kappa*\mathrm{exp}\left((\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{k}u^{k}\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\varphi_{-k+1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}*\mathrm{exp}\left(-(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{-k}u^{-k}\right).

Theorem 5.4
.

We have an algebra homomorphism
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{tw}
from the deformed quantum affine algebra to the twisted Hall algebra that is defined on the generators as follows:

\displaystyle C^{\pm 1/2}

\displaystyle\mapsto c^{\pm 1/2}

\displaystyle K^{\pm 1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\kappa^{\pm 1}

\displaystyle S^{\pm 1}

\displaystyle\mapsto s^{\pm 1}

\displaystyle E_{l}\mapsto e_{l},F_{l}

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{l},H_{n}\mapsto h_{n}

for all
l\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

We need to show that the elements
c^{\pm 1/2},\kappa^{\pm 1},e_{l},f_{l},h_{n}
satisfy the relations of the deformed quantum affine algebra. Relations (
5.1
), (
5.2
), (
5.3
), (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) are clear. Relations (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) are proved in Proposition
5.7
. Relation (
5.8
) is proved in Proposition
5.8
. Relations (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) are proved in Proposition
5.10
. Relation (
5.11
) is proved in Proposition
5.12
.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

The image
\mathrm{Im}(\theta_{\mathbf{v}})
is generated by the elements
[I_{0}],[P_{0}],R_{1},((q-1)[M])^{\pm 1/4}
and
((q-1)[M^{\prime}])^{\pm 1/4}
. Furthermore, the intersection
\mathrm{Im}(\theta_{\mathbf{v}})\cap Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is generated by
[I_{0}],[P_{0}],R_{1}
and
[M].

Proof.

We only prove the second claim, since the first one follows consequently. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the subalgebra of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
generated by
[I_{0}],[P_{0}],R_{1}
and
[M].
It is clear from the map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
defined above that the intersection
\mathrm{Im}(\theta_{\mathbf{v}})\cap Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is generated by
[I_{n}],[P_{n}],[I_{n}^{\prime}],[P_{n}^{\prime}],R_{n},R_{n}^{\prime},[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
. By Lemma
4.14
, we have:

[[I_{0}],[P_{0}]]=R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime},

which shows that
R_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
. Next, by Lemma
4.7
, we have that
[I_{n}],[P_{n}],[I_{n}^{\prime}],[P_{n}^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{A}
for all
n\geq 0
. (We use the fact that
R_{1}
and
R_{1}^{\prime}
are sums of all regular
(e,f)
-saturated (resp.
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-saturated representations) of dimension
(1,1)
.)
Next, Lemma
4.15
shows that:

[R_{1},R_{1}^{\prime}]=(q+1)([M]-[M^{\prime}]),

showing that
[M^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{A}
. Finally, the equalities

\displaystyle[[I_{n}],[P_{0}]]_{q}

\displaystyle=R_{n+1}-\delta_{n,1}q[M],

\displaystyle[[I_{n}^{\prime}],[P_{0}^{\prime}]]_{q}

\displaystyle=R_{n+1}^{\prime}-\delta_{n,1}q[M^{\prime}],

from Corollaries
4.17
and
4.18
for
n\geq 1
show that
R_{n},R_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

1.

The equality

[I_{0}][P_{0}]=[I_{0}\oplus P_{0}]+R_{1}

implies that
[I_{0}\oplus P_{0}]
can replace
R_{1}
in the generating set.

2.

While we work with the untwisted algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
in the above corollary, as the specified generators are all homogeneous elements in the Grothendieck group, the claim is also true in the twisted Hall algebra.

∎

In the rest of this section, we prove the technical results that are used in the proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Proposition 5.7
.

The sequences
(e_{n})
and
(f_{n})
satisfy the following relations for all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\displaystyle e_{k+1}*e_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{2}e_{l}*e_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{2}e_{k}*e_{l+1}-e_{l+1}*e_{k},

\displaystyle f_{k+1}*f_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{-2}f_{l}*f_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-2}f_{k}*f_{l+1}-f_{l+1}*f_{k}.

Proof.

Note that:

\displaystyle e_{k}

\displaystyle=s*c^{k/2}*A_{k},

\displaystyle f_{k}

\displaystyle=-s^{\prime}*c^{-k/2}*B_{k},

where the sequences
(A_{k})
and
(B_{k})
were defined in Section
4.1
. The relations that we are required to prove are homogeneous in
k+l
, and thus, to prove these relations, it suffices to show the following:

\displaystyle A_{k+1}*A_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{2}A_{l}*A_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{2}A_{k}*A_{l+1}-A_{l+1}*A_{k},

\displaystyle B_{k+1}*B_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{-2}B_{l}*B_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-2}B_{k}*B_{l+1}-B_{l+1}*B_{k}.

We only prove the former relation, since the latter follows from the former upon applying
\tau\circ\sigma
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
k\geq l
. First, we suppose that
k-l+1
is odd. Then, by Proposition
4.10
, we have the following identity involving the untwisted Hall product:

A_{k+1}A_{l}=q^{k-l+1}A_{l}A_{k+1}+(q^{k-l+1}-q^{k-l-1})\sum_{t=1}^{\frac{k-l}{2}}A_{l+t}A_{k+1-t}.

In terms of the twisted product ‘
*
’, the above relation reads

\mathbf{v}^{k-l+1}A_{k+1}*A_{l}=q^{k-l+1}\mathbf{v}^{l-k+1}A_{l}*A_{k+1}+(q^{k-l+1}-q^{k-l})\sum_{t=1}^{\frac{k-l-1}{2}}\mathbf{v}^{l-k+2t+1}A_{l+t}*A_{k+1-t},

which is equivalent to

A_{k+1}*A_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{2}A_{l}*A_{k+1}=(q-q^{-1})\sum_{t=1}^{\frac{k-l}{2}}\mathbf{v}^{2t}A_{l+t}*A_{k+1-t}.

If
k=l
, the sum on the right is empty, and we get the relation that we want to prove. If
k>l
, again by Proposition
4.10
we have

A_{k}*A_{l+1}=\mathbf{v}^{2}A_{l+1}*A_{k}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{t=1}^{\frac{k-l-2}{2}}\mathbf{v}^{2t}A_{l+1+t}*A_{k-t}.

Then, we can compute that

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{2}A_{k}*A_{l+1}-A_{l+1}*A_{k}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{v}^{4}-1)A_{l+1}*A_{k}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{t=1}^{\frac{k-l-2}{2}}\mathbf{v}^{2t+2}A_{l+1+t}*A_{k-t}

\displaystyle=(q-q^{-1})\sum_{t=1}^{\frac{k-l}{2}}\mathbf{v}^{2t}A_{l+t}*A_{k+1-t}

\displaystyle=A_{k+1}*A_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{2}A_{l}*A_{k+1},

completing the proof. The case when
k-l+1
is even follows similarly.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

The elements
e_{k}
and
f_{l}
for
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy the following relation:

e_{k}*f_{l}-f_{l}*e_{k}=\frac{c^{(k-l)/2}*\psi_{k+l}-c^{(l-k)/2}*\varphi_{k+l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.12
, in the untwisted Hall product we have

[B_{l},A_{k}]_{q^{k+l}}=R_{k+l+1}-q^{k+l}(q-1)^{k+l}[M^{\prime}]^{l}[M]^{k}R_{-k-l+1}^{\prime}.

Using this, we can compute

\displaystyle e_{k}*f_{l}-f_{l}*e_{k}

\displaystyle=-\kappa*c^{(k-l)/2}*(\mathbf{v}^{-1}A_{k}*B_{l}-\mathbf{v}B_{l}*A_{k})

\displaystyle=-\kappa*c^{(k-l)/2}*(\mathbf{v}^{k+l}A_{k}B_{l}-\mathbf{v}^{-k-l}B_{l}A_{k})

\displaystyle=q^{-(k+l)/2}\kappa*c^{(k-l)/2}*(B_{l}A_{k}-q^{k+l}A_{k}B_{l}).

Using the formula for
[B_{l},A_{k}]_{q^{k+l}}
from Theorem
4.12
, we get that the above expression is a sum of two terms. The first term
T_{1}
is equal to

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=q^{-(k+l)/2}\kappa*c^{(k-l)/2}*R_{k+l+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{(k-l)/2}*\psi_{k+l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}.

Similarly, the second term
T_{2}
is equal to

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=q^{-(k+l)/2}\kappa*c^{(k-l)/2}*q^{k+l}(q-1)^{k+l}*[M^{\prime}]^{l}*[M]^{k}*R_{-k-l+1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{(l-k)/2}*\varphi_{k+l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}},

We then obtain

e_{k}*f_{l}-f_{l}*e_{k}=T_{1}-T_{2}

completing the proof.
∎

Recall that we use the notation
[n]
for the quantum number
\dfrac{\mathbf{v}^{n}-\mathbf{v}^{-n}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle[h_{\pm 1},e_{k}]

\displaystyle=[2]c^{-1/2}*e_{k\pm 1},

\displaystyle[h_{\pm 1},f_{k}]

\displaystyle=-[2]c^{1/2}*f_{k\pm 1}.

Proof.

By chasing definitions of
\psi_{1}
and
h_{1}
, we see that
h_{1}=R_{1}.

The element
R_{1}
is a sum of
q+1
regular representations
R_{\pi}(1)
for
\pi\in\Sigma_{1}
, each having dimension
(1,1)
. Thus, by Corollary
4.9
, we have

\displaystyle[h_{1},e_{k}]

\displaystyle=[R_{1},e_{k}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{1}}[[R_{\pi}(1)],e_{k}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-1}c^{-1/2}*(q+1){e_{k+1}}

\displaystyle=[2]c^{-1/2}*e_{k+1}.

The proofs of the other assertions are similar. ∎

Proposition 5.10
.

For all
l\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following identities:

\displaystyle[h_{l},e_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}c^{-\lvert l\rvert/2}*e_{k+l},

\displaystyle[h_{l},f_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{-[2l]}{l}c^{\lvert l\rvert/2}*f_{k+l}.

Proof.

By
[
Szá06
, Theorem 4.12]
, Proposition
3.8
and Lemma
3.13
,
we know that for
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 0
, we have the following identity in the untwisted Hall algebra:

R_{n}[P_{m}]=q^{n}[P_{m}]R_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(q^{n+i}-q^{n+i-2})[P_{m+i}]R_{n-i}.

In terms of the twisted product, this equality is equivalent to

R_{n}*[P_{m}]=[P_{m}]*R_{n}+(1-q^{-2})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{v}^{i}q^{i}[P_{m+i}]*R_{n-i}.

Recalling the definitions of
\psi_{n}
and
e_{m}
, for all
m,n\geq 0
, the previous equality can be restated as

\psi_{n-1}*e_{m}=qe_{m}*\psi_{n-1}+(1-q^{-2})\sum_{i=1}^{n}q^{2i+1}c^{-i/2}*e_{m+i}*\psi_{n-i-1}.

(5.12)

Define

\displaystyle P(t)

\displaystyle:=\kappa^{-1}*\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi_{n-1}t^{n},

(5.13)

\displaystyle E(s)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m\geq 0}e_{m}s^{m},

(5.14)

\displaystyle F(s,t)

\displaystyle:=1+(1-q^{-2})\sum_{i\geq 1}q^{i}c^{-i/2}t^{i}s^{-i}=\frac{1-q^{-1}c^{-1/2}ts^{-1}}{1-qc^{-1/2}ts^{-1}}.

(5.15)

Note that
F(s,t)
commutes with both
P(t)
and
E(s)
. Then we can rewrite identity (
5.12
)
to get the following equality for all
m,n\geq 0
:

[t^{m}s^{n}]P(t)*E(s)=[t^{m}s^{n}]E(s)*P(t)*F(s,t),

where the notation
[t^{m}s^{n}]
refers to the coefficient of
t^{m}s^{n}
in the following power series.
By induction, it follows that for all
k\geq 1
, we have

[t^{m}s^{n}]P(t)^{k}*E(s)=[t^{m}s^{n}]E(s)*P(t)^{k}*F(s,t)^{k}.

This implies that

\displaystyle[t^{m}s^{n}]\log(P(t))*E(s)

\displaystyle=[t^{m}s^{n}]\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}P(t)^{k}*E(s)

\displaystyle=[t^{m}s^{n}]\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}E(s)*P(t)^{k}*F(s,t)^{k}

\displaystyle=[t^{m}s^{n}]E(s)*\log(P(t)*F(s,t))

\displaystyle=[t^{m}s^{n}]E(s)*\big(\log(P(t))+\log(F(s,t))\big)

\displaystyle=[t^{m}s^{n}]E(s)*\log(P(t))+[t^{m}s^{n}]E(s)*\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{q^{k}-q^{-k}}{k}c^{-k/2}t^{k}s^{-k}.

However, by definition, we know that
\log(P(t))=(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k\geq 1}h_{l}t^{l}
. Therefore, for all
l\geq 1
and
k\geq 0,
comparing the coefficients of
t^{l}s^{k}
on both sides of the above computation gives us that

[h_{l},e_{k}]=\frac{q^{l}-q^{-l}}{l(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})}c^{-l/2}*e_{k+l}=\frac{[2l]}{l}c^{-l/2}*e_{k+l}.

Next, if
l\neq 1
, we take the commutator of both sides of the above equality by
h_{-1}
. As
h_{-1}
commutes with
h_{l}
whenever
l>1
(by Proposition
5.11
), we get by Lemma
5.9
that

[h_{l},e_{k-1}]=\frac{[2l]}{l}c^{-l/2}*e_{k+l-1}.

Repeating this, we can shift
k
further and conclude that the required identity is true for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
assuming
l\geq 1
. (When
l=1
, the identity was proved in Lemma
5.9
.)

Next, in order to prove the required identity for the
f_{k}
’s, we apply
\tau\circ\sigma
to the identity for the
e_{k}
’s. From their definitions, we note that
\tau\circ\sigma
preserves the
\psi_{d}
’s and the
\varphi_{d}
’s, which implies that
\tau\circ\sigma(h_{l})=h_{l}
for all
l
. Further, we note that
\tau\circ\sigma(e_{k})=\mathbf{v}f_{k}
whereas
\tau\circ\sigma(c)=c^{-1}
.
Applying this to the above equality, we get

[h_{l},f_{k}]=-\frac{[2l]}{l}c^{l/2}*f_{k+l}.

Finally, we need to deal with the case when
l\leq-1
. Note that

\sigma(\varphi_{d})=\kappa^{-2d}*\psi_{-d},

and it follows that for all
l\geq 1
:

\sigma(h_{l})=-\kappa^{-2l}*h_{-l}.

Next, we see that

\displaystyle\sigma(e_{k})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{v}^{-2k+1}s^{\prime-4k}*c^{-k}*f_{-k}

\displaystyle\sigma(f_{k})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{v}^{2k-1}s^{-4k}*c^{k}*e_{-k}.

Then, applying
\sigma
to the identities for
l\geq 1
exactly gives us the required identities for
l\leq-1
, completing the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

For all
n\geq 1
, we have the following identities:

h_{n}*h_{-1}-h_{n}*h_{-1}=\delta_{n,1}[2]\frac{c-c^{-1}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}},

h_{1}*h_{-n}-h_{-n}*h_{1}=\delta_{n,1}[2]\frac{c-c^{-1}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}.

Proof.

We will only prove the first equality, since the second follows from it by applying
\tau
. Recall that

1+\psi(u):=\sum_{k\geq 0}\kappa^{-1}\psi_{k-1}u^{k}=\mathrm{exp}\left((\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{k}u^{k}\right).

Using Proposition
5.8
and Lemma
5.9
, we can compute the commutator for
k\geq 1

\displaystyle[h_{-1},\psi_{k-1}]

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})c^{(k-3)/2}[h_{-1},[e_{1},f_{k-2}]]

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})c^{(k-3)/2}\left([[h_{-1},e_{1}],f_{k-2}]+[e_{1},[h_{-1},f_{k-2}]]\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})c^{(k-3)/2}[2]\left(c^{-1/2}[e_{0},f_{k-2}]-c^{1/2}[e_{1},f_{k-3}]\right)

\displaystyle=[2](c^{-1}-c)\psi_{k-2}.

This implies that

[h_{-1},\psi(u)]=[2](c^{-1}-c)u(1+\psi(u)).

Note that the right hand side of the above equality commutes with
\psi(u)
.

In any associative algebra with elements
A
and
B
, if
[A,B]
commutes with
B
, then it is clear that

[A,f(B)]=[A,B]\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial B}f(B),

where
f
is a polynomial (or power series) function. Using this fact with
A=h_{-1}
and
B=\psi(u)
, we see that

\displaystyle\left[h_{-1},(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{k}u^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[h_{-1},\log(1+\psi(u))\right]

\displaystyle=\left[h_{-1},\psi(u)\right]\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi(u)}(\log(1+\psi(u)))

\displaystyle=[2](c^{-1}-c)u(1+\psi(u))\frac{1}{1+\psi(u)}

\displaystyle=[2](c^{-1}-c)u.

In particular, the coefficient of
u^{n}
in the RHS is zero when
n\geq 2
, showing that
[h_{-1},h_{n}]=0
for
n\geq 2
. Furthermore, comparing the coefficients of
u
on both sides gives precisely the claimed equality when
n=1
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

For all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, we have the following identity:

h_{l}*h_{k}-h_{k}*h_{l}=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{c^{l}-c^{-l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}.

Proof.

If
k,l>0
or
k,l<0
, it is clear that
h_{k}
and
h_{l}
commute, since the regular part of the Hall algebra of the Kronecker quiver is commutative (see
[
Szá06
, Sec. 3]
). Hence, without loss of generality, we suppose that
l>0
and
k<0
. The proof will be by induction on
\min\{|k|,l\}
. The base case was resolved by the previous proposition.

In general, without loss of generality, we suppose
|k|\geq l>1
. Note that, by definition, we have that:

h_{l}=\frac{1}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\kappa^{-1}\psi_{l-1}+P,

where
P
is some polynomial in the
\psi_{n}
’s for
n<l-1
. Furthermore, each
\psi_{n}
is a polynomial in the
h_{m}
’s with
1\leq m\leq n
. Therefore, by induction, we can assume that
[P,h_{k}]=0
.
Then, we can compute:

\displaystyle[h_{l},h_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})}\kappa^{-1}[\psi_{l-1},h_{k}]

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}c^{(l-1)/2}[[e_{0},f_{l-1}],h_{k}]

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}c^{(l-1)/2}\Big(-\frac{[2k]}{k}c^{k/2}[e_{k},f_{l-1}]+\frac{[2k]}{k}c^{-k/2}[e_{0},f_{k+l-1}]\Big)\text{ (By Proposition}~\ref{prop:rel78})

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}c^{(l-1)/2}\frac{[2k]}{k}\frac{-c^{(2k-l+1)/2}\psi_{k+l-1}+c^{(l-1)/2}\varphi_{k+l-1}+c^{-(2k+l-1)/2}\psi_{k+l-1}-c^{(l-1)/2}\varphi_{k+l-1}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\text{(By Proposition}~\ref{prop:rel6})

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}\frac{[2k]}{k}\frac{(-c^{k}+c^{-k})\psi_{k+l-1}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{c^{l}-c^{-l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}},

where the last equality follows since
\psi_{n}=0
for
n<-1
and
\psi_{-1}=\kappa
.
∎

6
Regular representations and Heisenberg subalgebras

In the Kronecker quiver the regular representations have the following property: the subalgebra of the Hall algebra
Hall(Q_{K})
generated by elements of the form
[R_{\phi}(n)]
, for fixed
\phi\in\Sigma
and varying
n\geq 1
, is canonically isomorphic to the classical Hall algebra studied by Hall and Steinitz. This classical Hall algebra is in turn isomorphic to the ring
Sym
of symmetric functions in infinitely many variables.

In this section (see Corollary
6.9
), we prove that a similar phenomenon occurs for the Rudakov quiver: for fixed
\phi\in\Sigma
and varying
n\geq 1
, the elements
[R_{\phi}(n)]
and
[R_{{\phi}}(n)^{\prime}]
canonically generate a (quantum) Heisenberg algebra inside
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
. Furthermore, we observe that up to isomorphism, this algebra only depends on the positive integer
d
such that
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}\subset\Sigma
, and not on
\phi
itself. Finally, as a corollary of the results of this section, we also give an independent Hall theoretic proof of Proposition
5.12
(see Proposition
6.13
).

By Corollary
3.15
, we know that regular representations
R_{\phi}(m)
and
R_{\pi}(n)^{\prime}
commute in the Hall algebra, as long as
\phi\neq\pi
. Henceforth, we fix an element
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
. Define the sequences

J_{m}:=R_{\phi}(m),\qquad J_{n}^{\prime}:=R_{{\phi}}(n)^{\prime}

for all
m,n\geq 1
. As a convention, we take
J_{0}
and
J_{0}^{\prime}
to be the zero representation.

Definition 6.1
.

We define
Hall_{\phi}
to be the subalgebra of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
generated by the elements
[J_{m}]
and
[J_{n}^{\prime}]
for
m,n\geq 1
.
∎

To determine the structure of the algebra
Hall_{\phi}
, we start with the following lemma which describes all extensions of the representation
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Any extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
is of the form
J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{k}
for some
0\leq k\leq\min(m,n)
.

Proof.

We break down the proof into six steps as follows:

Step 1:
We first show that any pre-projective module
P_{t}^{\prime}
or any pre-injective module
I_{t}
can not occur as a direct summand of any extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
. Suppose
I_{t}
is a summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
, that is, there is an exact sequence

0\to J_{n}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Theta}I_{t}\oplus V\to J_{m}\to 0,

for some representation
V
. As
J_{n}^{\prime}
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-injective, so is
\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})
. But
e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
are both zero on
I_{t}\oplus\{0\}\subseteq I_{t}\oplus V
, and so we conclude that
\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cap(I_{t}\oplus\{0\})=0
. This implies that we have an injective homomorphism

I_{t}\cong I_{t}\oplus\{0\}\to(I_{t}\oplus V)/\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cong J_{m}.

However, this isn’t possible since
\Hom(I_{t},J_{m})=0
by
[
Szá06
, Lemma 1.1(a)]
. This shows that
I_{t}
can never occur as a summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
. Applying
\tau
, we conclude that pre-projective modules
P_{t}^{\prime}
can’t occur as such a direct summand either.

Step 2:
We claim that any pre-projective module
P_{t}
or any pre-injective module
I_{t}^{\prime}
can not occur as a direct summand of any extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
. This is a consequence of the previous step using slope arguments: as
J_{m}
and
J_{n}^{\prime}
are both representations with zero slope, so are all of their extensions. Thus, if any such extension has either
P_{t}
or
I_{t}^{\prime}
(both of which are modules with positive slope) as a direct summand, it must also have a direct summand with negative slope, which is not possible because of the previous step. We conclude that
P_{t}
and
I_{t}^{\prime}
can’t occur as direct summands of the extensions either.

Step 3:
We show that the projective module
M^{\prime}
can not occur as a direct summand of any extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
. Suppose
M^{\prime}
is a summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
, that is, there is an exact sequence

0\to J_{n}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Theta}M^{\prime}\oplus V\to J_{m}\to 0,

for some representation
V
. As in Step 1, we try to compute
\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cap(M^{\prime}\oplus\{0\})
in
M^{\prime}\oplus V
. As
e=f=0
in
J_{n}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cap(M^{\prime}\oplus\{0\})\subseteq(\langle y_{2}\rangle\oplus\{0\})
(using notation from equation (
2.18
) for a basis of
M^{\prime}
). But then, as
J_{n}^{\prime}
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-injective, we conclude that
\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cap(M^{\prime}\oplus\{0\})=0
. Hence, we must have an injective homomorphism

M^{\prime}\cong M^{\prime}\oplus\{0\}\to(M^{\prime}\oplus V)/\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cong J_{m}.

This isn’t possible since
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}=0
in
J_{m}
, whereas both of these maps are non-zero in
M^{\prime}
. Hence, we conclude that
M^{\prime}
can not occur as a summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
.

Step 4:
We show that any regular representation other than
J_{k}
and
J_{k}^{\prime}
can’t occur as a direct summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
. Let
R_{\pi}(k)
be a regular representation, where
\pi\neq\phi
. Suppose we have an exact sequence:

0\to J_{n}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Theta}R_{\pi}(k)\oplus V\to J_{m}\to 0,

for some representation
V
. By exactly the same argument as the one used in Step 1, we conclude that
\Theta(J_{n}^{\prime})\cap(R_{\pi}(k)\oplus\{0\})
is trivial. This implies that
R_{\pi}(k)
is a submodule of
J_{m}
. However, by
[
Szá06
, Lemma 1.1(b)]
, we have that
\Hom(R_{\pi}(k),J_{m})=0
. This gives us the required contradiction, showing that
R_{\pi}(k)
can not occur as a direct summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
if
\pi\neq\phi
. Applying
\tau
, we conclude that
R_{\pi}(k)^{\prime}
can not occur as a direct summand either if
\pi\neq{\phi}
.

Step 5:
We now show that
J_{a}\oplus J_{b}
and
J_{a}^{\prime}\oplus J_{b}^{\prime}
can not occur as a direct summand of an extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
for some
a,b\geq 1
. (That is, any extension contains at most one of the
J_{a}
’s and at most one of the
J_{b}^{\prime}
as a direct summand.) Suppose we have a short exact sequence

0\to J_{n}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Theta}J_{a}\oplus J_{b}\oplus V\to J_{m}\to 0,

for some representation
V
. By the same argument as used in Steps 1 and 4, we can show that this sequence implies that
J_{a}\oplus J_{b}
is a submodule of
J_{m}
. This implies that
J_{1}\oplus J_{1}
is a submodule of
J_{m}
, which is not possible since
J_{m}
has a unique submodule isomorphic to
J_{1}
(
[
Szá06
, Lemma 1.1(g)]
)
This is thus a contradiction, and so
J_{a}\oplus J_{b}
can not occur as a direct summand of such an extension. Applying
\sigma
, we conclude that
J_{a}^{\prime}\oplus J_{b}^{\prime}
can’t occur as a direct summand either.

Step 6:
The five steps above imply that any extension of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
is of the form
J_{m-a}\oplus J_{n-b}^{\prime}\oplus M^{\prime c}
for some
a,b,c
. To complete the proof of the lemma, we need to show that
da=db=c
, where
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
. Comparing dimensions, we conclude that:
dm+dn=d(m-a)+d(n-b)+2c
which is equivalent to
da+db=2c
. For what follows, we assume that
\phi\neq 0
, without loss of generality. (If
\phi=0
, the same argument as below works after swapping the roles of
e
and
e^{\prime}
with those of
f
and
f^{\prime}
respectively.)

Next, we observe that
\dim(\text{Im}(e))=dm
in
J_{m}
, whereas
\dim(\text{Im}(e))=0
in
J_{n}^{\prime}
. Thus, in any extension
V
of
J_{m}
by
J_{n}^{\prime}
, we must have
\dim(\text{Im}(e))\geq dm
. On the other hand, in
J_{m-a}\oplus J_{n-b}^{\prime}\oplus M^{\prime c}
, we have
\dim(\text{Im}(e))=d(m-a)+0+c=dm-da+c.
Thus, we get that
dm-da+c\geq dm
which implies that
c\geq da
. Similarly, comparing
\dim(\text{Im}(e^{\prime}))
, we get the inequality
c\geq db
. These inequalities along with the equality
da+db=2c
imply that
da=db=c
, completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

As a consequence of the above lemma, we can express the Hall product as

[J_{m}][J_{n}^{\prime}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\min\{m,n\}}c_{k}[J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}],

for some coefficients
c_{k}
. The following lemma determines these coefficients, where for
a\geq 0
we define

\rho_{a}:=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a\neq 0\\
(1-q^{-d})^{-1}&\text{if }a=0\end{cases}\\
.

Lemma 6.3
.

In the above Hall product, we have
c_{0}=q^{d^{2}mn}
. For
k\geq 1
, we have the following equality:

c_{k}={q^{d^{2}mn-d^{2}k^{2}-dk}}\big\lvert GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\big\rvert\rho_{m-k}\rho_{n-k}(1-q^{-d}).

Proof.

The coefficient
c_{k}
is equal to the number of subrepresentations
V\subseteq J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}
such that
V\cong J_{n}^{\prime}
and
(J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk})/V\cong J_{m}
. When
k=0
, any such subrepresentation
V
is the image of a map of the form:

J_{n}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f\oplus Id}J_{m}\oplus J_{n}^{\prime},

for any
f\in\Hom(J_{n}^{\prime},J_{m})
. The space
\Hom(J_{n}^{\prime},J_{m})
has dimension
d^{2}mn
by Lemma
3.10
, which gives that
c_{0}=q^{d^{2}mn}
.

Now, we assume that
k\geq 1
. Furthermore, we assume that
\phi\neq 0
, without loss of generality. (If
\phi=0
, the same argument as below works after swapping the roles of
e
and
e^{\prime}
with those of
f
and
f^{\prime}
respectively.) We first fix some coordinates. On
M^{dk}
, we fix a basis
\{x_{i},y_{i},z_{i},w_{i}\}
for
1\leq i\leq dk
, where each such quadruple spans a copy of
M
and we have

\hbox to104.57pt{\vbox to71.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.28523pt\lower-34.90532pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.95837pt}{-31.39146pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Next, on
J_{n-k}^{\prime}
, we fix bases
\{r_{1},r_{2},\cdots,r_{d(n-k)}\}
at vertex
1
and
\{s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{d(n-k)}\}
at vertex
2
, so that the matrix for the map
e^{\prime}
with respect to this basis is the identity, whereas that of
f^{\prime}
is a block matrix with
d\times d
blocks and is equal to the
d(n-k)\times d(n-k)
matrix

M_{{\phi}}(n-k):=\begin{bmatrix}M_{{\phi}}&I_{d\times d}&&&\\
&M_{{\phi}}&I_{d\times d}&&\\
&&.&.&\\
&&&.&.\\
&&&&M_{{\phi}}\end{bmatrix},

where
I_{d\times d}
is the identity matrix and all the empty blocks are zero. Similarly for
J_{m-k}
, fix bases
\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{d(m-k)}\}
at vertex
1
and
\{u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{d(m-k)}\}
at vertex
2
, so that the matrix for the map
e
with respect to this basis is the identity, whereas that of
f
is a block matrix with
d\times d
blocks and is equal to
M_{\phi}(m-k)
.

With the above convention, a basis for
J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}
at the vertex
2
is given by the set
\{y_{i},z_{i},s_{j},u_{l}\}
for
1\leq i\leq dk
,and
1\leq j\leq d(n-k)
, and
1\leq l\leq d(m-k)
. On this basis,
e^{\prime}
acts by zero on the
y_{i}
’s and the
u_{i}
’s.
On the other hand, if
V\subseteq J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}
such that
V\cong J_{n}^{\prime}
, then the dimension of
\text{Im}(e^{\prime})
in
V
is exactly
dn
. This implies that for
V
, we can write a basis at the vertex
2
of the form

\{z_{i}+a_{i}+b_{i},s_{j}+\widetilde{a_{j}}+\widetilde{b_{j}},\}

for
1\leq i\leq dk
and
1\leq j\leq d(n-k)
, where
a_{i},\widetilde{a_{j}}
lie in the span of the
y_{i}
’s and
b_{i},\widetilde{b_{j}}
lie in the span of the
u_{j}
’s. The choice of these
a_{i}
’s,
\widetilde{a_{j}}
’s,
b_{i}
’s and
\widetilde{b_{j}}
’s uniquely determines a subspace at vertex
2
and we’d like to find the number of ways of choosing these vectors so as to ensure that the spanned subrepresentation is isomorphic to
J_{n}^{\prime}
and the quotient is isomorphic to
J_{m}
.

With respect to this basis at vertex
2
and the set
\{w_{i},r_{j}\}
at the vertex
1
, we can write the matrices for
e^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}
in
V
. The matrix for
e^{\prime}
is the identity, whereas that for
f^{\prime}
is a
dn\times dn
matrix of the form

\begin{bmatrix}M_{1}&M_{2}\\
0&M_{\phi}(n-k)\end{bmatrix},

where
M_{1}
is a
dk\times dk
matrix determined by the
a_{i}
’s and
M_{2}
is a
dk\times d(n-k)
matrix determined by the
\widetilde{a_{j}}
’s. Furthermore, given any matrices
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
of the above dimension, we can choose the
a_{i}
’s and the
\widetilde{a_{j}}
’s uniquely so that
f^{\prime}
has the above form.

As
V\cong J_{n}^{\prime}
, we must have that the above matrix is similar to
M_{\phi}(n)
. For this to be the case, we must have that
M_{1}
is similar to
M_{\phi}(k)
. The number of such matrices
M_{1}
is given by
|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|/|Z(M_{\phi}(k))|
, where
Z(M_{\phi}(k))
is the centralizer of
M_{\phi}(k)
in
GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. By
[
Mac15
, Chapter II, (1.6)]
, we have that
|Z(M_{\phi}(k))|=q^{dk}(1-q^{-d})
. Hence, the number of such matrices
M_{1}
, which is equal to the number of valid choices for the vector
a_{i}
’s, is equal to

\frac{|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}{q^{dk}(1-q^{-d})}.

Furthermore, if
M_{1}
is similar to
M_{\phi}(k)
, in order to have the whole matrix above to be similar to
M_{\phi}(n)
, by Lemma
6.4
, we have that there are
q^{d^{2}k(n-k)}\rho_{n-k}(1-q^{-d})
choices for the matrix
M_{2}
, and thus, this is the number of valid choices for the vectors
\widetilde{a_{j}}
’s.

Next, we need to figure out the number of valid choices for the
b_{i}
’s and
\widetilde{b_{j}}
’s so that
(J_{m-k}\oplus J^{\prime}_{n-k}\oplus M^{dk})/V\cong J_{m}
. For this quotient, we can choose as basis the set
\{x_{i},t_{j}\}
at vertex
1
and
\{y_{i},u_{j}\}
at vertex
2
, where
1\leq i\leq dk
and
1\leq j\leq d(m-k)
. With respect to these basis, the matrix for
e
is the identity matrix, whereas that for
f
is a
dm\times dm
matrix of the form

\begin{bmatrix}M_{1}&0\\
M_{3}&M_{{\phi}}(m-k)\end{bmatrix},

where the matrix
M_{3}
is a
d(m-k)\times dk
matrix that is determined by the
b_{i}
’s. The matrix
M_{1}
was already chosen to be similar to
M_{{\phi}}(k)
, and so, by Lemma
6.4
again, we get that we have
q^{d^{2}k(m-k)}\rho_{m-k}(1-q^{-d})
choices for the matrix
M_{3}
, and thus for the vectors
b_{i}
, so that the above
dn\times dn
matrix is similar to
M_{\phi}(m)
.

Finally, the
\widetilde{b_{j}}
’s are
d(n-k)
arbitrary vectors in a
d(m-k)
dimensional vector space, and hence, can be chosen in
q^{d^{2}(m-k)(n-k)}
ways. So, multiplying all the choices above, we get the Hall number

\displaystyle c_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}{q^{dk}(1-q^{-d})}\cdot q^{d^{2}k(n-k)}\rho_{k,n-k}(1-q^{-d})\cdot q^{d^{2}k(m-k)}\rho_{k,m-k}(1-q^{-d})\cdot q^{d^{2}(m-k)(n-k)}

\displaystyle={q^{d^{2}mn-d^{2}k^{2}-dk}}|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|\rho_{m-k}\rho_{n-k}(1-q^{-d}).

∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Fix non-negative integers
a,b
and a monic irreducible polynomial
\pi
of degree
d
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Consider the block matrix:

A=\begin{bmatrix}M_{\pi}(a)&\overline{M}\\
0&M_{\pi}(b)\end{bmatrix},

where
\overline{M}
is some
da\times db
matrix. Then, the number of matrices
\overline{M}
such that the matrix
A
above is similar to
M_{\pi}(a+b)
is exactly
q^{d^{2}ab}\rho_{ab}(1-q^{-d})
.

Proof.

When either
a
or
b
is zero, the claim is clear. Henceforth, we assume that
a
and
b
are both positive. We can view
\overline{M}=(M_{i,j})
as a block matrix with
d\times d
blocks
M_{i,j}
with
1\leq i\leq a
and
1\leq j\leq b
. For the purpose of this proof, let
X=M_{\pi}(1)
. Then, the matrix
A
can be written in a
d\times d
block form, such that all the diagonal blocks are equal to
X
. Furthermore, all but (possibly) one of the superdiagonal blocks are equal to the identity
I
, whereas the
a^{th}
superdiagonal block is equal to
M_{a,1}
.

Suppose
\pi(t)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}t^{i}
for some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We can view
\pi
as a polynomial function over the space of square matrices (also denoted by
\pi
). Furthermore, the derivative of
\pi
on the space of matrices at the point
X
is a linear map
\pi_{X}^{\prime}
given by

\pi_{X}^{\prime}(Y)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}X^{j}YX^{i-j-1}.

Then, the evaluation of
\pi
on
A
is given by

\pi(A)=\begin{bmatrix}0&\pi_{X}^{\prime}(I)&*&*&*&*&\cdots&*\\
0&0&\pi_{X}^{\prime}(I)&*&*&*&\cdots&*\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0&\pi_{X}^{\prime}(M_{a,1})&*&\cdots&*\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&\pi_{X}^{\prime}(I)&\cdots&*\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Here, the diagonal blocks are zero because
\pi(X)=0
by Cayley-Hamilton theorem. Note that all but (possibly) one of the superdiagonal blocks of
\pi(A)
are equal to
\pi_{X}^{\prime}(I)
, which is equal to the derivative of
\pi(t)
(as a usual polynomial) applied to
X
. Hence,
\pi_{X}^{\prime}(I)
is invertible since the minimal polynomial of
X
is
\pi(t)
, which is separable.

In order to show that
A
is similar to
M_{\pi}(a+b)
, we need to show that the minimal polynomial of
A
is
\pi(t)^{a+b}
. That is equivalent to showing that
\pi(A)
is nilpotent of order
a+b
, but of no smaller order. By the above computation, and using the fact that
\pi_{X}^{\prime}(I)
is invertible, we get that
\pi(A)
is nilpotent of order
a+b
if and only if
\pi_{X}^{\prime}(M_{a,1})\neq 0
. So, the claim of the lemma is equivalent to showing that the kernel of the linear map
\pi_{X}^{\prime}
on the space of
d\times d
matrices has dimension exactly
d^{2}-d
.

That the final assertion is true can be seen by going to a field extension of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. (We can do this since the rank of a linear map is preserved under field extension.) So, without loss of generality, we can suppose that
\pi(t)
splits into linear factors and that
X
is diagonal. Then, it is an easy computation to show that
\pi_{X}^{\prime}(Y)
is zero precisely when all the diagonal entries of
Y
are zero.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

We have the following identity:

[J_{m}][J_{n}^{\prime}]=q^{d^{2}mn}[J_{m}\oplus J_{n}^{\prime}]+\sum_{k=1}^{\min\{m,n\}}q^{d^{2}(m-k)(n-k)-dk}(q-1)^{dk}(1-q^{-d})[M]^{dk}\rho_{m-k}\rho_{n-k}[J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}].

(6.1)

Proof.

Note that any automorphism of the representation
M
is determined by the image of
x_{1}
, and all the available options are given by
ax_{1}+bx_{2}
where
a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
and
b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Thus, we see that
|\mathrm{Aut}(M)|=q(q-1)
. By a similar argument, we have that
|\mathrm{Aut}(M^{n})|=\lvert M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\rvert\lvert GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\rvert=q^{n^{2}}|GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|
for all
n\geq 1
. Then, by a repeated application of Lemma
4.2
and Corollary
2.28
, we have the following equality:

[M]^{dk}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{dk-1}|\Hom(M,M^{i})|^{-1}\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}(M^{dk})|}{|\mathrm{Aut}(M)|^{dk}}[M^{dk}]=|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|(q-1)^{-dk}[M^{dk}].

Then, again by Lemma
4.2
and Corollary
2.28
, we get

\displaystyle[M]^{dk}[J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|(q-1)^{-dk}|\Hom(J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime},M^{dk})|[J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}]

\displaystyle=|GL_{dk}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|(q-1)^{-dk}q^{d^{2}k(m+n-2k)}[J_{m-k}\oplus J_{n-k}^{\prime}\oplus M^{dk}].

Combining this with Lemma
6.3
proves the claim.
∎

We introduce some shorthand notation that will be useful in upcoming computations and will make the above equality (
6.1
) look cleaner:

\mathcal{J}_{m}:=[J_{m}]\rho_{m},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}:=[J_{n}^{\prime}]\rho_{n},

A_{m,n}:=q^{d^{2}mn}\rho_{m}\rho_{n}[J_{m}\oplus J^{\prime}_{n}],\qquad\mathcal{X}:=\left(\frac{q-1}{q}[M]\right)^{d},\qquad\mathcal{Y}:=\left(\frac{q-1}{q}[M^{\prime}]\right)^{d}

where, by convention,
A_{r,s}=0
if
r<0
or
s<0
.

Remark 6.6
.

Note that when
m,n>0
, we have
\mathcal{J}_{m}=[J_{m}],\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}=[J_{n}^{\prime}]
and
A_{m,n}=q^{d^{2}mn}[J_{m}\oplus J_{n}^{\prime}]
.
∎

In terms of this notation, equation (
6.1
) becomes

\mathcal{J}_{m}\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}=(1-q^{-d})\sum_{k=0}^{\min\{m,n\}}\rho_{k}\mathcal{X}^{k}A_{m-k,n-k}.

(6.2)

So, we note that the product
\mathcal{J}_{m}\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}
can be expressed as a sum involving the elements
A_{m-k,n-k}
for
k\geq 0
. The following lemma does the inverse job:

Lemma 6.7
.

For all
m,n\geq 0
, we have the following:

\displaystyle A_{m,n}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{d})\sum_{k\geq 0}\rho_{k}q^{-dk}\mathcal{X}^{k}\mathcal{J}_{m-k}\mathcal{J}_{n-k}^{\prime},

\displaystyle A_{m,n}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{d})\sum_{k\geq 0}\rho_{k}q^{-dk}\mathcal{Y}^{k}\mathcal{J}_{n-k}^{\prime}\mathcal{J}_{m-k}.

The proof is by an induction on
m+n
using Equation (
6.2
). Combining the results of the two lemmas above, we conclude with the main result of this section, which again follows by a straightforward induction:

Theorem 6.8
.

For all
m,n\geq 0
, we have:

\mathcal{J}_{m}\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}\mathcal{J}_{m}+\frac{(q^{d}-1)(\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{Y})}{q^{d}\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{Y}}\sum_{k\geq 1}q^{-dk}((q^{d}\mathcal{X})^{k}-\mathcal{Y}^{k})\mathcal{J}_{n-k}^{\prime}\mathcal{J}_{m-k}.

Next, we state a ‘logarithmic’ version of the above theorem, which will allow us to provide a new proof of Proposition
5.12
. To that end, define the generating series

E_{\phi}(t):=1+\sum_{m\geq 1}q^{md}(1-q^{-d})[J_{m}]t^{md}=\sum_{m\geq 0}q^{md}(1-q^{-d})\mathcal{J}_{m}t^{md}=\sum_{m\geq 0}E_{m,\phi}t^{md},

(6.3)

where we define
E_{m,\phi}:=q^{md}(1-q^{-d})\mathcal{J}_{m}
for
m\geq 0
. Similarly, we define

F_{\phi}(s):=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}q^{nd}(1-q^{-d})[J_{n}^{\prime}]s^{nd}=\sum_{n\geq 0}q^{nd}(1-q^{-d})\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}s^{nd}=\sum_{n\geq 0}F_{n,\phi}s^{nd},

(6.4)

where
F_{n,\phi}:=q^{nd}(1-q^{-d})\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}
for
n\geq 0
. Next, define the sequence
(M_{k})
for
k\geq 1
as

M_{k}=q^{-dk}(q^{d}-1)(\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{Y})\frac{(q^{d}\mathcal{X})^{k}-\mathcal{Y}^{k}}{q^{d}\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{Y}}.

and define a generating series

M(s,t):=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}q^{2dk}M_{k}t^{dk}s^{dk}.

Then, a reformulation of Theorem
6.8
is given by the the generating series identity

E_{\phi}(t)F_{\phi}(s)=M(s,t)F_{\phi}(s)E_{\phi}(t).

(6.5)

(Note that
M(s,t)
is independent of the choice of
\phi
.) We can factorize
M(s,t)
as follows:

\displaystyle M(s,t)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}q^{2dk}M_{k}t^{dk}s^{dk}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}q^{dk}(q^{d}-1)(\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{Y})\frac{(q^{d}\mathcal{X})^{k}-\mathcal{Y}^{k}}{q^{d}\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{Y}}t^{dk}s^{dk}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-q^{d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{X})(1-q^{2d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{Y})}{(1-q^{2d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{X})(1-q^{d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{Y})}.

Define the formal logarithms of the series
E_{\phi}(t)
and
F_{\phi}(s)
:

\displaystyle C_{\phi}(t)

\displaystyle:=\log(E_{\phi}(t))=\sum_{m\geq 1}C_{m,\phi}t^{dm},

(6.6)

\displaystyle D_{\phi}(s)

\displaystyle:=\log(F_{\phi}(s))=\sum_{n\geq 1}D_{n,\phi}s^{dn}.

As
M(s,t)
commutes with both
E_{\phi}(t)
and
F_{\phi}(s)
, we get by
[
CLL+18
, Corollary 6.20]
that

M(s,t)=\exp([C_{\phi}(t),D_{\phi}(s)]).

Therefore, we have the following:

\displaystyle[C_{\phi}(t),D_{\phi}(s)]

\displaystyle=\log(M(s,t))

\displaystyle=\log\Bigg(\frac{(1-q^{d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{X})(1-q^{2d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{Y})}{(1-q^{2d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{X})(1-q^{d}t^{d}s^{d}\mathcal{Y})}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{q^{dk}t^{dk}s^{dk}\mathcal{X}^{k}}{k}-\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{q^{2dk}t^{dk}s^{dk}\mathcal{Y}^{k}}{k}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{q^{2dk}t^{dk}s^{dk}\mathcal{X}^{k}}{k}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{q^{dk}t^{dk}s^{dk}\mathcal{Y}^{k}}{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{q^{dk}t^{dk}s^{dk}}{k}(q^{dk}-1)(\mathcal{X}^{k}-\mathcal{Y}^{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(q-1)^{dk}t^{dk}s^{dk}}{k}(q^{dk}-1)([M]^{dk}-[M^{\prime}]^{dk}).

Comparing coefficients of
t^{dn}s^{dm}
on both sides of the above equality, we get:

Corollary 6.9
.

For all
m,n\geq 1
, we have the following identity:

[C_{m,\phi},D_{n,\phi}]=\delta_{m,n}\frac{(q-1)^{dn}(q^{dn}-1)}{n}([M]^{dn}-[M^{\prime}]^{dn}).

Note that since the elements
C_{m,\phi}
and
D_{n,\phi}
generate the algebra
Hall_{\phi}
, the above corollary (along with the fact that
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
are central) completely describes the structure of this algebra.

Before we proceed, we record another set of generators for the algebra
Hall_{\phi}
and an analog of Theorem
6.8
for these generators. Define the sequences

\mathcal{P}_{m}:=q^{dm(m-1)/2}[R_{\phi}(1)^{\oplus m}],\qquad\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\prime}:=q^{dn(n-1)/2}[R_{{\phi}}(1)^{\prime\oplus n}],

for all
m,n\geq 0
. The subalgebra of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
generated by the
\mathcal{P}_{m}
’s (resp.
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\prime}
’s) is isomorphic to
{Sym}
and is equal to the algebra generated by the
\mathcal{J}_{m}
’s (resp.
\mathcal{J}_{n}^{\prime}
’s). Thus, the elements
\mathcal{P}_{m}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\prime}
also generate the subalgebra
Hall_{\phi}
of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}).
Then, the following theorem is a fun computational exercise, which follows from the above corollary and the explicit isomorphism of the classical Hall algebra with
{Sym}
(see
[
Mac15
, Chapter III, (3.4)]
).

Theorem 6.10
.

For all
m,n\geq 1
, the following identity holds in the Hall algebra:

\mathcal{P}_{m}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\prime}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})^{dk}\mathcal{P}_{n-k}^{\prime}\mathcal{P}_{m-k}\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}\left(\frac{\mathbf{v}^{-dt}[M]^{d}-\mathbf{v}^{dt}[M^{\prime}]^{d}}{\mathbf{v}^{d(t+1)}-\mathbf{v}^{-d(t+1)}}\right).

Remark 6.11
.

The curious looking product in the right hand side of the above expression appears via an application of the
q
-binomial theorem involving the quantum integers defined in Section
2.1
.
∎

Next, we describe a slightly more explicit formulation for the sequence
(h_{n})
from the previous section in terms of the sequences defined in equation (
6.6
).

Lemma 6.12
.

For all
k\geq 1
, we have the following equality:

h_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{v}^{-k}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\sum_{d|k}\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{d}}C_{k/d,\pi}.

Similarly, for all
k\leq-1
, we have the following equality:

h_{k}=-\frac{\kappa^{-2k}\mathbf{v}^{k}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\sum_{d|k}\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{d}}D_{-k/d,\pi}.

Proof.

We only need to prove the lemma when
k\geq 1
, since for
k\leq-1
, the statement follows by applying
\sigma
. The generating function for the sequence
\psi_{d}
is as follows:

\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi_{k-1}t^{k}=(q-1)\kappa*\sum_{k\geq 0}R_{k}\mathbf{v}^{-k}t^{k}.

Recall that

\displaystyle R_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{d}}{q-1}\sum\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{1}}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{2}}}\right)\cdots\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{l_{r}}}\right)[R_{\pi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus R_{\pi_{2}}(n_{2})\oplus\cdots R_{\pi_{r}}(n_{r})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(q-1)}\sum\prod_{k=1}^{r}q^{l_{k}(n_{k}-1)}(q^{l_{k}}-1)[R_{\pi_{k}}(n_{k})],\text{ (By Lemma}~\ref{lem:rnexpression})

where the sum is over all finite sets
\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\cdots,\pi_{r}\}
of monic irreducible polynomials such that
\deg(\pi_{i})=l_{i}
and
\sum l_{i}n_{i}=d
. Then, using the unique factorization for polynomials over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, the generating function
P(t)
defined in (
5.13
) for
\psi_{d}
can be factorized as

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi_{k-1}t^{k}

\displaystyle=\kappa*\prod_{\pi\in\Sigma}\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 0}(q^{deg(\pi)(k-1)}(q^{deg(\pi)}-1)[R_{\pi}(k)](\mathbf{v}^{-1}t)^{k\cdot deg(\pi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa*\prod_{\pi\in\Sigma}E_{\pi}(\mathbf{v}^{-1}t),

where for any
\pi\in\Sigma_{d}
, where the series
E_{\pi}(t)
was defined in Equation (
6.3
). Recall that the formal logarithm of this series is given by
C_{\pi}(t)
.
So, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}h_{k}t^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\log\Big(\kappa^{-1}*\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi_{k-1}t^{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\sum_{\pi}\log(E_{\pi}(\mathbf{v}^{-1}t))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\sum_{\pi}\sum_{k\geq 0}C_{k,\pi}(\mathbf{v}^{-1}t)^{dk}.

Hence, for all
k\geq 1
, we have

h_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{v}^{-k}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}\sum_{d|k}\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{d}}C_{k/d,\pi}.

∎

Proposition 6.13
.

For all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, we have the following identity:

h_{l}*h_{k}-h_{k}*h_{l}=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{c^{l}-c^{-l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}}.

Proof.

If
k,l>0
or
k,l<0
, it is clear that
h_{k}
and
h_{l}
commute. Hence, without loss of generality, we suppose that
l>0
and
k<0
. Now, by Lemma
3.15
,
for any
n,n^{\prime}>0
and any
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, we have that

[C_{n,\pi},D_{n^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}]=0

if
\pi\neq{\pi^{\prime}}
. Using this fact and the expressions for the
h_{k}
’s derived in Lemma
6.12
, we get that

\displaystyle[h_{l},h_{k}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathbf{v}^{k-l}\kappa^{-2k}}{(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})^{2}}\sum_{d|(k,l)}\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{d}}[C_{l/d,\pi},D_{-k/d,{\pi}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathbf{v}^{k-l}\kappa^{-2k}}{(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})^{2}}\sum_{d|(k,l)}\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{d}}\delta_{l/d,-k/d}\frac{(q-1)^{l}(q^{l}-1)}{l/d}([M]^{l}-[M^{\prime}]^{l})

(By Corollary
6.9
)

\displaystyle=-\delta_{l,-k}\frac{\mathbf{v}^{-2l}\kappa^{2l}}{(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})^{2}}\frac{(q-1)^{l}(q^{l}-1)}{l}([M]^{l}-[M^{\prime}]^{l})\sum_{d|l}\sum_{\pi\in\Sigma_{d}}d

\displaystyle=-\delta_{k,-l}\frac{(q^{l}-1)}{lq^{l}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})^{2}}(c^{-l}-c^{l})\sum_{d|l}d|\Sigma_{d}|

\displaystyle=\delta_{k,-l}\frac{(q^{l}-1)}{lq^{l}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})^{2}}(c^{l}-c^{-l})(q^{l}+1)\text{ (By Lemma~\ref{lem:finite})}

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{c^{l}-c^{-l}}{\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}},

completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

For any
l\geq 1
, we have the following equality:

\sum_{d|l}d|\Sigma_{d}|=q^{l}+1.

Proof.

Recall that for
d>1
, the set
\Sigma_{d}
consists of monic irreducible polynomials of degree
d
, whereas
\Sigma_{1}
consists of all monic polynomials of degree
1
and the polynomial
0
. Over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, we have the factorization

x^{q^{l}}-x=\prod_{\phi}\phi(x),

where the product runs over all monic irreducible polynomials whose degree divides
l
. Then the lemma follows by comparing degrees on both sides (and adding 1 to the right to account for the polynomial 0).
∎

7
The image and kernel

Much of the literature about (point-counting) Hall algebras of quivers involves the
composition subalgebra
, which is the subalgebra generated by the simple objects. However, our main theorem shows that the image of the map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{tw}

is significantly larger than the composition subalgebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
. In this subsection we give an abstract characterization of this image which we call the
spherical subalgebra
– this definition also applies to other categories and may be of independent interest.

The main theorem of this section is the following:

Theorem 7.1
.

The map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{tw}
is injective, and its image is precisely the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{tw}
(see Definition
7.2
).

Proof.

Injectivity is proved in Proposition
7.22
and Corollary
7.23
, and the image of
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
is computed in Proposition
7.19
.
∎

7.1
The spherical subalgebra

In this section we define the spherical subalgebra and establish some of its basic properties.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a finitary abelian category.

1.

For an object
Y
, let
\alpha(Y)=\big\lvert\Aut(Y)\big\rvert
(as before), and define

c(Y):=\dim\left(\End_{\mathcal{C}}(Y)\right).

We will write
X\preceq Y
if
c(X)\leq c(Y)
.

2.

[
BG16
]
Define a partial order on the set of isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{C}
where
N\vartriangleleft M
if
M\cong N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime}
and there is a nonsplit short exact sequence
N^{\prime}\to N\to N^{\prime\prime}
.

3.

If
C
is a set of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, we write

\mathrm{min}_{\preceq}(C):=\{X\in C\mid X\preceq Y\textrm{ for all }Y\in C\}

for the set of objects in
C
that minimize the partial order
\preceq
.

4.

Given an element
\beta\in K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, write
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
for the set of objects in the class
\beta
, and define

{\Theta}_{\beta}:=\sum_{Y\in\min_{\preceq}(\mathcal{C}_{\beta})}\alpha(Y)[Y].

(7.1)

We will call a term
Y\in\min_{\preceq}(\mathcal{C}_{\beta})
in this sum a
minimizer
, or a
\preceq
-
minimizer in class
\beta
.

5.

The
spherical subalgebra
of
\mathcal{C}
is the subalgebra of the Hall algebra
Hall(\mathcal{C})
generated by the
{\Theta}_{\beta}
:

Hall^{{\mathrm{sph}}}(\mathcal{C}):=\langle{\Theta}_{\beta}\mid\beta\in K_{0}(\mathcal{C})\rangle\subset Hall(\mathcal{C}).

6.

Let
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{tw}
be the localization of
Hall^{\mathrm{sph}}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
inside the localization
\mathcal{H}
. Note that since
Hall^{\mathrm{sph}}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
is generated by homogeneous elements, the subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
remains a subalgebra after twisting the product in
\mathcal{H}
. We therefore will omit the notation “
tw
” from
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
and let the reader infer from context whether the twist is implied.

∎

Remark 7.3
.

Instead of the order on objects where
X\preceq Y
if
\dim(\End(X))\leq\dim(\End(Y))
, one could instead use a more refined order based on the graded dimension of
\Hom^{\bullet}(X,X)
. When
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{R}ep({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
, it turns out that all elements
{\Theta}_{(m,n)}
would be the same with this refined order, with one exception:
{\Theta}^{\mathrm{refined}}_{(2,2)}=(q^{2}-q)([M]+[M^{\prime}])
. It follows that the subalgebra generated by the
{\Theta}^{\mathrm{refined}}_{(m,n)}
is exactly the same as the one generated by the
{\Theta}_{(m,n)}
, so the simpler definition is sufficient for our purposes.
∎

Our partial order
\preceq
was inspired by the partial order
\vartriangleleft
defined in
[
BG16
]
; we first restate one of their key propositions in our notation, which essentially says that the order
\preceq
is a refinement of
\vartriangleleft
.

Proposition 7.4

(
[
BG16
, Prop. 4.8]
)
.

For any short exact sequence
N^{\prime}\to N\to N^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have

c(N)\leq c(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime}).

If this is an equality, then the short exact sequence splits.

Remark 7.5
.

We include several elementary remarks:

1.

If
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is an (anti-)autoequivalence , then
F(\Theta_{\beta})=\Theta_{F(\beta)}
, so
F
preserves the spherical subalgebra.

2.

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{R}ep(Q)
for some quiver
Q
without oriented cycles, then simple objects are obviously minimal with respect to the ordering
\preceq
, since simples have 1-dimensional endomorphism rings. (This also turns out to be true for the quiver-with-relations
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
.)
This means the composition subalgebra of
Q
is a subalgebra of the spherical subalgebra.

3.

Our results show that the containment is proper for
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
. For example, the composition subalgebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
is generated by the simples
[I_{0}]
and
[P_{0}]
, so the dimension of the degree
(1,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}=K_{0}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
graded piece of the composition subalgebra is 2 (a basis is
[I_{0}][P_{0}]
and
[P_{0}][I_{0}]
). However, in the spherical subalgebra, the 3 elements
[I_{0}][P_{0}]
,
R_{1}
, and
R_{1}^{\prime}
all have degree
(1,1)
, and they are linearly independent by
[
BG16
, Thm. 2.4]
(see Theorem
7.20
).

4.

Given a dimension vector
{\underline{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
for the Rudakov quiver
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
, let

H({\underline{d}})=\Hom_{Vect}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d_{1}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d_{2}})^{2}\oplus\Hom_{Vect}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d_{2}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d_{1}})^{2},

and let
H_{\mathrm{Rud}}({\underline{d}})\subset H({\underline{d}})
be the subset of quadruples
(e,f,e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
that satisfy the relations of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
. There is an obvious action of the
gauge group

{\rm{GL}}({\underline{d}}):={\rm{GL}}(d_{1})\times{\rm{GL}}(d_{2})
on
H({\underline{d}})
, and this action preserves the subset
H_{\mathrm{Rud}}({\underline{d}})
. Let
Orb({\underline{d}})
be the set of
{\rm{GL}}({\underline{d}})
orbits inside the set
H_{\mathrm{Rud}}({\underline{d}})
. We then have the equality

\frac{{\Theta}_{\underline{d}}}{\lvert{\rm{GL}}({\underline{d}})\rvert}=\sum_{Y\in\min_{\preceq}(Orb({\underline{d}}))}\frac{[Y]}{\lvert{\rm{GL}}({\underline{d}})\cdot Y\rvert}

(7.2)

where we have written
[Y]
for the isomorphism class of the module defined by an element
Y\in H({\underline{d}})
. The equality holds because
\alpha(Y)
is the size of the stabilizer of
Y
in
{\rm{GL}}({\underline{d}})
.

∎

The following remark should be compared to Lemma
2.15
, and the remark thereafter.

Remark 7.6
.

The term “spherical subalgebra” has been used in the literature already in the context of the Hall algebra
Hall(\mathrm{Coh}(X))
of the category of coherent sheaves on a smooth curve
X
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. It turns out that our definition gives the same subalgebra in this context. More explicitly, when
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the authors of
[
BS12b
]
define

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}_{0,d}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{T\in Tor(\mathbb{P}^{1}):\bar{T}=(0,d)}[T],

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{k}{\mathbbm{1}}_{0,k}z^{k}

\displaystyle=:\exp\left(\sum_{r}\frac{T_{r}}{[r]_{v}}z^{r}\right),

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{k}\Theta^{BS}_{k}z^{k}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left((v^{-1}-v)\sum_{r}T_{r}z^{r}\right).

The first sum is over all torsion sheaves of degree
d
, and the elements
T_{r}
and
{\Theta}_{r}^{BS}
are defined using the generating series equalities above. Then
[
BS12b
, Prop. 4.3]
shows that the families
\{{\mathbbm{1}}_{0,n}\}
,
\{T_{r}\}
, and
\{{\Theta}_{r}^{BS}\}
each generate the spherical subalgebra.

In
[
BS12b
, Remark 4.6]
, the authors give the following additional description of the elements
{\Theta}_{r}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{n,x}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{P}^{1})/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{n},

\displaystyle\Theta^{BS}_{r}

\displaystyle=v^{-r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}),\\
(n_{1},\cdots,n_{m})\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(1-v^{2\deg(x_{i})})[\mathcal{T}_{n_{i},x_{i}}],

(7.3)

where the sum is over collections
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})
of distinct points in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and non-negative integers
(n_{1},\cdots,n_{m})
satisfying
\sum_{i}n_{i}\deg(x_{i})=r
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{T}_{n,x}
is the unique
\preceq
-minimal sheaf of class
(0,n)\in K_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
that is supported at the point
x
. Also, a torsion sheaf
\mathcal{T}
splits canonically as a direct sum
\mathcal{T}=\oplus_{i}\mathcal{T}_{x_{i}}
, where the
x_{i}
are distinct points and each
\mathcal{T}_{x_{i}}
is supported at
x_{i}
. Finally, if
i\not=j
then
\Hom(\mathcal{T}_{x_{i}},\mathcal{T}_{x_{j}})=0=\Ext^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{x_{i}},\mathcal{T}_{x_{j}})
, which implies that the summands in (
7.3
) are exactly the
\preceq
-minimal objects of class
(0,n)\in K_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
. By
[
Mac15
, Chapter II, (1.6)]
, if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
has degree
d_{i}
, we have that
\alpha(\mathcal{T}_{n_{i},x_{i}})=q^{d_{i}n_{i}}(1-q^{-d_{i}})
, which shows that after the substitution
v\mapsto v^{-1}
, we have
\Theta^{BS}_{r}=\Theta_{(0,r)}\in Hall^{{\mathrm{sph}}}(\mathrm{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{1}))
, up to an overall scalar. ∎

Below we compute the
\Theta_{\beta}
explicitly when
\mathcal{C}
is the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules. It will be helpful to first prove two general statements which are useful for controlling the terms appearing in the sum defining
{\Theta}_{\beta}
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
Y
and
Z
be objects in
\mathcal{C}
. If
Y\oplus Z\in\min_{\preceq}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\beta}\right)
for some
\beta\in K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
,
then in the Hall algebra we have the identity
[[Y],[Z]]_{t}=0
, for
t:=\frac{\lvert\Hom(Z,Y)\rvert}{\lvert\Hom(Y,Z)\rvert}
.

Proof.

If
W
is a nonsplit extension of
Y
by
Z
, Proposition
7.4
implies
c(W)<c(Y\oplus Z)
. Combining this with the assumption
Y\oplus Z\preceq W
implies
that
\Ext^{1}(Y,Z)=0
. By symmetry we have
\Ext^{1}(Z,Y)=0
, so Lemma
4.2
implies

[[Y],[Z]]_{t}=\frac{\alpha({Y\oplus Z)}}{\alpha(Y)\alpha(Z)}\left(\frac{1}{\big\lvert\Hom(Y,Z)\big\rvert}-\frac{t}{\big\lvert\Hom(Z,Y)\big\rvert}\right)[Y\oplus Z]=0.

∎

The following lemma is essentially contained in
[
BG16
]
; we state it here in the form we will be using it for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 7.8
.

If
N^{\prime}\to N\to N^{\prime\prime}
is a nonsplit short exact sequence, then we have the strict inequality

c(N\oplus Z)<c(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime}\oplus Z)

for any
Z
. In particular,
N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime}\oplus Z
is not
\preceq
-minimal for any
Z
.

Proof.

From the proof of
[
BG16
, Prop. 4.8]
, for any
Z
we have the inequalities

\displaystyle\dim\Hom(N,Z)

\displaystyle\leq\dim\Hom(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime},Z),

\displaystyle\dim\Hom(Z,N)

\displaystyle\leq\dim\Hom(Z,N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime}).

We then compute

\displaystyle c(N\oplus Z)

\displaystyle=c(N)+c(Z)+\dim\Hom(N,Z)+\dim\Hom(Z,N)

\displaystyle\leq c(N)+c(Z)+\dim\Hom(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime},Z)+\dim\Hom(Z,N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle<c(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime})+c(Z)+\dim\Hom(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime},Z)+\dim\Hom(Z,N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=c(N^{\prime}\oplus N^{\prime\prime}\oplus Z),

where the strict inequality follows from
[
BG16
, Prop. 4.8]
(see Proposition
7.4
) and the nonsplit assumption.
∎

7.2
Surjectivity: spherical generators for the Rudakov quiver

We now use our earlier results to study the spherical subalgebra when
\mathcal{C}
is the category of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules. Recall that in Theorem
5.4
we constructed a map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{tw}
from the deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
to the twisted Hall algebra
\mathcal{H}^{tw}
. In this section we show
that the image of the map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
is exactly the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{tw}
. This proof relies heavily on the classification of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules, so we begin with a series of technical lemmas.

Lemma 7.9
.

If
X
is a
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-module with dimension vector
(d_{1},d_{2})
, then

c(X\oplus M)=c(X\oplus M^{\prime})=c(X)+d_{1}+d_{2}+2.

In particular, if
X
and
Y
have the same dimension vector and
X\prec Y
, then
X\oplus M\prec Y\oplus M
and
X\oplus M^{\prime}\prec Y\oplus M^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Corollary
2.28
shows
\Hom(M,X)=X_{1}
and
\Hom(X,M)=X_{2}^{\vee}
, so
c(X\oplus M)=c(X)+d_{1}+d_{2}+c(M)
by the bilinearity of the functor
\Hom(-,-)
.
∎

Recall that we say that a module with dimension vector
(d_{1},d_{2})
has positive slope if
d_{2}-d_{1}>0
. The following two lemmas essentially say that a sum of indecomposables with differently signed slopes cannot be
\preceq
-minimal.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
Z
be in the set
S=\{I_{n},P_{n},I^{\prime}_{n},P^{\prime}_{n}\,\mid\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
.

1.

If both of
\Ext^{1}(Z,P_{k})
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{k},Z)
are zero for some
k\geq 1
,
then
Z\in\{P_{k-1},P_{k},P_{k+1}\}
.

2.

If both of
\Ext^{1}(Z,P_{0})
and
\Ext^{1}(P_{0},Z)
are zero, then
Z\in\{I^{\prime}_{1},P_{0},P_{1}\}
.

Proof.

Both of these claims follow from Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
and Theorem
4.12
.
∎

Remark 7.11
.

For a fixed
k
, the auto-equivalences
\sigma
,
\sigma\circ\tau
,
\tau
and
{\mathbbm{1}}
act transitively on the set
\{I_{k},P_{k},I^{\prime}_{k},P^{\prime}_{k}\}
, so the statements in the above lemma can be translated to these sequences of modules through these equivalences.
∎

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
Z
be in the set
S=\{I_{n},P_{n},I^{\prime}_{n},P^{\prime}_{n}\,\mid\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
. If
R
is a regular module, then at least one of
\Ext^{1}(R,Z)
or
\Ext^{1}(Z,R)
is nonzero.

Proof.

Let
Z=P^{\prime}_{n}
. (The proof for other
Z
is similar.) If
R
is an
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-saturated regular representation, then the claim follows from Theorem
2.17
(1.1f). Hence, by the bilinearity of
\Ext^{1}(-,-)
, it is sufficient to assume
R=R_{\phi}(k)
for some
\phi\in\Sigma_{d}
. By the proof of Lemma
7.7
, it suffices to show that there is no constant
t\not=0
such that
[R,P^{\prime}_{n}]_{t}=0
. Towards this end, suppose
[R,P^{\prime}_{n}]_{t}=0
for some
t
and let
X=R_{\pi}(1)
with
\pi\in\Sigma_{1}
chosen so that
[R,X]=0
. Then we use the
q
-Jacobi relation (
4.7
) (evaluated at
a=t
,
b=1
,
c=q
) to compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=[R,P^{\prime}_{n}]_{t}

\displaystyle=[X,[R,P^{\prime}_{n}]_{t}]

\displaystyle=-tq^{-1}[P^{\prime}_{n},[X,R]]_{qt^{-1}}-q^{-1}[R,[P^{\prime}_{n},X]_{q}]_{t}

\displaystyle=-q^{-1}(q-1)[M][R,P^{\prime}_{n-1}]_{t}

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}q^{-n}(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}[R,P^{\prime}_{0}]_{t}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}q^{-n-1}(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}[R,[I_{0},X]_{q}]_{t}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}q^{-n-1}(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}[R,I_{1}]_{t},

where in each inductive step we take a commutator with
X
and use the identity
[P^{\prime}_{n},X]_{q}=(q-1)[M][P^{\prime}_{n-1}]
from Lemma
4.8
, and the fact that
[M]
is central. Since
R
and
I_{1}
are
(e,f)
-saturated, we may use
[
Szá06
, Cor. 4.9]
to compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}q^{-n-1}(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}[R,I_{1}]_{t}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}q^{-n-1}(q-1)^{n}[M]^{n}\left([R][I_{1}]-tq^{dk}[R][I_{1}]-tI_{1+dk}-t\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}c_{i}[R_{d}(k-i)][I_{1+di}]\right),

where the
c_{i}
are constants. Every term in the sum above is in PBW order (see
[
BG16
, Theorem 2.4]
or Theorem
7.20
), and the
I_{1+dk}
-term has a nonzero coefficient, which is a contradiction.

∎

Corollary 7.13
.

If
Y,Z\in\{I_{n},P_{n},I^{\prime}_{n},P^{\prime}_{n},R_{\phi}(l),R_{\phi}(l)^{\prime}\,\mid\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},l\geq 1,\phi\in\Sigma\}
and
Y\oplus Z
is
\preceq
-minimal, then the sign of
Y
’s slope is that same as that of
Z
’s.

Proof.

If
Y\oplus Z
is minimal, then Proposition
7.4
implies that
\Ext^{1}(Y,Z)=\Ext^{1}(Z,Y)=0
; combining this with the two lemmas above implies that
Y
has positive slope if and only if
Z
does (since the
P_{k}
and
I^{\prime}_{k}
are precisely the indecomposables with positive slope and
P_{k}^{\prime}
and
I_{k}
are the ones with negative slope), etc.
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

We have the identities

\displaystyle{\Theta}_{(n,0)}

\displaystyle=\lvert GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\rvert\left[I_{0}^{\oplus n}\right]

\displaystyle{\Theta}_{(0,n)}

\displaystyle=\lvert GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\rvert\left[P_{0}^{\oplus n}\right]
