\displaystyle{\Theta}_{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=(q-1)\left(R_{1}+R_{1}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle{\Theta}_{(2,2)}

\displaystyle=(q-1)\left(q[M]+q[M^{\prime}]+R_{2}+R_{2}^{\prime}\right)

(7.4)

in the Hall algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
.

Proof.

The sums of simples
I_{0}
and
P_{0}
are obviously the unique objects with dimension vectors
(n,0)
and
(0,n)
, respectively, which proves the first two identities.
The sum of simples
I_{0}\oplus P_{0}
is non-minimal since the simples have nontrivial extensions; all other objects with dimension vector
(1,1)
are regular and have
c(R)=1
by Theorem
2.17
, which implies the third equality (since all terms in the sum defining
R_{1}
and
R_{1}^{\prime}
have the automorphism group
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
). To see the last equality, we split into cases using the classification of indecomposable
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-modules.

Suppose
Y
is a
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-module with dimension
(2,2)
. By dimension arguments,
Y
is either a sum of regulars or is in one of the following sets:

Y_{1}:=\{M,M^{\prime}\},\quad Y_{2}:=\{I_{0}\oplus P_{1},I_{0}\oplus I_{1}^{\prime},P_{0}\oplus I_{1},P_{0}\oplus P_{1}^{\prime}\},\quad Y_{3}:=\{R\oplus I_{0}\oplus P_{0}\},\quad Y_{4}:=\{I_{0}\oplus I_{0}\oplus P_{0}\oplus P_{0}\}

where the
R
in the definition of
Y_{3}
is regular of dimension
(1,1)
.

To prove our claim, we will show that
c(Y)\geq 2
, and the equality is attained only for modules in the sets
S
or
\sigma(S)
, where
S=\{M,R_{\phi}(1),R_{\pi}(2),R_{\pi}(1)\oplus R_{\pi^{\prime}}(1)\}
where
\phi\in\Sigma_{2}
and
\pi\not=\pi^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{1}
.

By
[
Szá06
, Lemma 1.1]
(see Theorem
2.17
),
c(R_{\phi}(1))=2=c(R_{\pi}(2))
. By the same lemma, if
Y=R_{\pi}(1)\oplus R_{\pi^{\prime}}(1)
, then
c(Y)=2
if
\pi\not=\pi^{\prime}
and
c(Y)=4
if
\pi=\pi^{\prime}
. Next, if
Y=R_{\pi}(1)\oplus R_{\pi^{\prime}}(1)^{\prime}
, then
c(Y)=4
by Lemma
3.10
. Corollary
2.28
shows
c(M)=2
. This shows that if
Y\in S
or
Y\in\sigma(S)
, then
c(Y)=2
, and that sums of regulars that are not in
S
or
\sigma(S)
are not minimal.

Lemma
7.8
shows objects in
Y_{3}
or
Y_{4}
are not
\preceq
-minimal, since
I_{0}
and
P_{0}
have a nontrivial extension. Combining Lemmas
7.7
and
7.10
, and some care with notation shows that none of the objects in
Y_{2}
are minimal.

Summarizing, we have shown that the terms appearing in
\Theta_{(2,2)}
are exactly the terms displayed in equation (
7.4
). The coefficients of the terms other than
M
and
M^{\prime}
are computed in Lemma
2.15
.
Finally, to compute the coefficient
\alpha(M)=q(q-1)
, note that any map
M\to M
is uniquely determined by the image
x_{1}\mapsto ax_{1}+bx_{2}
, and the map is invertible iff
a\not=0
.
∎

Lemma 7.15
.

The elements
R_{1}
and
M
are in the spherical Hall algebra
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
7.14
we compute

2R_{1}=R_{1}+R_{1}^{\prime}+R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{q-1}{\Theta}_{(1,1)}+\frac{1}{(q-1)^{2}}\left[{\Theta}_{(1,0)},{\Theta}_{(0,1)}\right],

where the last step uses the identity
[I_{0},P_{0}]=R_{1}-R_{1}^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.14
. Next, invariance of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
under auto-equivalences combined with the identities
[R_{1},P_{0}]_{q}=(q+1)P_{1}
(see Lemma
4.7
),
[I_{0},P_{1}]_{q}=R_{2}-q[M]
(see Theorem
4.5
) and
[R_{1},R_{1}^{\prime}]=(q+1)([M]-[M^{\prime}])
(see Corollary
3.15
) imply

\{R_{2}-q[M],R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M],R_{2}-q[M^{\prime}],R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M^{\prime}],[M]-[M^{\prime}],\Theta_{(2,2)}\}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}.

We then compute

4q[M]=\frac{1}{q-1}{\Theta}_{(2,2)}-(R_{2}-q[M])-(R_{2}^{\prime}-q[M])+q([M]-[M^{\prime}]),

which shows that
[M]
is in the spherical subalgebra.
∎

Now we give explicit expressions for
{\Theta}_{\beta}
for each
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\subset K_{0}({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Suppose
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
. Then, there exist unique
j,a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
solving the equation

(m,m+d)=a(j+1,j+2)+b(j,j+1).

Explicitly,
j=\lfloor\frac{m}{d}\rfloor
, and
a=m-jd
and
b=d-(m-jd)
.

Proof.

For any
j
, the above choices of
a
and
b
are uniquely determined by the fact that
(j+1,j+2)
and
(j,j+1)
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The only
j
for which both
a
and
b
are nonnegative is
j=\lfloor\frac{m}{d}\rfloor
.
∎

Recall that we write
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}(m,n)
for the set of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
representations with dimension vector
(m,n)
.

Lemma 7.17
.

Suppose
m,d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, and let
j=\lfloor\frac{m}{d}\rfloor
. Then the only minimizers in
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}{(m,m+d)}
are

P(m,m+d):=P_{j+1}^{\oplus(m-jd)}\oplus P_{j}^{\oplus(d+jd-m)}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\tau(P(m,m+d)).

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Theta}_{(m,m+d)}

\displaystyle={\alpha(P(m,m+d))}\left([P(m,m+d)]+[\tau(P(m,m+d))]\right)

\displaystyle=c_{d,m}\alpha(P(m,m+d))\left([P_{j}]^{d+jd-m}[P_{j+1}]^{m-jd}+[I_{j+1}^{\prime}]^{m-jd}[I_{j}^{\prime}]^{d+jd-m}\right).

(7.5)

Similarly, the only minimizers in
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}{(m+d,m)}
are

I(m+d,m):=I_{j+1}^{\oplus(m-jd)}\oplus I_{j}^{\oplus(d+jd-m)}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\tau(I(m+d,m)),

so

\displaystyle{\Theta}_{(m+d,m)}

\displaystyle={\alpha(I(m+d,m))}\left([I(m+d,m)]+[\tau(I(m+d,m))]\right)

\displaystyle=c_{d,m}\alpha(I(m+d,m))\left([I_{j+1}]^{m-jd}[I_{j}]^{d+jd-m}+[P_{j}^{\prime}]^{d+jd-m}[P_{j+1}^{\prime}]^{m-jd}\right).

(7.6)

The constant
c_{d,m}
in equations (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) is given by:

c_{d,m}^{-1}=\mathbf{v}^{(d^{2}-d)/2-(m-jd)(d+jd-m)}[m-jd]![d+jd-m]!.

Proof.

We only prove the first set of claims, since the proof in the latter case follows from it by applying
\sigma
.
Suppose
Y
is a minimizer in
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}{(m,m+d)}
. Since
d>0
, we know
Y
must contain an indecomposable summand with positive slope. By Lemmas
7.8
and
7.12
, this implies
Y
cannot contain a regular summand. By Lemma
7.10
, if
P_{k}
is a summand of
Y
, then the only other possible summands are
P_{k-1}
or
P_{k+1}
(but not both), and
M
or
M^{\prime}
,
which means that without loss of generality, we can assume

Y=P_{k}^{a}\oplus P_{k+1}^{b}\oplus M^{r}\oplus M^{\prime r^{\prime}}.

First, suppose that
r=r^{\prime}=0
; then dimension counting using Lemma
7.16
shows the numbers
m
and
d
uniquely determine
k
,
a
, and
b
, so the only possibility for
Y
in this case is
P(m,m+d)
. Similarly, if
I_{k}^{\prime}=\tau(P_{k})
is a summand of
Y
(and
M
and
M^{\prime}
are not), then we must have
Y=\tau(P(m,m+d))
.

To show that
M
and
M^{\prime}
cannot be summands of
Y
we compute
c(Y)
. First, Theorem
2.17
shows

\displaystyle c(P(m,m+d))

\displaystyle=c(P_{j+1}^{a}\oplus P_{j}^{b})

\displaystyle=c({P_{j+1}^{a}})+\dim(\Hom(P_{j+1}^{a},P_{j}^{b}))+\dim(\Hom(P_{j}^{b},P_{j+1}^{a}))+c(P_{j}^{b})

\displaystyle=a^{2}+0+2ab+b^{2}

\displaystyle=d^{2}.

Now if
r=1
, the same dimension argument as in the previous paragraph shows that
Y=P(m-2,m-2+d)\oplus M
, so we use Lemma
7.9
to compute

c(P(m-2,m-2+d)\oplus M)=d^{2}+(m-2+m-2+d+2)\geq d^{2}+d.

This shows that
M\oplus P(m-2,m-2+d)
is not minimal, which shows
r\not=1
; then Lemma
7.9
shows
r
cannot be bigger than
1
either by induction on
r
. A similar argument shows
r^{\prime}=0
. To finish the proof, equation (
7.5
) follows from Theorem
2.17
[1c], and the computation of the coefficient
c_{d,m}
follows from Lemma
4.2
and
[
Kir16
, Equation 4.9]
. ∎

Lemma 7.18
.

For
m\geq 3
, we have the following identity:

{\Theta}_{(m,m)}=(q-1)\left(R_{m}+R^{\prime}_{m}\right).

Proof.

Suppose
X
is a minimal object of dimension
(m,m)
. If
X
is indecomposable, then either
X
or
\sigma(X)
is isomorphic to a regular representation
R_{\sigma}(k)
, where
\sigma\in\Sigma_{d}
and
dk=m
. In this case,
c(X)=m
by Theorem
2.17
.

Now suppose that
Y,Z\subset X
are nontrivial indecomposable summands.

1.

By Corollary
7.13
,
Y
cannot be in the set
\{I_{n},I^{\prime}_{n},P_{n},P^{\prime}_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
: if it were, then the slope of the dimension vector of all other summands would have the same sign as the slope of the dimension vector of
Y
. This is impossible since the slope of
X
is 0.

2.

If
Y=R_{\pi}(j)
and
Z=R_{\phi}(k)
are both
(e,f)
-regular, we must have
\pi\not=\phi
(otherwise they have a nontrivial extension between them, which is ruled out by Lemma
7.8
).

3.

If
Y
is
(e,f)
-regular and
Z
is
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-regular, then
\Hom(Y,Z)=\Hom_{Vect}(Y_{1},Z_{1})
by Lemma
3.10
. This implies
c(X)\geq m+\dim(Y_{1})\dim(Z_{1})>m
since
Y,Z
are nontrivial and have slope zero.

4.

If
Y=M
, then
\Hom(M,X)=X_{1}
by Corollary
2.28
. This implies
c(X)\geq m+c(Z)>m
, where the last inequality follows by the assumption that
Z
is nontrivial (since
m>2
). An analogous argument implies
M^{\prime}
is also not a summand of
X
.

The only remaining possibilities for
X
are modules of the form

R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n}):=R_{\phi_{1}}(n_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus R_{\phi_{r}}(n_{r}),\qquad\mathrm{or}\qquad\sigma(R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n}))

where the
\phi_{i}
are all distinct and
m=\sum_{i}n_{i}\deg(\phi_{i})
. Then Lemma
2.15
shows the coefficient of the term
R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n})
in
R_{m}
is exactly
\alpha(R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n}))
. Finally, the results in
[
Szá06
]
(see Theorem
2.17
) show
c(R_{\underline{\phi}}(\underline{n}))=m
for any allowed choice of
\underline{\phi}
and
\underline{n}
, so each of these appears as a term in the sum defining
{\Theta}_{(m,m)}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.19
.

The image of the map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{tw}
is exactly the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
.

Proof.

Corollary
5.5
shows that the intersection of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
with the image of
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{tw}
is generated by the elements
[I_{0}],[P_{0}],R_{1}
and
[M]
. Lemmas
7.14
and
7.15
show that these generators are contained in
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
, and Lemmas
7.17
and
7.18
show that all generators
{\Theta}_{(d_{1},d_{2})}
of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{sph}}
are contained in the image of
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
.
∎

7.3
Injectivity, PBW bases

In this section, we prove that the map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
constructed in Theorem
5.4
is injective. The main idea of the proof is to construct a spanning set for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
and show that it maps to a linearly independent set under the constructed homomorphism (see Proposition
7.22
). As a consequence, we will also conclude that the spanning set is a basis for
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
(see Corollary
7.23
). The key ingredient in the proof is the following result:

Theorem 7.20

(
[
BG16
, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finitary Krull-Schmidt exact category. Then, for any total order on the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable objects in
\mathcal{A}
, the set of ordered monomials of indecomposable objects forms a basis of the Hall algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Define the following set
\mathcal{S}
of ordered monomials in the generators of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
:

C^{n/2}S^{m}E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}F_{\beta}^{q_{\beta}}H_{{\gamma}}^{r_{\gamma}},

where
{\alpha}
and
{\beta}
are finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequences of integers,
{\gamma}
is a finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequence of non-zero integers,
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta}
and
r_{\gamma}
are sequences of non-negative integers, and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Here, we are using multi-exponent notation

E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}=E_{\alpha_{1}}^{p_{1}}E_{\alpha_{2}}^{p_{2}}\cdots E_{\alpha_{k}}^{p_{k}},

where
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{k}),
etc.

The following lemma is an easy consequence of the defining relations of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
:

Lemma 7.21
.

The set
\mathcal{S}
spans the space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
as a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space.

Under the map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
, the set
\mathcal{S}
maps (up to scalars) to the set
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
of the following monomials in the localized Hall algebra
\mathcal{H}
:

(q-1)^{(a+b)/4}[M]^{a/4}[M^{\prime}]^{b/4}[P_{\alpha}]^{p_{\alpha}}[I_{\beta}^{\prime}]^{q_{\beta}}[I_{{\gamma}}]^{r_{\gamma}}[P_{\delta}^{\prime}]^{s_{\delta}}R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}R_{\nu}^{\prime n_{\nu}},

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu
and
\nu
are finite (possibly) empty decreasing sequences of positive integers (we allow the sequences
\alpha
and
\gamma
to contain the integer
0
to account for the two simples),
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta},r_{\gamma},s_{\delta},m_{\mu}
and
n_{\nu}
are sequences of non-negative integers, and
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proposition 7.22
.

The set
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is linearly independent in the algebra
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

Since
[M]^{1/4}
and
[M^{\prime}]^{1/4}
are central units in
\mathcal{H}
, we just need to show that the following elements are linearly independent:

X_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}:=[P_{\alpha}]^{p_{\alpha}}[I_{\beta}^{\prime}]^{q_{\beta}}[I_{{\gamma}}]^{r_{\gamma}}[P_{\delta}^{\prime}]^{s_{\delta}}R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}R_{\nu}^{\prime n_{\nu}}.

For a given sequence
\mu
, we have that

R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}=R_{\mu_{1}}^{m_{1}}R_{\mu_{2}}^{m_{2}}\cdots R_{\mu_{k}}^{m_{k}},

where
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{k})
. Note that we can expand each of the
R_{n}
(and the
R_{n}^{\prime}
’s) out to express
X_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}
as a sum of ordered monomials of indecomposable objects (see Lemma
2.15
).

We fix the lexicographic order on the set of sequences
\mu
: we say that
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{k})
is greater than
\mu^{\prime}=(\mu_{1}^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime},\dots,\mu_{l}^{\prime})
if any of the following conditions is true:

•

\mu_{1}>\mu_{1}^{\prime}

•

\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}^{\prime}
and
m_{1}>m_{1}^{\prime}

•

\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}=m_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mu_{2}>\mu_{2}^{\prime}

\vdots

Next, fix an (arbitrary) element
\phi_{d}\in\Sigma_{d}
for all
d\geq 1
. Then, by the definition of
R_{n}
, we note that the following term occurs as a summand (with non-zero coefficient) of
R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}
:

[R_{\phi_{\mu_{1}}}(1)]^{m_{1}}[R_{\phi_{\mu_{2}}}(1)]^{m_{2}}\cdots[R_{\phi_{\mu_{k}}}(1)]^{m_{k}}.

We call this the leading term
L_{\mu}
of
R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}
. Similarly, we define a leading term
L_{\nu}^{\prime}
of
R_{\nu}^{\prime n_{\nu}}
. So, we see that the monomial
X_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}
contains the following product of indecomposable elements as a summand:

L_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}:=[P_{\alpha}]^{p_{\alpha}}[I_{\beta}^{\prime}]^{q_{\beta}}[I_{{\gamma}}]^{r_{\gamma}}[P_{\delta}^{\prime}]^{s_{\delta}}L_{\mu}L_{\nu}^{\prime}.

By Theorem
7.20
, the elements
L_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}
are linearly independent for varying sextuples of sequences
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu)
.

It is clear that the leading term
L_{\mu}
of
R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}
can not occur as a summand in
R_{\mu^{\prime}}^{m_{\mu^{\prime}}}
if
\mu
is greater than
\mu^{\prime}
in the lexicographic order. As a consequence, the monomial
L_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}
can not occur as a summand of
X_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}
unless
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta=\beta^{\prime}
,
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
,
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
and
\mu
and
\nu
are both lesser than or equal to
\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}
respectively. Then, by an upper-triangular argument, we conclude that the linear independence of the
L_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}
’s implies the linear independence of the
X_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu,\nu}
’s.
∎

Corollary 7.23
.

The set
\mathcal{S}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
) is linearly independent and forms a
\mathbb{C}
-basis for the space
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
). Furthermore, the set
\mathcal{S}
also forms a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-basis for the space
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
.

Proof.

The set
\mathcal{S}
is linearly independent in
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
, since its homomorphic image
\tilde{S}
is, proving the first claim. To prove the latter claim, we observe that if there is a linear relation between the elements of
\mathcal{S}
over
\mathbb{C}(v)
, then that relation must evaluate to zero whenever
v
is specialized to
v=\mathbf{v}(=q^{1/2})
for all but finitely many prime powers
q
. This implies that the relation must itself be zero, proving linear independence.
∎

Remark 7.24
.

By a straightforward modification of the above proof, it follows that for any ordering on the the set of generators
\{C^{1/2},S,E_{\alpha},F_{\beta},H_{\gamma}\},
the set of ordered monomials gives a basis of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
.
∎

8
The deformed quantum affine algebra

In this section we establish some algebraic properties of
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
and show that it is a flat deformation of
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
. We also find an integral form and provide a Levendorskii type finite presentation.

8.1
Algebraic properties

In this section, we describe structural properties of the deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
. First of all, we note that just like the algebras in
[
FT19a
]
, the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
has a triangular decomposition: let
\mathcal{U}^{>0}
,
\mathcal{U}^{0}
and
\mathcal{U}^{<0}
be the subalgebras of
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
generated by the sets
\{E_{l}:l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,
\{H_{n},S^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}:n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}
and
\{F_{l}:l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
respectively. The following proposition is an immediate consequence of Corollary
7.23
and Remark
7.24
:

Proposition 8.1
.

The multiplication map

\mathcal{U}^{>0}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{<0}\to\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}

is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}(v)
-vector spaces.

The above decomposition has an interpretation in terms of slope for the algebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
. Recall that a representation
V=(V_{1},V_{2})
of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
is said to have positive, zero or negative slope depending upon whether
\dim(V_{1})
is lesser than, equal to or greater than
\dim(V_{2})
, respectively.
Let
Hall^{>0}
,
Hall^{0}
and
Hall^{<0}
be the subalgebras of
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
generated by indecomposables having positive, zero and negative slopes, respectively. Then, the following is an analog of the above proposition for
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
and is a consequence of Theorem
7.20
:

Proposition 8.2
.

The multiplication map

Hall^{>0}\otimes Hall^{0}\otimes Hall^{<0}\to Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})

is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces.

The map
\theta_{\mathbf{v}}
constructed in Theorem
5.4
sends
\mathcal{U}^{>0}
into
\mathcal{H}^{>0}
, which is defined as the sublagebra of
\mathcal{H}^{tw}
obtained by adjoining
((q-1)[M])^{-1/4}
and
((q-1)[M^{\prime}])^{-1/4}
to
Hall^{>0}
, etc. The structure of the slope subalgebras
\mathcal{H}^{>0}
and
\mathcal{H}^{<0}
was studied in Section
4.1
, whereas that of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
was studied in Section
6
.

The algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
has other decompositions that are in some sense ‘orthogonal’ to the above decomposition: fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{k}^{+}
and
\mathcal{U}_{k}^{+}
be the following subalgebras of
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
:

\mathcal{U}_{k}^{+}:=\langle\{E_{n+k-1},F_{n-k},H_{n},S^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}:n\geq 1\}\rangle,\qquad\mathcal{U}_{k}^{-}:=\langle\{E_{n+k},F_{n-k+1},H_{n},S^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}:n\leq-1\}\rangle.

Then, it is clear from the defining relations that both of these subalgebras are spanned by ordered monomials in their respective generators. These ordered monomials are linearly independent by Corollary
7.23
, giving bases for both of these spaces. Furthermore, both of these algebras can be viewed as left as well as right modules over the commutative algebra
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathbb{C}[C^{\pm 1/2},S^{\pm 1}]
. Then, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 8.3
.

The multiplication map

\mathcal{U}_{k}^{+}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{-}\to\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}

is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}(v)
-vector spaces.

Proof.

The surjectivity is clear. To see injectivity, we note that the algebras
\mathcal{U}^{\pm}_{k}
are free as left (or right)
\mathcal{Z}
-modules, which follows from Corollary
7.23
.
∎

For
k=0
and
k=1
, the above decomposition can be interpreted Hall theoretically using Definition
3.3
. Let
Hall_{0}^{+}\subset Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})
(resp.
Hall_{1}^{+}
) be the subspace spanned by
(e,f)
-injective (resp.
(e,f)
-surjective) objects, and let
Hall_{0}^{-}
(resp.
Hall_{1}^{-}
) be the subspace spanned by
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-injective (resp.
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-surjective) objects. By Lemma
3.5
, both
Hall_{0}^{\pm}
and
Hall_{1}^{\pm}
are subalgebras, not just subspaces.

Proposition 8.4
.

For
k=0,1
, the multiplication map

\mu:Hall_{k}^{+}\otimes Hall_{k}^{-}\to Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})

is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

Proof.

The proof follows from Theorem
7.20
after observing that the set of
(e,f)
-surjective representations and
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-surjective representations (resp.
(e,f)
-injective and
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
-injective representations) exactly partition the set of indecomposable
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-representations (see Remark
3.4
and the preceding table). ∎

For
k=0,1
, the map constructed in Theorem
5.4
sends
\mathcal{U}_{k}^{\pm}
into
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\pm}
, which is defined as the sublagebra of
\mathcal{H}^{tw}
obtained by adjoining
((q-1)[M])^{-1/4}
and
((q-1)[M^{\prime}])^{-1/4}
to
Hall_{k}^{\pm}
.

Next, we compute the center of
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
. Recall that by Corollary
7.23
, the space
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
has a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-basis given by the following set
\mathcal{S}
of ordered monomials:

C^{n/2}S^{m}E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}F_{\beta}^{q_{\beta}}H_{{\gamma}}^{r_{\gamma}},

where
{\alpha}
and
{\beta}
are finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequences of integers,
{\gamma}
is a finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequence of non-zero integers,
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta}
and
r_{\gamma}
are sequences of non-negative integers, and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Denote by
|p_{\alpha}|
the sum of all exponents occurring in
E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}
, etc.

Proposition 8.5
.

The center of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is equal to
\mathbb{C}[C^{\pm 1/2}]
.

Proof.

Suppose
z
is an element in the center. Then
z
is a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-linear combination of elements of the form

C^{n/2}S^{m}E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}F_{\beta}^{q_{\beta}}H_{{\gamma}}^{r_{\gamma}}

from the set
\mathcal{S}
. Pick one such monomial
\mathfrak{m}
in
z
with non-empty
\alpha
, that is maximal with respect to the lexicographic order on
\alpha
. By relation (
2.9
), we note that the commutator
[H_{1},\mathfrak{m}]
is a non-zero linear combination of terms that are bigger than
\mathfrak{m}
with respect to the lexicographic order.
However, this contradicts the fact that
[H_{1},z]
must be zero. Hence, the element
z
can only be a linear combination of elements in which the sequence
\alpha
is empty. By a similar argument, we can conclude that
\beta
must also be empty.

So,
z
must be a linear combination of elements of the form

C^{n/2}S^{m}H_{{\gamma}}^{r_{\gamma}}.

Next, pick
k
such that
H_{k}
occurs with a non-zero power in a monomial in
z
. Then, by relation (
2.11
), it is clear that the commutator of
H_{-k}
with
z
can not be zero. This shows that
z
can only consist of terms of the form
C^{n/2}S^{m}
. To that end, note that

[C^{n/2}S^{m},E_{0}]=(1-v^{-m})C^{n/2}S^{m}E_{0}.

This forces
m
to be zero in any monomial in
z
. Therefore,
z
must be a Laurent polynomial in
C^{1/2}
, completing the proof.
∎

8.2
Integral form

In this section, we find an integral form of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
via the construction of an integral version of the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
. Recall that
\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
is generated as a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra by the set:

\mathcal{G}:=\{[P_{l}],[I_{l}],[P_{l+1}^{\prime}],[I_{l+1}^{\prime}],R_{l+1},R^{\prime}_{l+1},((q-1)[M])^{\pm 1/4},((q-1)[M^{\prime}])^{\pm 1/4}:l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

We constructed a basis
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
of
\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
in Section
7.3
consisting of the following ordered monomials in the elements of
\mathcal{G}
:

(q-1)^{(a+b)/4}[M]^{a/4}[M^{\prime}]^{b/4}[P_{\alpha}]^{p_{\alpha}}[I_{\beta}^{\prime}]^{q_{\beta}}[I_{{\gamma}}]^{r_{\gamma}}[P_{\delta}^{\prime}]^{s_{\delta}}R_{\mu}^{m_{\mu}}R_{\nu}^{\prime n_{\nu}},

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\mu
and
\nu
are finite (possibly) empty decreasing sequences of positive integers (we allow the sequences
\alpha
and
\gamma
to contain the integer
0
to account for the two simples),
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta},r_{\gamma},s_{\delta},m_{\mu}
and
n_{\nu}
are sequences of non-negative integers, and
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Note that the set
\tilde{S}
is independent of the field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
we are working over. Pick monomials
X,Y,Z\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
and let
P^{Z}_{X,Y}(q)
denote the coefficient of
Z
in the product
XY
in
\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
.

Proposition 8.6
.

For any
X,Y,Z\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
, the structure constant
P^{Z}_{X,Y}(q)
is a Laurent polynomial in
q^{1/2}
with integer coefficients (depending upon
X,Y
and
Z
).

Proof.

We claim that for any two elements
P,Q\in\mathcal{G}
, the commutator
[P,Q]
can be expressed as a linear combination of ordered monomials in the elements of
\mathcal{G}
such that the coefficient of each summand is a Laurent polynomial in
q^{1/2}
with integer coefficients. This claim implies the proposition, since the product
XY
can then be written as a linear combination of ordered monomials by swapping the order of the multiplicands one-by-one and introducing Laurent polynomials in
q^{1/2}
with integer coefficients at each step.

When either of
P
or
Q
is
((q-1)[M])^{\pm 1/4}
or
((q-1)[M^{\prime}])^{\pm 1/4}
, the above claim is clear. If both
P
and
Q
have positive slopes or negative slopes, the claim follows from Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
. If one of them has negative slope and the other has positive slope, the claim is a consequence of Theorem
4.12
.

Next, recall the sequences
(\phi_{d}),(\psi_{-d})
and
(h_{n})
and the element
\kappa
, defined before Thereom
5.4
, that satisfy:

\displaystyle\psi_{d}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-d-1}(q-1)\kappa*R_{d+1},

\displaystyle\varphi_{-d}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{-d-1}(q-1)\kappa^{2d+1}*R^{\prime}_{d+1},

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi_{k-1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=\kappa*\mathrm{exp}\left((\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{k}u^{k}\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\varphi_{-k+1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}*\mathrm{exp}\left(-(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{-k}u^{-k}\right).

If one of
P
and
Q
has zero slope and other other has non-zero slope, then the claim is a consequence of Proposition
5.10
and the fact that
R_{d}
(resp.
R_{d}^{\prime}
) can be expressed as polynomial functions of
h_{n}
’s (resp.
h_{-n}
’s) with
1\leq n\leq d
with coefficients that are Laurent polynomials in
q^{1/2}
. Furthermore, when
R_{d}^{\prime}
is expressed as
a polynomial of the
h_{-n}^{\prime}s
, the coefficients are all multiples of
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{-1}
. Hence, the case when
P
and
Q
both have zero slope is a consequence of Proposition
5.12
.
∎

The above proposition shows that we can define the
universal integral Hall algebra

\mathcal{H}_{v}
as follows:

Definition 8.7
.

Define
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{v}
to be the
\mathbb{Z}[v,v^{-1}]
-algebra generated by the set
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
where the product is defined via:

X\cdot Y:=\sum_{Z\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}P^{Z}_{X,Y}(v)Z

for any
X,Y\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}.
We also define the
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]
-algebra
\mathcal{H}_{v}
:

\mathcal{H}_{v}:=\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[v,v^{-1}]}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{v}.

Finally, for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, we define the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{v}}
to be the specialization of
\mathcal{H}_{v}
at
v=\mathbf{v}
.
∎

It is clear that the specialized algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{v}}
for
\mathbf{v}=q^{1/2}
(where
q
is any prime power) is exactly the spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{H}^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
of the localized and twisted Hall algebra of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
-representations over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. This fact, in turn, implies that the polynomials
P^{Z}_{X,Y}(v)
are zero for all but finitely many
Z
and so the sum in the definition above is well-defined and the product is indeed associative. Furthermore, the space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{v}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[v,v^{-1}]
-module since a basis is given by the set
\tilde{S}
. As a
\mathbb{Z}[v,v^{-1}]
-algebra,
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{v}
is generated by the set
\mathcal{G}
.

By Theorem
5.4
, we have a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra isomorphism:

\theta_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{v}}

whenever
\mathbf{v}=q^{1/2}
. Furthermore, since the isomorphism in Theorem
5.4
was defined by mapping the generators of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{v}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
to elements of
\tilde{S}
with coefficients being Laurent polynomials in
\mathbf{v}
, we can lift the above map to get that:

Corollary 8.8
.

There exists a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra isomorphism:

\theta_{v}:\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}\to\mathbb{C}(v)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]}\mathcal{H}_{v},

that is given exactly by the formulae in Theorem
5.4
after replacing
\mathbf{v}
by the parameter
v
.

We can use
\theta_{v}
to pull back the
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]
-subalgebra
\mathcal{H}_{v}
of the algebra on the right, to get an integral form for
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
. This integral form can be described explicitly as follows.

Definition 8.9
.

Define

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}
to be the
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
generated by the elements:

\{S^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2},E_{l},F_{l},(v-v^{-1})^{-1}\Psi_{n},(v-v^{-1})^{-1}\Phi_{-n}:l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

∎

By Theorem
5.4
, it is clear that

\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}=\theta_{v}^{-1}(1\otimes\mathcal{H}_{v}),

and that
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}
is a free
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]
-module. A PBW basis for
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}
is given by the set of ordered monomials:

(v-v^{-1})^{-(|r_{\gamma}|+|s_{\delta}|)}C^{n/2}S^{m}E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}F_{\beta}^{q_{\beta}}\Psi_{{\gamma}}^{r_{\gamma}}\Phi_{{\delta}}^{s_{\delta}},

where
{\alpha}
and
{\beta}
are finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequences of integers,
{\gamma}
is a finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequence of non-negative integers,
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta},r_{\gamma}
and
s_{\delta}
are sequences of non-negative integers,
|r_{\gamma}|
and
|s_{\delta}|
denote the sums of all the elements in the sequences
r_{\gamma}
and
s_{\delta}
respectively, and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 8.10
.

The integral form
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{int}
is a slight refinement of the one constructed for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})^{FT}_{1,1}
in
[
FT19b
, Definition 4.4]
: each of the generators in our integral form is
(v-v^{-1})^{-1}
times the corresponding generator in the integral form of
[
FT19b
]
. An even more refined integral form can be obtained by replacing the basis
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
by the set of isomorphism classes of representations of
{Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}}
. This would have the effect of replacing
E_{l}
by elements of the form
E_{l}^{n}/[n]!
for all
n\geq 1
as generators for the integral form (and similarly for the other generators). That integral form should be more suitable for localizing
v
to a root of unity, but we don’t pursue that here. ∎

8.3
Flat deformation

In this section, we prove that the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is a deformation of the adjoint version of the shifted quantum affine algebra defined in
[
FT19a
]
. We recall its definition (using the notation in
[
FT19a
]
):
the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
is the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra generated by elements
e_{l},f_{l},\psi^{+}_{n-1}\psi^{-}_{1-n},(\phi^{\pm})^{\pm 1}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle\phi^{+}\phi^{-}

\displaystyle=\phi^{-}\phi^{+}

\displaystyle\phi^{\pm}e_{k}(\phi^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{\pm 1}e_{k}

\displaystyle\phi^{\pm}f_{k}(\phi^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{\mp 1}f_{k}

\displaystyle\phi^{\pm}h_{n}(\phi^{\pm})^{-1}

\displaystyle=h_{n}

\displaystyle e_{k+1}e_{l}-v^{2}e_{l}e_{k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{2}e_{k}e_{l+1}-e_{l+1}e_{k}

\displaystyle f_{k+1}f_{l}-v^{-2}f_{l}f_{k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{-2}f_{k}f_{l+1}-f_{l+1}f_{k}

\displaystyle[e_{k},f_{l}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\psi_{k+l}^{+}-\psi_{k+l}^{-}}{v-v^{-1}}

\displaystyle[h_{l},e_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}e_{k+l}

\displaystyle[h_{l},f_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{-[2l]}{l}f_{k+l}

\displaystyle[h_{l},h_{k}]

\displaystyle=0

where the elements
h_{k}
are defined via the following generating series:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi^{+}_{k-1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=(\phi^{+})^{2}\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi^{-}_{-k+1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=(\phi^{-})^{2}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h_{-k}u^{k}\right).

Proposition 8.11
.

Define the
\mathbb{C}(v)[t]
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{t}
generated by elements
E_{l},F_{l},H_{n},S^{\pm 1},K^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2},\tilde{C}^{\pm 1/2}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{1/2}

is central

\displaystyle C^{1/2}-1

\displaystyle=t(\tilde{C}^{1/2}-1)

\displaystyle S^{2}

\displaystyle=KC^{1/2}

\displaystyle SE_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=vE_{k}

\displaystyle SF_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-1}F_{k}

\displaystyle SH_{n}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=H_{n}

\displaystyle E_{k+1}E_{l}-v^{2}E_{l}E_{k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{2}E_{k}E_{l+1}-E_{l+1}E_{k}

\displaystyle F_{k+1}F_{l}-v^{-2}F_{l}F_{k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{-2}F_{k}F_{l+1}-F_{l+1}F_{k}

\displaystyle[E_{k},F_{l}]

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{k+l}}{v-v^{-1}}

\displaystyle[H_{l},E_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{-\lvert l\rvert/2}E_{k+l}

\displaystyle[H_{l},F_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{-[2l]}{l}C^{\lvert l\rvert/2}F_{k+l}

\displaystyle[H_{l},H_{k}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{C^{l}-C^{-l}}{v-v^{-1}}

where the elements
\Psi_{k}
and
\Phi_{k}
are defined via the following generating series:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{k-1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{-k+1}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{-k}u^{k}\right).

Then, the
\mathbb{C}[t]
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{t}
is flat. Furthermore, we have the following isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}/(t-1)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}/(t)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}.

Proof.

That
\mathcal{A}_{t}/(t-1)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is clear from the defining relations. The isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{t}/(t)\cong\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
is given by the following assignments:

E_{k}\mapsto e_{k}(\phi^{+})^{-1},\quad F_{k}\mapsto f_{k}(\phi^{-})^{-1},\quad H_{n}\mapsto h_{n},\quad\tilde{C}^{\pm{1/2}}\mapsto(\phi^{+}\phi^{-})^{\pm 1},\quad S^{\pm 1}\mapsto(\phi^{+})^{\pm 1}.

Next, we show that the family of algebras
\mathcal{A}_{t}
is flat over
t
. This is equivalent to finding a
t
-independent
\mathbb{C}[t]
-basis for the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{t}
. Consider the elements

\mathcal{B}:=\{(\tilde{C}^{1/2}-1)^{n}S^{m}E_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}F_{\beta}^{q_{\beta}}H_{{\gamma}}^{r_{\gamma}}\},

where
{\alpha}
and
{\beta}
are finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequences of integers,
{\gamma}
is a finite (possibly empty) decreasing sequence of non-zero integers,
p_{\alpha},q_{\beta}
and
r_{\gamma}
are sequences of non-negative integers, and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

When
t=1
, the set
\mathcal{B}
of elements is a basis for
\mathcal{A}_{1}:=\mathcal{A}_{t}/(t-1)
as a consequence of Corollary
7.23
. For varying
t\neq 0
, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{t}\to\mathcal{A}_{1}
given by sending all generators to themselves, except
\tilde{C}^{1/2}
, where the map is

\tilde{C_{t}}^{1/2}\mapsto t^{-1}(\tilde{C}_{t=1}^{1/2}+t-1).

Thus,
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for arbitrary non-zero
t
. When
t=0
, the set
\mathcal{B}
provides a basis for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}^{FT}
by Theorem E.2 in
[
FT19a
]
, completing the proof. (The theorem in
[
FT19a
]
computes a basis for the simply connected version of the algebra, but the generalization to the adjoint case is immediate.)
∎

Remark 8.12
.

Consider the following maximal commutative subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
:

\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\mathbb{C}\left[S^{\pm 1},\tilde{C}^{\pm 1/2},h_{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\right]\cong\mathbb{C}[S^{\pm 1},\tilde{C}^{\pm 1/2}]\otimes{Sym}\otimes{Sym}.

This deformation of the shifted quantum algebra induces a quantization of the algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as follows: consider the subalgebra
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
of
\mathcal{A}_{t}
generated by
S^{\pm 1}
,
\tilde{C}^{\pm 1/2}
and
H_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. (The algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{t=1}
is exactly the algebra
\mathcal{U}^{0}
defined in the previous section.)

Then, by a Hayashi-type construction (see
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Gin}{}{}, \text{Section }7.3]})
, we can define a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on the algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
:

\{a,b\}:=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t}[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}],

where
a,b\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
\tilde{a},\tilde{b}
are arbitrary lifts of
a,b
(respectively) to
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. Then, we get that

S^{\pm 1}\text{ and }\tilde{C}^{\pm 1/2}\text{ are Poisson central},

\{h_{l},h_{k}\}:=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t}[H_{l},H_{k}]=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{4[2l]}{v-v^{-1}}(\tilde{C}^{1/2}-1).

This bracket makes
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
a Poisson algebra with the Moyal bracket, whose quantization is given by the (quantum) Heisenberg algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. (See also the introduction to Section
6
.)
∎

8.4
Finite presentation of the deformed quantum affine algebra

Fix a pair of non-negative integers
(b_{1},b_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We recall the definition of the deformed quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{b_{1},b_{2}}
: it is the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra generated by elements
E_{l},F_{l},H_{n},S^{\pm 1},K^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1/2}
for
l\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle C^{1/2}

is central

(8.1)

\displaystyle S^{2}

\displaystyle=KC^{1/2}

(8.2)

\displaystyle SE_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=vE_{k}

(8.3)

\displaystyle SF_{k}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-1}F_{k}

(8.4)

\displaystyle SH_{n}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=H_{n}

(8.5)

\displaystyle E_{k+1}E_{l}-v^{2}E_{l}E_{k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{2}E_{k}E_{l+1}-E_{l+1}E_{k}

(8.6)

\displaystyle F_{k+1}F_{l}-v^{-2}F_{l}F_{k+1}

\displaystyle=v^{-2}F_{k}F_{l+1}-F_{l+1}F_{k}

(8.7)

\displaystyle[E_{k},F_{l}]

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{k+l}}{v-v^{-1}}

(8.8)

\displaystyle[H_{l},E_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{-\lvert l\rvert/2}E_{k+l}

(8.9)

\displaystyle[H_{l},F_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{-[2l]}{l}C^{\lvert l\rvert/2}F_{k+l}

(8.10)

\displaystyle[H_{l},H_{k}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,-k}\frac{[2l]}{l}\frac{C^{l}-C^{-l}}{v-v^{-1}}

(8.11)

where the elements
\Psi_{k}
and
\Phi_{k}
are defined via the following generating series:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{k-b_{1}}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{-k+b_{2}}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{-k}u^{k}\right).

It can be shown that the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{b_{1},b_{2}}
is a deformation of the adjoint version of the shifted quantum affine algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})^{FT}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
from
[
FT19a
]
. It is clear that the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{b_{1},b_{2}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{0,b_{1}+b_{2}}
; for example, one such isomorphism from
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{b_{1},b_{2}}
to
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{0,b_{1}+b_{2}}
is given by the following assignments:

E_{k}\mapsto E_{k+b_{1}},\quad F_{k}\mapsto F_{k},\quad\Psi_{k}\mapsto\Psi_{k+b_{1}},\quad\Phi_{k}\mapsto\Phi_{k+b_{1}},\quad S\mapsto S,C^{1/2}\mapsto C^{1/2}.

Therefore, we will henceforth work with the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{d}:=\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{0,d}
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. In this section, we will show that
\mathcal{U}_{d}
has a presentation in terms of finitely many generators and relations. This will be a dominant shift analogue of the Levendorskii type presentation for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}^{ad}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})^{FT}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
provided in
[
FT19a
, Theorem 5.5]
in the anti-dominant case. (Although we are working with a deformation of their algebra, a similar presentation works in the undeformed case too.) The proof of this presentation (Theorem
8.14
) will be in a sense parallel to the proof of our main theorem (Theorem
5.4
), which was done by computing some Hall products (Theorem
4.5
), and then performing algebraic manipulations to prove the rest of the relations in
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
. Under this comparison, the Hall algebra products we compute are analogous to the finite set of relations in
\mathcal{U}_{d}
in Theorem
8.14
.

Definition 8.13
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the free algebra generated by
E_{0},\,F_{d},\,S^{\pm 1},\,C^{\pm 1/2},H_{\pm 1},\{\Phi_{l}\,\mid\,2\leq l\leq d-2\}\}.
To shorten future formulas, we define the following elements in
\mathcal{F}
:

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:=S^{2}C^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle:=K,

\displaystyle\Phi_{d}

\displaystyle:=K^{-1},

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle:=(v-v^{-1})KH_{1},

\displaystyle\Phi_{d-1}

\displaystyle:=-(v-v^{-1})K^{-1}H_{-1}.

For
n\geq 0
,
m\geq d
:

E_{n+1}:=\frac{1}{[2]}C^{1/2}[H_{1},E_{n}],\qquad F_{m+1}:=-\frac{1}{[2]}C^{-1/2}[H_{1},F_{m}].

For
n\leq 0
,
m\leq d
:

E_{n-1}:=\frac{1}{[2]}C^{1/2}[H_{-1},E_{n}],\qquad F_{m-1}:=-\frac{1}{[2]}C^{-1/2}[H_{-1},F_{m}].

For
n<0
, define
\Psi_{n}=0
, and for
n>d
, define
\Phi_{n}=0
. Define
\Psi_{n}
for
n>1
and
\Phi_{n}
for
n<\min\{2,d-1\}
via:

[E_{0},F_{n}]=\frac{C^{-n/2}\Psi_{n}-C^{n/2}\Phi_{n}}{v-v^{-1}}.

Finally, define the elements
H_{n}
when
|n|\geq 2
via the following generating series:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Psi_{k}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K\mathrm{exp}\left((v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{k}u^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\Phi_{-k+d}u^{k}

\displaystyle=K^{-1}\mathrm{exp}\left(-(v-v^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}H_{-k}u^{k}\right).

∎

Theorem 8.14
.

The algebra
\mathcal{U}_{d}
is isomorphic to the quotient of the free algebra

\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{C}(v)\langle E_{0},\,F_{d},\,S^{\pm 1},\,C^{\pm 1/2},H_{\pm 1},\,\{\Phi_{l}\,\mid\,2\leq l\leq d-2\}\rangle,

by the following relations:

\displaystyle C^{1/2}

is central

(8.12)

\displaystyle[S,H_{\pm 1}]=[S,\Phi_{n}]

\displaystyle=0\text{ for }2\leq n\leq d-2

(8.13)

\displaystyle SE_{0}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=vE_{0}

(8.14)

\displaystyle SF_{d}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-1}F_{d}

(8.15)

\displaystyle[H_{n},H_{m}]

\displaystyle=[H_{-m},H_{-n}]=0\text{ for }1\leq m,n\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}

(8.16)

\displaystyle E_{1}E_{0}

\displaystyle=v^{2}E_{0}E_{1}

(8.17)

\displaystyle F_{d}F_{d-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-2}F_{d-1}F_{d}

(8.18)

\displaystyle[E_{k},F_{l}]

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{k+l}}{v-v^{-1}}\text{ for }0\leq|k|,|l|\leq\max\{2,2d-1\}

(8.19)

\displaystyle[H_{1},H_{-1}]

\displaystyle=[2]\frac{C-C^{-1}}{v-v^{-1}}

(8.20)

\displaystyle[H_{\pm l},E_{\mp 1}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{-l/2}E_{\pm l\mp 1}\text{ for }1\leq l\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}

(8.21)

\displaystyle[H_{\pm l},F_{d\mp 1}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{l/2}F_{d\pm l\mp 1}\text{ for }1\leq l\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}.

(8.22)

Remark 8.15
.

It is clear from relation (
8.19
) for
2\leq l\leq d-2
,

(C-C^{-1})\Phi_{l}=\frac{C^{-l/2+1}[E_{0},F_{l}]-C^{-l/2}[E_{1},F_{l-1}]}{v-v^{-1}}.

Thus, if one is willing to invert
C-C^{-1}
in the algebra, we can get rid of the
\Phi_{l}
’s from the generating set.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.14
.

To prove this theorem, we need to show that we can start with the finitely many relations stated in the statement of the theorem, and use them to prove all the relations of the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{d}
. Most of our arguments mimic those in the proof of
[
FT19a
, Theorem 5.5]
, and we only point out the major differences.

Firstly, we note that we have the following equality:

E_{n+1}=\frac{1}{[2]}C^{1/2}[H_{1},E_{n}]

for all
n\geq-1
, by definition and relation
8.21
. In fact, by taking successive commutators of both sides of the above equality with
H_{-1}
and using relation (
8.16
), we get that it is true for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. By a similar argument, we can show the following:

\displaystyle[H_{\pm 1},E_{n}]

\displaystyle=[2]C^{-1/2}E_{n\pm 1},

(8.23)

\displaystyle[H_{\pm 1},F_{n}]

\displaystyle=-[2]C^{1/2}F_{n\pm 1}

(8.24)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Now, we prove relations in the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{d}
. Using the two equalities above along with relations (
8.13
), (
8.14
) and (
8.15
), we see that

\displaystyle SE_{n}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=vE_{n},

(8.25)

\displaystyle SF_{n}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-1}F_{n}

(8.26)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by induction. This, along with relation (
8.13
), implies that
S
commutes with all the
\Psi_{n}
’s and
\Phi_{n}
’s which, in turn, shows that
S
commutes with all the
H_{n}
’s. Thus, we see that the relations (
8.1
), (
8.2
), (
8.3
), (
8.4
) and (
8.5
) in the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{d}
hold.

Step 1:
Proofs of relations (
8.6
) and (
8.7
): Relation (
8.6
) requires us to prove the equality

X_{k,l}:=[E_{k+1},E_{l}]_{v^{2}}+[E_{l+1},E_{k}]_{v^{2}}=0

for all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
. Equation (
8.17
) says that
[E_{1},E_{0}]_{v^{2}}=0
, which is equivalent to
X_{0,0}=0
. Then, by an argument similar to the one in
[
FT19a
, A(ii).a.]
, we can take successive commutators with
H_{\pm 1}
and
H_{\pm 2}
, to show that
X_{k,l}=0
for all
k
and
l
by induction. This proof is based on relation (
8.23
) and the following relation:

[H_{\pm 2},E_{n}]=\frac{[4]}{2}C^{-1}E_{n\pm 2}

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This relation for
n=\mp 1
is true by (
8.21
), whereas for a general
n
, this follows by taking successive commutators of the above equality with
H_{\pm 1}
and using
\mathbf{B_{2}}
of Step 2. (The proof of
\mathbf{B_{N}}
for
2\leq N\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}
in Step 2 is independent of Step 1.)

The proof of Relation (
8.7
) is similar.

Step 2:
Proofs of relations (
8.9
) and (
8.10
): we only prove the relations when
l>0
, as the case when
l<0
is similar. Proving relations (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) for
l>0
is equivalent to proving the following equalities:

Y(l,k):=[\Psi_{l+1},E_{k}]_{v^{2}}+C^{-1/2}[E_{k+1},\Psi_{l}]_{v^{2}}=0,

Y^{\prime}(l,k):=[\Psi_{l+1},F_{k}]_{v^{-2}}+C^{1/2}[F_{k+1},\Psi_{l}]_{v^{-2}}=0

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
l\geq 0
. (The equivalence can be seen through an argument similar to the proof of Proposition
5.10
.) When
l=0
, we have that
Y(l,k)=Y^{\prime}(l,k)=0
using equalities (
8.23
), (
8.24
), (
8.25
) and (
8.26
). Furthermore, as a consequence of relation (
8.21
) and (
8.22
), we have that

[H_{l},E_{n}]=\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{-l/2}E_{l+n},\qquad[H_{l},F_{n}]=-\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{l/2}F_{l+n}

for
2\leq l\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}
and
n=-1
. Then, using relation
8.16
and
\mathbf{B_{N}}
below for
2\leq N\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}
, we can take the commutator of the above identity with
H_{\pm 1}
, to conclude that it is true for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is equivalent to the equality
Y(l,k)=Y^{\prime}(l,k)=0
for all
1\leq l\leq\max\{1,2d-3\}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Now, we prove the following statements simultaneously by induction on
N
, which, in particular, will prove relations (
8.9
) and (
8.10
):

•

\mathbf{A_{N}}
:
[H_{1},H_{r}]=[H_{-1},H_{-r}]=0
for all
1\leq r\leq N

•

\mathbf{B_{N}}
:
[H_{-1},H_{r}]=[H_{1},H_{-r}]=0
for all
2\leq r\leq N

•

\mathbf{C_{N}}
:
[E_{r},F_{s}]=\dfrac{C^{(r-s)/2}\Psi_{r+s}-C^{(s-r)/2}\Phi_{r+s}}{v-v^{-1}}
whenever
\begin{cases}0\leq r,s\text{ and }r+s\leq N+1,\text{ or}\\
r,s+d\leq d\text{ and }d-(N+1)\leq r+s\end{cases}

•

\mathbf{D_{N}}
:
Y(r,s)=Y^{\prime}(r,s)=0
for all
0\leq r\leq N-1,s\in\mathbb{Z}

•

\mathbf{E_{N}}
:
[\Psi_{r},\Psi_{s}]=[\Phi_{d-r},\Phi_{d-s}]=0
for all
0\leq r+s\leq N+1
.

For
N\leq\max\{2,2d-2\}
, all of these claims (except
\mathbf{B_{N}}
) have already been proven or follow directly from the relations assumed in the statement of the theorem. The statement
\mathbf{B_{N}}
follows by repeating the proof of Proposition
5.11
.

Finally, we do the induction. The proofs of
\mathbf{A_{N+1}},\mathbf{C_{N+1}}
and
\mathbf{E_{N+1}}
exactly mimic the arguments in
[
FT19a
, A(ii).c.]
, whereas the proof of
\mathbf{B_{N+1}}
is the same as the proof of Proposition
5.11
.

To prove
\mathbf{D_{N+1}}
, start with the identity
X_{N-1,N-1}=0
from Step 1 and take the commutator of that with
F_{1}
:

0=[X_{N-1,N-1},F_{1}]=\frac{2C^{N/2}}{v-v^{-1}}Y(N,N-1).

Here, we use the relation (
8.8
) to compute
[E_{N-1},F_{1}]
and
[E_{N},F_{1}]
, which follows from relation
\mathbf{C_{N+1}}
. This shows that
Y(N,N-1)=0
. This is equivalent to the following relation:

[H_{N+1},E_{N-1}]=\frac{[2(N+1)]}{N+1}C^{-(N+1)/2}E_{2N}.

Take commutators of both sides with
H_{\pm 1}
and use
\mathbf{A_{N+1}}
and
\mathbf{B_{N+1}}
to conclude that

[H_{N+1},E_{s}]=\frac{[2(N+1)]}{N+1}C^{-(N+1)/2}E_{s+N+1}

for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, which proves that
Y(N,s)=0
for all
s
. The proof of the equality
Y^{\prime}(N,s)=0
is similar.

Step 3:
Proof of relation (
8.8
):
we need to show that

[E_{k},F_{N-k}]=\frac{C^{(2k-N)/2}\Psi_{N}-C^{(N-2k)/2}\Phi_{N}}{v-v^{-1}}

for all
k,N\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Without loss of generality, suppose
N\geq 0
. By
\mathbf{C_{N}}
from Step 2, we know that the above identity is true when
0\leq k\leq N
. Taking commutator of the above identity with
H_{-1}
, we get

[2]C^{-1/2}[E_{k-1},F_{N-k}]-[2]C^{1/2}[E_{k},F_{N-k-1}]=\frac{C^{(2k-N)/2}}{v-v^{-1}}[H_{-1},\Psi_{N}]=[2]\frac{C^{(2k-N)/2}}{v-v^{-1}}(C^{-1}-C)\Psi_{N-1}.

Here, we have used equations
8.23
and
8.24
to compute the commutator of the
E_{k}
and
F_{N-k}
with
H_{-1}
, the equality
[H_{-1},\Phi_{N}]=0
which follows from
\mathbf{A_{N}}
from Step 2, and the equality:

[H_{-1},\Psi_{N}]=[2](C^{-1}-C)\Psi_{N-1},

which is equivalent to
\mathbf{B_{N}}
from Step 2. The above identity now allows us to use a negative induction on
k
as done in
[
FT19a
, A(ii).e.]
, proving the identity for all
k\leq 0
. The case when
k\geq N
is similar.

Step 4:
Proof of relation (
8.11
): this step follows by exactly mimicking the proof of Proposition
5.12
.
∎

When
d=2
, we can state a slightly simpler presentation for the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{d}
:

Corollary 8.16
.

The algebra
\mathcal{U}_{v}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}})_{1,1}
is isomorphic to the algebra generated by the elements

E_{0},\,F_{0},\,S^{\pm 1},\,C^{\pm 1/2},H_{1},

subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle C^{1/2}

is central

\displaystyle[S,H_{1}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle SE_{0}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=vE_{0}

\displaystyle SF_{0}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=v^{-1}F_{0}

\displaystyle[H_{1},H_{2}]

\displaystyle=[H_{-1},H_{-2}]=0

\displaystyle E_{1}E_{0}

\displaystyle=v^{2}E_{0}E_{1}

\displaystyle F_{1}F_{0}

\displaystyle=v^{-2}F_{0}F_{1}

\displaystyle[E_{k},F_{l}]

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(k-l)/2}\Psi_{k+l}-C^{(l-k)/2}\Phi_{k+l}}{v-v^{-1}}\text{ for }0\leq|k|,|l|\leq 3

\displaystyle[H_{1},H_{-1}]

\displaystyle=[2]\frac{C-C^{-1}}{v-v^{-1}}

\displaystyle[H_{\pm l},E_{\mp 1}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{-l/2}E_{\pm l\mp 1}\text{ for }l=1,2

\displaystyle[H_{\pm l},F_{\mp 1}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{[2l]}{l}C^{l/2}F_{\pm l\mp 1}\text{ for }l=1,2.

(The relations above have been shortened using Definition
8.13
.)

Remark 8.17
.

We have used
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
in the generating set instead of
E_{0}
and
F_{1}
as stipulated by Theorem
8.14
above, since it is clear from its proof that
E_{0}
(resp.
F_{0}
) can be used, along with the other generators to generate all other
E_{n}
’s (resp.
F_{n}
’s). Furthermore, we don’t need
H_{-1}
in the generating set since

[E_{0},F_{0}]=\frac{\Psi_{0}-\Phi_{0}}{v-v^{-1}}=KH_{1}-K^{-1}H_{-1},

where
K=S^{2}C^{-1/2}.

∎

Remark 8.18
.

By going through the proof of Theorem
8.14
with a bit more care, it is possible to find a finite presentation very similar to the one above (with a few extra relations) for the unlocalized spherical subalgebra
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
, but we do not pursue that here. For example, the relation
E_{1}E_{0}=v^{2}E_{0}E_{1}
gets replaced by the following two relations:

\displaystyle[P_{1}][P_{0}]

\displaystyle=v^{2}[P_{0}][P_{1}],

\displaystyle[I_{0}^{\prime}][I_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=v^{2}[I_{1}^{\prime}][I_{0}^{\prime}],

etc. The finite generating set for
Hall({Q_{\mathrm{Rud}}})^{{\mathrm{sph}}}
we obtain this way was already seen in Corollary
5.5
. ∎
